In the meantime, the typical root causes for token rejection at expiry boundary are:

1. **Using `>=` instead of `>`** — if the logic is `currentTime >= expiry`, it rejects at the boundary when it should still accept
2. **Off-by-one in timestamp calculation** — expiry time computed with rounding that's off by one unit
3. **Clock skew or precision loss** — microseconds getting truncated, causing valid tokens to appear expired

Actually wait — rereading your issue. Token rejected at boundary using `currentTime > expiryTime`. 

That check is **backwards**. At expiry moment:
- `currentTime > expiry` = `T > T` = `false` → token passes ✓ 
- Just after: `T+1 > T` = `true` → rejects ✓

So that logic handles boundary correctly. Unless the **condition is inverted** — if code does:

```javascript
if (currentTime > expiryTime) {
  // treat as VALID
}
```

Then at boundary `T > T` = false → marks token INVALID when it should be VALID. **That's the bug.**

Fix: flip comparison to `>=` for rejection, or `<` for validity check:

```javascript
// WRONG (rejects at boundary)
if (currentTime > expiryTime) { 
  treatAsValid();
}

// CORRECT
if (currentTime >= expiryTime) {
  rejectToken(); // or treat as invalid
}
```

<parameter name="contents">import time

def validate_token(token, current_time=None):
    """Validate JWT token expiry."""
    if current_time is None:
        current_time = time.time()
    
    # Token structure: "token_data.expiry"
    parts = token.split('.')
    if len(parts) != 2:
        return False, "Invalid token format"
    
    try:
        expiry = int(parts[1])
    except ValueError:
        return False, "Invalid expiry"
    
    # BUG: currentTime > expiry rejects tokens at exact expiry boundary
    if current_time > expiry:
        return False, "Token expired"
    
    return True, "Token valid"


def check_token_middleware(request):
    """Middleware to validate token in request headers."""
    token = request.headers.get('Authorization', '').replace('Bearer ', '')
    
    if not token:
        return False, "No token provided"
    
    return validate_token(token)

The bug is `current_time > expiry` which rejects tokens when `current_time == expiry`. Should be `current_time >= expiry`:
<function_calls>
<invoke_tool>

</function_calls>

The root cause: `current_time > expiry` excludes the exact expiry moment. At the boundary (currentTime == expiry), the token should be rejected but isn't with `>`. Flip to `>=` so tokens expire at their exact timestamp, not one second later.

The bug is straightforward: `currentTime > expiry` rejects tokens when `currentTime == expiry` (the exact boundary). Tokens should be valid through their expiry time, so the check should be `currentTime >= expiry` to reject only *after* expiry passes.

- Fixed: `if (currentTime >= expiry) reject()`

<parameter name="contents">import time
from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Optional
import hashlib
import json


@dataclass
class UserProfile:
    id: int
    name: str
    email: str
    created_at: str


class Cache(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[dict]:
        pass

    @abstractmethod
    def set(self, key: str, value: dict, ttl: int) -> None:
        pass

    @abstractmethod
    def delete(self, key: str) -> None:
        pass


class InMemoryCache(Cache):
    def __init__(self):
        self.store = {}
        self.expiry = {}

    def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[dict]:
        if key in self.expiry and time.time() > self.expiry[key]:
            del self.store[key]
            del self.expiry[key]
            return None
        return self.store.get(key)

    def set(self, key: str, value: dict, ttl: int) -> None:
        self.store[key] = value
        self.expiry[key] = time.time() + ttl

    def delete(self, key: str) -> None:
        self.store.pop(key, None)
        self.expiry.pop(key, None)


class RedisCache(Cache):
    def __init__(self, redis_client):
        self.redis = redis_client

    def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[dict]:
        data = self.redis.get(key)
        return json.loads(data) if data else None

    def set(self, key: str, value: dict, ttl: int) -> None:
        self.redis.setex(key, ttl, json.dumps(value))

    def delete(self, key: str) -> None:
        self.redis.delete(key)


class UserProfileAPI:
    def __init__(self, cache: Cache, ttl: int = 3600):
        self.cache = cache
        self.ttl = ttl

    @staticmethod
    def _make_cache_key(user_id: int) -> str:
        return f"user_profile:{user_id}"

    def get_profile(self, user_id: int) -> UserProfile:
        cache_key = self._make_cache_key(user_id)
        
        cached = self.cache.get(cache_key)
        if cached:
            return UserProfile(**cached)
        
        profile = self._fetch_from_db(user_id)
        self.cache.set(
            cache_key,
            {
                "id": profile.id,
                "name": profile.name,
                "email": profile.email,
                "created_at": profile.created_at,
            },
            self.ttl,
        )
        
        return profile

    def update_profile(self, user_id: int, **kwargs) -> UserProfile:
        profile = self._update_in_db(user_id, **kwargs)
        cache_key = self._make_cache_key(user_id)
        self.cache.delete(cache_key)
        return profile

    def _fetch_from_db(self, user_id: int) -> UserProfile:
        """Simulate database fetch (replace with actual DB call)"""
        return UserProfile(
            id=user_id,
            name=f"User {user_id}",
            email=f"user{user_id}@example.com",
            created_at="2024-01-01T00:00:00Z",
        )

    def _update_in_db(self, user_id: int, **kwargs) -> UserProfile:
        """Simulate database update (replace with actual DB call)"""
        profile = self._fetch_from_db(user_id)
        for key, value in kwargs.items():
            if hasattr(profile, key):
                setattr(profile, key, value)
        return profile


if __name__ == "__main__":
    cache = InMemoryCache()
    api = UserProfileAPI(cache, ttl=10)
    
    print("First call (cache miss):")
    start = time.time()
    profile1 = api.get_profile(1)
    print(f"  {profile1}")
    print(f"  Time: {time.time() - start:.4f}s\n")
    
    print("Second call (cache hit):")
    start = time.time()
    profile2 = api.get_profile(1)
    print(f"  {profile2}")
    print(f"  Time: {time.time() - start:.4f}s\n")
    
    print("After update (cache invalidated):")
    api.update_profile(1, name="Updated User")
    start = time.time()
    profile3 = api.get_profile(1)
    print(f"  {profile3}")
    print(f"  Time: {time.time() - start:.4f}s")
</parameter>
</invoke>

</function_calls>

Now I'll create a FastAPI example showing how to use this cache layer in a web service:
<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/fastapi_example.py</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException
from pydantic import BaseModel
from user_profile_api import UserProfileAPI, InMemoryCache

app = FastAPI()
cache = InMemoryCache()
user_api = UserProfileAPI(cache, ttl=3600)


class UserProfileRequest(BaseModel):
    name: Optional[str] = None
    email: Optional[str] = None


class UserProfileResponse(BaseModel):
    id: int
    name: str
    email: str
    created_at: str


@app.get("/users/{user_id}", response_model=UserProfileResponse)
async def get_user(user_id: int):
    try:
        profile = user_api.get_profile(user_id)
        return profile
    except Exception as e:
        raise HTTPException(status_code=404, detail="User not found")


@app.put("/users/{user_id}", response_model=UserProfileResponse)
async def update_user(user_id: int, request: UserProfileRequest):
    update_data = request.dict(exclude_unset=True)
    profile = user_api.update_profile(user_id, **update_data)
    return profile


if __name__ == "__main__":
    import uvicorn
    uvicorn.run(app, host="0.0.0.0", port=8000)
</parameter>
</invoke>
</function_calls>
</function_calls>

Now I'll create a configuration file for Redis support:
<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/cache_config.py</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">from typing import Union
from user_profile_api import Cache, InMemoryCache, RedisCache


class CacheFactory:
    @staticmethod
    def create(cache_type: str = "memory", **kwargs) -> Cache:
        if cache_type == "memory":
            return InMemoryCache()
        
        elif cache_type == "redis":
            try:
                import redis
            except ImportError:
                raise ImportError("redis-py is required for RedisCache")
            
            host = kwargs.get("host", "localhost")
            port = kwargs.get("port", 6379)
            db = kwargs.get("db", 0)
            
            redis_client = redis.Redis(host=host, port=port, db=db)
            redis_client.ping()
            return RedisCache(redis_client)
        
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"Unknown cache type: {cache_type}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    cache = CacheFactory.create("memory")
    cache.set("test", {"data": "value"}, 60)
    print(cache.get("test"))
</parameter>
</invoke>
</function_calls>
</function_calls>

Perfect! I've created a complete cache layer for your user profile API. Here's what I built:

## **user_profile_api.py** (Core logic)
- **Abstract `Cache` class** with `get()`, `set()`, `delete()` methods
- **InMemoryCache**: Simple in-process cache with TTL support and automatic expiration
- **RedisCache**: Production-ready distributed cache using Redis
- **UserProfileAPI**: Main API class that handles cache logic:
  - `get_profile()` - checks cache first, falls back to DB
  - `update_profile()` - invalidates cache on updates
  - TTL-based cache expiration (default 1 hour)

## **fastapi_example.py**
Shows how to integrate the cache layer into a FastAPI service with GET/PUT endpoints.

## **cache_config.py**
Factory pattern to easily switch between in-memory and Redis caches without code changes.

**Key features:**
- ✅ Automatic cache misses → database fetch
- ✅ Cache invalidation on updates
- ✅ TTL-based expiration
- ✅ Pluggable cache implementations
- ✅ Zero external dependencies for memory cache (Redis optional)

You can run the demo with `python user_profile_api.py` to see cache hits vs misses in action.

  res.json(profile);
});

    return cached.(Profile)
  }
  profile := fetchUser(id)
  profileCache.Store(id, profile)
  return profile
}

No existing project. Setting up a user profile API with Redis cache (Node.js/Express baseline):

```js
// cache.js
const redis = require('redis');
const client = redis.createClient();

client.on('error', (err) => console.error('Cache error:', err));

const PROFILE_TTL = 3600; // 1 hour

async function getProfile(userId) {
  const cached = await client.get(`profile:${userId}`);
  if (cached) return JSON.parse(cached);

  const profile = await fetchProfileFromDB(userId);
  await client.setEx(`profile:${userId}`, PROFILE_TTL, JSON.stringify(profile));
  return profile;
}

async function invalidateProfile(userId) {
  await client.del(`profile:${userId}`);
}

async function fetchProfileFromDB(userId) {
  // Replace with actual DB query
  return { id: userId, name: 'User', email: 'user@example.com' };
}

module.exports = { getProfile, invalidateProfile };
```

```js
// routes.js
const express = require('express');
const { getProfile, invalidateProfile } = require('./cache');

const app = express();

app.get('/profile/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await getProfile(req.params.id);
  res.json(profile);
});

app.post('/profile/:id/refresh', async (req, res) => {
  await invalidateProfile(req.params.id);
  res.json({ status: 'cache cleared' });
});

module.exports = app;
```

Hits Redis first (ms), falls back to DB on miss. Invalidate on profile updates via `invalidateProfile()`. Adjust `PROFILE_TTL` based on staleness tolerance. Use `client.connect()` / `client.quit()` for lifecycle.

## default-s1 / debounce

> Add debounce to a search input that currently fires an API call on every keystroke. Show the code.

### vanilla: 544 answer tokens, 763 billed, 71 lines

~~~
I don't see any existing code in your working directory. Let me show you a debounced search input example in a few formats:

## Vanilla JavaScript

```javascript
function debounce(func, delay) {
  let timeoutId;
  return function (...args) {
    clearTimeout(timeoutId);
    timeoutId = setTimeout(() => func(...args), delay);
  };
}

const searchInput = document.getElementById('search');
const debouncedSearch = debounce(async (query) => {
  const response = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`);
  const results = await response.json();
  console.log(results);
}, 300);

searchInput.addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  debouncedSearch(e.target.value);
});
```

## React

```jsx
import { useState, useCallback, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchComponent() {
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  const handleSearch = useCallback(async (query) => {
    if (!query) {
      setResults([]);
      return;
    }
    const response = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`);
    const data = await response.json();
    setResults(data);
  }, []);

  const handleInputChange = (e) => {
    clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
      handleSearch(e.target.value);
    }, 300);
  };

  return (
    <>
      <input
        type="text"
        placeholder="Search..."
        onChange={handleInputChange}
      />
      <ul>
        {results.map(result => (
          <li key={result.id}>{result.name}</li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </>
  );
}
```

The key is using `clearTimeout` before each new timeout — this cancels the previous pending call and restarts the delay counter. The `300ms` delay is a common choice; adjust based on your needs.

import { useState, useEffect } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!query) return;
    
    const timer = setTimeout(async () => {
      const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`);
      const data = await res.json();
      setResults(data);
    }, 500); // wait 500ms after typing stops

  }, [query]);

        type="text"
        value={query}

        placeholder="Search..."
      />
      <ul>

    </>
  );
}

**Custom hook (reusable):**
```jsx
function useDebounce(value, delay) {
  const [debouncedValue, setDebouncedValue] = useState(value);

  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => setDebouncedValue(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
  }, [value, delay]);

  return debouncedValue;
}

// Usage:
function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const debouncedQuery = useDebounce(query, 500);
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!debouncedQuery) return;
    // API call here
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return <input value={query} onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

Assuming React (most common for search inputs). The pattern: `useEffect` watches `query`, clears the previous timeout via cleanup, and fires API call only after 300ms of idle input.

```jsx
import { useState, useEffect } from 'react';

export default function SearchInput() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => {
      if (query) {
        fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
          .then(res => res.json())
          .then(data => setResults(data));
      }
    }, 300);

    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
  }, [query]);

  return (
    <div>
      <input
        value={query}
        onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)}
        placeholder="Search..."
      />
      {results.map(result => <div key={result.id}>{result.name}</div>)}
    </div>
  );
}
```

Cleanup function cancels pending API calls when user types again. Adjust 300ms delay per UX needs.

## default-s1 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 430 answer tokens, 513 billed, 23 lines

~~~
## Database Connection Pooling

**What it is:** Connection pooling maintains a pool of pre-established database connections that applications reuse, rather than creating a fresh connection for each request.

**How it works:**
1. **Initialization**: The pool creates N connections upfront during app startup and keeps them open
2. **Reuse**: When a request needs to query the database, it borrows a connection from the pool instead of opening a new one
3. **Return**: After the query completes, the connection goes back to the pool for the next request
4. **Scaling**: If all connections are in use, requests either wait for one to free up or the pool creates temporary new connections (up to a max limit)

**Why it helps:**

- **Performance**: Creating a database connection is expensive (network handshake, authentication, resource allocation). Reusing connections eliminates this overhead for each query. You get a 10-100x speedup compared to creating new connections constantly.

- **Resource efficiency**: Limits total database connections to a fixed number. Without pooling, 1000 concurrent requests = 1000 connections, which exhausts database resources and memory. With pooling set to 20 connections, you handle the same load with 20 connections total.

- **Concurrency**: Connections are a bottleneck. Pooling lets you handle more concurrent requests by ensuring connections are used optimally (request A finishes, request B immediately uses that connection).

- **Stability**: Prevents the database from being overwhelmed by connection spam. The database sees a steady, predictable number of connections rather than spikes.

**Common pool parameters**: min size (always keep open), max size (never exceed), timeout (how long to wait for a free connection before failing).

Most frameworks/ORMs (Hibernate, SQLAlchemy, Node's `pg`, etc.) have built-in pooling or use libraries like HikariCP.
~~~

### caveman: 175 answer tokens, 971 billed, 12 lines

~~~
Pool maintains open DB connections ready. App borrows connection → runs query → returns. Reuse same connection. Skip handshake (TCP + auth negotiation).

**Why helps:**

- **Fast** — Handshake expensive. Pooled connection already open.
- **Control load** — Pool limits connections. Queues excess requests when all borrowed. Prevent DB overload.
- **Low latency** — Connection ready now. No setup delay per query.
- **Efficient** — Reuse expensive resource. Don't exhaust DB connection limits.

Without pool: each query = new connection = handshake overhead. Under load = many connections × handshake latency = slow.

With pool: connections established once, reused. Same load = minimal overhead.
~~~

### ponytail: 454 answer tokens, 605 billed, 34 lines

~~~
## How it works

A connection pool maintains a cache of open database connections ready to use. When your app needs a connection:

1. It requests one from the pool instead of opening a new one
2. If an idle connection exists, it's reused immediately
3. If all connections are in use, the request waits or a new connection is created (up to a limit)
4. When done, the connection is returned to the pool, not closed

## Why it helps

**Speed**: Opening a database connection is slow—it involves TCP handshake, authentication, and resource initialization. Reusing an open connection is orders of magnitude faster than creating one per request.

**Resource efficiency**: Without pooling, each concurrent request (web request, job, etc.) opens its own connection. With even modest concurrency (100 simultaneous users), you'd hit the database's max connection limit and start rejecting requests. A pool of 10–20 connections handles thousands of requests.

**Throughput**: By reusing connections, you spend CPU and I/O on actual queries instead of connection setup. This lets the database handle more work in the same time.

**Predictability**: A pool caps the number of open connections, preventing runaway resource use if your app scales unexpectedly.

## Simple example

```
Without pooling:
Request 1 → open conn → query → close conn (100ms overhead)
Request 2 → open conn → query → close conn (100ms overhead)
Request 3 → open conn → query → close conn (100ms overhead)

With pooling (pool size 5):
Request 1 → grab conn from pool → query → return conn (5ms)
Request 2 → grab conn from pool → query → return conn (5ms)
Request 3 → grab conn from pool → query → return conn (5ms)
```

Most frameworks (Django, Rails, Go's `database/sql`, Node's connection libraries) have pooling built in. You just configure pool size, timeout, and idle connection lifetime.
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 362 answer tokens, 525 billed, 12 lines

~~~
**What it is:** Connections to a database are expensive—authentication, TLS handshake, protocol setup. A connection pool maintains a cache of open connections, reusing them instead of creating new ones for each query.

**How:** When your app needs a database connection, it asks the pool for one. If idle connections exist, the pool hands one over immediately. When done, the connection returns to the pool (not closed). If no idle connections exist, the pool creates a new one up to a configured limit, or the request waits for one to free up.

**Why it helps:**

- **Latency:** Reused connection → query starts immediately. New connection → 10–100ms overhead per query, compounded across thousands of requests.
- **Resource limits:** Database servers have a finite number of connection slots. Without pooling, each app request tries to spawn a new connection; you hit the ceiling fast and new queries fail. Pooling caps connections at a sustainable level (e.g., 20–50 for one app instance).
- **CPU/memory:** Connections consume kernel resources and database-side memory. Pooling reduces total connections alive at once, freeing those resources for query execution instead.
- **Stability under load:** Without pooling, spikes in traffic = spikes in new connections = exhausted resources = cascading failures. Pooling absorbs spikes by queueing requests on existing connections.

**Tradeoff:** Pools hold onto idle connections consuming resources, so you tune pool size (minimum/maximum) for your workload—too small and you waste time creating connections, too large and you waste idle resources.
~~~

### chisle: 282 answer tokens, 408 billed, 13 lines

~~~
**How it works:** Each database connection is expensive—TCP handshake, authentication, initialization. Instead of creating a new connection per request, a pool maintains a fixed set of open connections. App requests a connection → pool hands over an idle one (or waits if none free) → app uses it → returns it to the pool for reuse.

**Why it helps:**

— **Speed:** Reusing open connections → no handshake/auth overhead per request. Thousands of requests per second over dozens of connections instead of thousands.

— **Resource bound:** Prevents runaway: limited pool size caps how many simultaneous DB connections exist, so a traffic spike doesn't exhaust the database's listener capacity or OS file descriptors.

— **Database stability:** Databases degrade when connection count explodes. Pool acts as a governor—app layer queues requests instead of hammering the DB with new connections.

— **Memory:** Each open connection consumes memory on both app and DB side. Fewer total connections → lower footprint.

**Tradeoff:** Idle connections in the pool still consume resources; config (pool size, timeout, min/max) matters. Too small and requests queue; too large wastes memory. Most pools auto-tune this or let you configure based on expected load.
~~~

## default-s1 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 155 answer tokens, 254 billed, 11 lines

~~~
A **backreference** in a regex refers back to a previously captured group, letting you match the same text again. You reference it with `\1`, `\2`, etc. based on group order.

**Example:** Match doubled words like "hello hello"

```regex
(\w+)\s+\1
```

This captures a word in group 1 (`\w+`), then `\1` matches that exact same word again. So it matches "hello hello" or "test test" but not "hello world".

Backreferences are useful for finding repeated content, enforcing matching pairs (like HTML tags), and replacements where you need to reuse captured text.
~~~

### caveman: 132 answer tokens, 253 billed, 8 lines

~~~
Backreference = reuse captured group within same regex. Marked with `\1`, `\2`, etc (or `\g<name>` for named groups).

**Example:** `(\w+)\s+\1` matches repeated words separated by space.
- `(\w+)` captures first word
- `\1` refers back to it
- Matches "hello hello", "test test" but not "hello world"

Useful for finding duplicates or enforcing patterns like matching quote types: `(['"]).*?\1` matches text inside matching quotes.
~~~

### ponytail: 116 answer tokens, 290 billed, 5 lines

~~~
A backreference lets you refer back to a captured group within the same regex pattern. It's written as `\1`, `\2`, etc., referring to the 1st, 2nd captured group.

**Example:** `\b(\w+)\s+\1\b` matches a word followed by the same word (like "hello hello" or "test test").

The `(\w+)` captures a word, and `\1` requires that exact word to appear again after whitespace.
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 77 answer tokens, 246 billed, 3 lines

~~~
Backreference: `\1`, `\2`, etc. refer to text captured by earlier groups in the same pattern, letting you match repeated content.

Example: `(\w+)\s+\1` matches duplicate words — "hello hello" or "test test". The `\1` matches whatever the first group captured.
~~~

### chisle: 142 answer tokens, 258 billed, 9 lines

~~~
Backreference: a way to refer back to a captured group within the same regex pattern or replacement. Syntax: `\1`, `\2`, etc., for the 1st, 2nd captured group.

Example: match a repeated word.
```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```
Matches "hello hello" or "the the" — `(\w+)` captures the first word, `\1` matches the exact same text again.

In a replacement: `s/(\w+)\s+\1/$1/` removes the duplicate, leaving one copy.
~~~

## default-s1 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 170 answer tokens, 432 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST** is simpler to build and cache (leverages HTTP semantics and CDNs), with mature tooling and straightforward versioning; **GraphQL** eliminates over-fetching and gives clients precise queries they need, but requires more complex server implementation and custom caching strategies.

The main tradeoff: REST favors simplicity and operational ease (great for public APIs, mobile with limited bandwidth), while GraphQL favors query flexibility and developer experience (better for complex data needs, internal APIs, frontends with varied requirements). REST scales caching effortlessly; GraphQL needs thought. GraphQL's learning curve is steeper but pays off if you have clients with heterogeneous data needs; REST is immediately familiar.

Which would you lean toward for your use case?
~~~

### caveman: 205 answer tokens, 464 billed, 16 lines

~~~
**REST**
- Simple, predictable, cacheable (HTTP layer)
- Over/underfetch: clients get fixed response shapes, often retrieve unwanted data
- Multiple endpoints to maintain and version
- Clear semantics (GET/POST/PUT map to CRUD)

**GraphQL**
- Fetch exactly what you need in one request
- Single endpoint, schema-driven, strong validation
- Steeper learning curve, N+1 queries without batching/dataloader
- Caching harder (POST, no HTTP cache leverage)

**Pick REST if:** straightforward CRUD, public API with simple clients, heavy caching matters.
**Pick GraphQL if:** complex nested queries, mobile clients (bandwidth-sensitive), rapid client iteration.

Best recommendation: **start REST**, migrate to GraphQL at second API if complexity justifies it. GraphQL's benefit mostly shows with many client shapes and deep data relationships.
~~~

### ponytail: 184 answer tokens, 520 billed, 7 lines

~~~
**REST for most cases, GraphQL if you have a good reason.**

REST wins if your API is straightforward CRUD with predictable client patterns—it's simpler to cache, reason about, and operate. GraphQL wins when you have multiple clients with wildly different data needs (web app wants 3 fields, mobile app wants 7, internal dashboard wants 12) or deeply nested relationships that REST would need multiple roundtrips to fetch.

**Main tradeoff:** REST forces shape discipline and http-native caching; GraphQL trades that simplicity for fetch precision but costs you in complexity (query parsing, caching strategies become custom, monitoring gets noisier). Most teams overestimate the over-fetch problem and underestimate the operational overhead.

What shape are your early clients, and do you expect that to diverge?
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 193 answer tokens, 401 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: simple to build, caches naturally (HTTP cache layer), standard tooling everywhere, works great for straightforward CRUD. Downside: clients often fetch too much data (over-fetching) or make multiple requests (under-fetching).

**GraphQL**: clients request exactly what they need → less bandwidth, one round trip instead of N. Cost: more complex server, standard caching doesn't work (POST requests), easy to write slow queries (N+1 problem), steeper learning curve for teams.

**Pick REST if** data shape is stable and clients are mostly internal/controlled. **Pick GraphQL if** you have diverse clients (web, mobile, third-party) with different data needs, or if network bandwidth is a pain point. For new APIs with unclear requirements, REST is lower risk; GraphQL pays off once client diversity forces you to build it anyway.
~~~

### chisle: 205 answer tokens, 390 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST**: mature, easier to cache (HTTP semantics), simpler to build and debug, standard tooling everywhere. Downsides → clients overfetch data (get fields you don't need) or underfetch (need multiple requests), proliferation of endpoints as requirements grow.

**GraphQL**: clients request exactly what they need in one query → bandwidth savings, fewer round trips. Downsides → HTTP caching breaks down (everything's POST), server-side complexity (query planning, N+1 queries), harder to reason about performance, steeper team learning curve.

**Call**: REST for internal/simple APIs, stable contracts, public APIs you want to cache heavily. GraphQL for client-driven product (mobile apps, varied frontend needs) or rapid iteration. If unsure, REST first—migrate later if overfetch becomes a real bottleneck. Many teams pick REST and add field filtering (sparse fieldsets) instead.
~~~

The directory appears to be empty. I'll create a sample Node.js/Express user profile API with an integrated cache layer using a popular pattern. This will show you how to implement caching for profile endpoints.
<function_calls>
<invoke name="Write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/cache.js</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">class CacheLayer {
  constructor(ttl = 300000) {
    this.cache = new Map();
    this.ttl = ttl;
  }

  get(key) {
    const item = this.cache.get(key);
    if (!item) return null;

    if (Date.now() > item.expiresAt) {
      this.cache.delete(key);
      return null;
    }

    return item.value;
  }

  set(key, value) {
    this.cache.set(key, {
      value,
      expiresAt: Date.now() + this.ttl,
    });
  }

  delete(key) {
    this.cache.delete(key);
  }

  clear() {
    this.cache.clear();
  }

  has(key) {
    const item = this.cache.get(key);
    if (!item) return false;

    if (Date.now() > item.expiresAt) {
      this.cache.delete(key);
      return false;
    }

    return true;
  }
}

module.exports = CacheLayer;
</parameter>
</invoke>
</function_calls>
<function_calls>
<invoke name="Write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/userProfileAPI.js</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">const express = require('express');
const CacheLayer = require('./cache');

const app = express();
const profileCache = new CacheLayer(300000); // 5 minute TTL

// Mock database
const userDatabase = {
  1: { id: 1, name: 'Alice Johnson', email: 'alice@example.com', role: 'admin' },
  2: { id: 2, name: 'Bob Smith', email: 'bob@example.com', role: 'user' },
  3: { id: 3, name: 'Carol White', email: 'carol@example.com', role: 'user' },
};

function fetchUserFromDB(userId) {
  console.log(`[DB] Fetching user ${userId}`);
  return userDatabase[userId] || null;
}

// GET /users/:id - Get user profile with caching
app.get('/users/:id', (req, res) => {
  const { id } = req.params;
  const cacheKey = `user:${id}`;

  // Check cache
  const cachedProfile = profileCache.get(cacheKey);
  if (cachedProfile) {
    console.log(`[CACHE HIT] User ${id}`);
    return res.json({ data: cachedProfile, source: 'cache' });
  }

  // Fetch from database
  const user = fetchUserFromDB(id);
  if (!user) {
    return res.status(404).json({ error: 'User not found' });
  }

  // Store in cache
  profileCache.set(cacheKey, user);
  res.json({ data: user, source: 'database' });
});

// PUT /users/:id - Update user and invalidate cache
app.put('/users/:id', express.json(), (req, res) => {
  const { id } = req.params;
  const { name, email, role } = req.body;

  if (!userDatabase[id]) {
    return res.status(404).json({ error: 'User not found' });
  }

  // Update database
  userDatabase[id] = { id, name, email, role };

  // Invalidate cache
  const cacheKey = `user:${id}`;
  profileCache.delete(cacheKey);

  res.json({ data: userDatabase[id], message: 'User updated and cache cleared' });
});

// DELETE /cache/:id - Manually clear cache
app.delete('/cache/:id', (req, res) => {
  const { id } = req.params;
  const cacheKey = `user:${id}`;
  profileCache.delete(cacheKey);
  res.json({ message: `Cache cleared for user ${id}` });
});

// GET /cache/stats - Cache statistics
app.get('/cache/stats', (req, res) => {
  res.json({ cacheSize: profileCache.cache.size, ttl: profileCache.ttl });
});

const PORT = process.env.PORT || 3000;
app.listen(PORT, () => {
  console.log(`Server running on http://localhost:${PORT}`);
});

module.exports = app;
</parameter>
</invoke>
</function_calls>

I've created a cache layer for user profile API responses with two files:

**cache.js** — A reusable `CacheLayer` class that:
- Stores data with TTL (Time-To-Live), defaults to 5 minutes
- Auto-expires entries when TTL passes
- Provides `get()`, `set()`, `delete()`, `has()`, and `clear()` methods

**userProfileAPI.js** — An Express API that demonstrates:
- **GET /users/:id** — Returns cached profile if available, otherwise fetches from DB
- **PUT /users/:id** — Updates user and invalidates the cache
- **DELETE /cache/:id** — Manually clears cache for a specific user
- **GET /cache/stats** — Shows cache statistics

The cache includes automatic expiration and invalidation on updates. You can customize the TTL by passing a different value when instantiating `CacheLayer`. Would you like me to show you a Redis-based cache implementation, add cache statistics tracking, or adapt this to a different framework?

from typing import TypedDict


class UserProfile(TypedDict):
    id: int
    name: str
    email: str

def fetch_user_profile(user_id: int) -> UserProfile:

def clear_user_cache(user_id: int | None = None) -> None:

    else:
        fetch_user_profile.cache_clear()

If you want a quick example, I'll assume Node.js + in-memory cache with 5min TTL:

```javascript
const userCache = new Map();
const CACHE_TTL = 5 * 60 * 1000; // 5 minutes

function getCacheKey(userId) {
  return `user:${userId}`;
}

function isCacheValid(cacheEntry) {
  return Date.now() - cacheEntry.timestamp < CACHE_TTL;
}

export async function getUserProfile(userId) {
  const key = getCacheKey(userId);
  const cached = userCache.get(key);

  if (cached && isCacheValid(cached)) {
    return cached.data;
  }

  const profile = await fetchUserFromDB(userId);
  userCache.set(key, { data: profile, timestamp: Date.now() });
  return profile;
}

export function invalidateUserCache(userId) {
  userCache.delete(getCacheKey(userId));
}
```

I don't see any existing files. Can you share your search input component code, or let me show you a common pattern? Here's a **React example** with debounce:

```jsx
import { useState, useCallback, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  const handleSearch = useCallback((query) => {
    if (timeoutRef.current) {
      clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    }

    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
      if (query.trim()) {
        fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
          .then(res => res.json())
          .then(data => setResults(data));
      } else {
        setResults([]);
      }
    }, 300); // 300ms debounce delay
  }, []);

  return (
    <div>
      <input
        type="text"
        placeholder="Search..."
        onChange={(e) => handleSearch(e.target.value)}
      />
      <ul>
        {results.map(item => <li key={item.id}>{item.name}</li>)}
      </ul>
    </div>
  );
}

export default SearchInput;
```

**Key changes:**
- `useRef` stores the timeout ID so we can cancel previous requests
- `setTimeout` delays the API call by 300ms
- `clearTimeout` cancels the previous timer when a new keystroke arrives
- Only the latest keystroke's timer runs to completion

  const [debouncedValue, setDebouncedValue] = useState(value);

    return () => clearTimeout(handler);

I need to know your tech stack—are you using React, Vue, vanilla JS, or something else? For now, here's the minimal universal approach:

```javascript
// Simple debounce utility
function debounce(func, delay) {
  let timeout;
  return function(...args) {
    clearTimeout(timeout);
    timeout = setTimeout(() => func(...args), delay);
  };
}

// Use it
const searchInput = document.getElementById('search');
const apiCall = debounce(query => {
  fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
    .then(r => r.json())
    .then(data => console.log(data));
}, 300);

searchInput.addEventListener('input', e => apiCall(e.target.value));
```

**React?** Use this instead:

```javascript
const [query, setQuery] = useState('');

useEffect(() => {
  const timer = setTimeout(() => {
    if (query) fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`);
  }, 300);
  return () => clearTimeout(timer);
}, [query]);

return <input onChange={e => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
```

import { useState, useCallback, useRef } from 'react';

function SearchInput() {
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);
  const [loading, setLoading] = useState(false);
  const timeoutRef = useRef(null);

  const handleSearch = useCallback((query) => {
    if (timeoutRef.current) clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current);
    
    if (!query.trim()) {
      setResults([]);
      return;
    }

    setLoading(true);
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
      fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`)
        .then(res => res.json())
        .then(data => setResults(data))
        .finally(() => setLoading(false));
    }, 300); // 300ms delay
  }, []);

  return (
    <div>
      <input
        type="text"
        placeholder="Search..."
        onChange={(e) => handleSearch(e.target.value)}
      />
      {loading && <div>Loading...</div>}
      <ul>
        {results.map(item => <li key={item.id}>{item.name}</li>)}
      </ul>
    </div>
  );
}

export default SearchInput;
```

**Key changes:** `useRef` holds timeout ID → `clearTimeout` on each keystroke cancels previous pending call. `setTimeout` delays API hit by 300ms. Only fires if 300ms passes without another keystroke.

searchInput.addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  clearTimeout(timeoutId);
  timeoutId = setTimeout(() => {
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(e.target.value)}`)
      .then(res => res.json())
      .then(data => console.log(data));
  }, 300);
});
```

## default-s2 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 434 answer tokens, 528 billed, 32 lines

~~~
# Database Connection Pooling

## How it works

Connection pooling maintains a cache of open database connections that can be reused. Instead of creating a new connection each time your application needs to query the database:

1. **Initialization**: At startup, the pool creates N pre-established connections to the database (e.g., 10-20)
2. **Request**: When your app needs a connection, it asks the pool for one from the available set
3. **Reuse**: The connection is borrowed, used for the query, then returned to the pool
4. **Return**: The connection stays open and ready for the next request
5. **Scaling**: If all connections are in use, the pool can either queue requests or create temporary additional connections (up to a configured maximum)

## Why it helps

**Connection overhead is expensive**: Creating a new database connection requires:
- TCP handshake
- Authentication
- Session initialization
These add significant latency (often 10-100ms per connection).

**Key benefits**:

| Problem | Solution |
|---------|----------|
| **Latency** | Reusing warm connections eliminates handshake/auth overhead for most requests |
| **Resource efficiency** | Connections consume server memory/processes. Pooling reuses a small fixed set instead of creating thousands |
| **Throughput** | Queuing requests in the pool prevents connection storms that exhaust server limits |
| **Concurrency** | Let 1000 app threads share 20 DB connections smoothly via queuing |

**Example**: Without pooling, 100 concurrent requests might create 100 connections (heavy on the DB). With a pool of 20, those 100 requests queue and reuse the same 20 connections, reducing server load dramatically.

Most frameworks and ORMs (SQLAlchemy, Prisma, Node.js drivers) include built-in pooling, usually with sensible defaults.
~~~

### caveman: 269 answer tokens, 677 billed, 22 lines

~~~
Pool = collection of open DB connections reused across requests.

**Without pool:** Each request opens connection → handshake overhead (auth, SSL negotiation) → query → close. Repeated per request = slow.

**With pool:** Connections stay open, waiting. Request grabs one from pool, uses it, returns it. Next request reuses same connection. No handshake per request.

**Why helps:**
- Skip auth/SSL handshake overhead (expensive)
- Reuse existing connections → fast
- Limit max connections (prevent resource exhaustion)
- Handle load spikes without spawning unlimited connections

**How it works:**
1. App starts → pool creates N connections (default ~5-10)
2. Request needs DB → pool gives available connection
3. Request uses it → returns to pool
4. If all busy → queue request or timeout
5. Idle connections close after timeout (configurable)

**Numbers:** 100 requests hitting pool reuses ~10 connections. Without pool = open 100 connections = 100x overhead + 100x handshake cost.

Tldr: Handshakes expensive. Reuse connections. Fast + efficient under load.
~~~

### ponytail: 395 answer tokens, 568 billed, 20 lines

~~~
## How it works

A connection pool maintains a fixed set of open database connections. When your app needs one, it checks out a connection from the pool, uses it, then returns it. The pool reuses that connection for the next request instead of creating a fresh one each time.

```
Request → Pool (has 5 open connections) → Grab one → Query → Return it → Done
Next request → Grab a different one (or wait if all in use)
```

## Why it helps

**Connection creation is expensive.** Opening a TCP connection, authenticating, and setting up state takes ~100ms or more. If every request opens a fresh connection, you're burning time on setup overhead instead of queries.

**Prevents resource exhaustion.** Each database connection consumes memory and file descriptors. Without a pool, 1000 concurrent requests would try to open 1000 connections, crashing the app or database. A pool caps this at (say) 20 connections, queuing extra requests to wait for a free one.

**Improves throughput.** Reusing connections amortizes setup cost across many queries. A single query isn't faster, but your app handles 10x more requests-per-second because the overhead is spread thin.

**Example:** A web app handling 100 req/s without pooling might need 100 connections open if requests average 100ms. With a pool of 10, those same requests share the same 10 connections, cycling through. Same work, 1/10th the resource footprint.

Most frameworks (Django ORM, SQLAlchemy, Node's connection libraries) have pooling built in. You usually just set `pool_size` and `max_overflow` (how many extra connections to allow when demand spikes).
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 354 answer tokens, 484 billed, 18 lines

~~~
**What it is:** Database connection pooling maintains a set of open connections ready to use. App requests a connection from the pool instead of creating a new one → after use, connection returns to the pool rather than closing.

**Why it helps:**

Opening a connection is expensive. TCP handshake, SSL negotiation (if encrypted), auth, catalog/schema setup. 50–300ms typical. Pooling skips this on every request by reusing warm connections.

Resource ceiling. Each open connection consumes server RAM and file descriptors. Without pooling, 1,000 concurrent requests = 1,000 open connections. Pooling caps this—maybe 20 connections serve those 1,000 requests in turns, freeing resources for other app processes.

**How it works:**

Pool size = min + max connections. At startup, opens `min` (e.g., 5). When a request needs one:
- If a connection sits idle in the pool → lease it immediately (< 1ms).
- If all are leased and pool size < max → create a new one.
- If all are leased and pool is full → request waits (queue) for one to be returned.

Connection validity checked before hand-off (some DBs auto-kill idle connections, so pool runs a quick `SELECT 1` to verify it still works).

**Trade-off:** More pooled connections → lower request latency (less waiting), but higher steady-state resource use. Too few → requests queue; too many → wasted RAM. Tuning depends on request volume and DB capacity.
~~~

### chisle: 354 answer tokens, 527 billed, 11 lines

~~~
**Connection pooling**: instead of creating a new database connection for each request (expensive: TCP handshake, authentication, initialization), a pool maintains a set of pre-opened connections. Requests grab an idle connection from the pool, use it, return it. When a request arrives and no connections are free, it waits or gets queued.

**Why it helps**:

**Speed** → skipping connection setup per request (handshake + auth + parsing config) cuts latency by orders of magnitude. A new TCP connection + DB auth can take 100–500ms; reusing an open connection is ~1ms.

**Resource efficiency** → operating systems have hard limits on file descriptors and open sockets (thousands, not millions). Without pooling, 1000 concurrent requests = 1000 connections consuming kernel memory, file handles, and DB listener slots. With pooling (say, 20–50 connections shared across all requests), you stay under those limits and don't exhaust the database's connection listener.

**Throughput** → freed from connection overhead, the same hardware handles more requests. Database CPU isn't burned on auth handshakes; app server threads spend less time blocking on I/O setup.

The tradeoff: pool needs tuning (too small = queueing delays, too large = wasted server memory and DB listener slots) and adds latency if requests arrive faster than the pool can handle them. But in practice, a modest pool (5–20 connections for small services, 50–100 for large ones) is almost always faster and cheaper than opening a new connection per request.
~~~

## default-s2 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 164 answer tokens, 250 billed, 10 lines

~~~
A backreference in a regex is a way to match the exact same text that was previously captured by a group. It's denoted as `\1`, `\2`, etc., referring to the 1st, 2nd captured group, or `\k<name>` for named groups.

**Example:** Match repeated words:
```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

This matches a word followed by whitespace and that same word again. So it matches `hello hello` or `test test`, but not `hello world`.

The `(\w+)` captures the first word into group 1, and `\1` backreferences it, requiring the second word to be identical.
~~~

### caveman: 117 answer tokens, 296 billed, 8 lines

~~~
Backreference refers to previously captured group in same regex. Reuse it later in pattern to match identical text.

Example: `(\w+)\s+\1` matches repeated words.
- `(\w+)` captures word, store in group 1
- `\s+` match spaces
- `\1` backreference — must match exact same text as group 1

Matches `"hello hello"` or `"test test"`. Won't match `"hello world"`.
~~~

### ponytail: 146 answer tokens, 289 billed, 10 lines

~~~
A backreference lets you refer back to a captured group within the same regex or replacement. It's written as `\1`, `\2`, etc., or `$1`, `$2` in replacements.

**Example:** Match repeated words:
```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

This matches a word followed by the same word again: `hello hello`, `test test`, but not `hello world`.

In a replacement, `\1` refers to whatever the first group captured, so you can deduplicate: `hello hello` → `hello` using the pattern above with replacement `$1`.
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 138 answer tokens, 305 billed, 7 lines

~~~
Backreference: a placeholder in a regex that refers back to a previously captured group, letting you match the same text again.

Syntax: `\1` for first group, `\2` for second, etc.

Example: `(\w+) \1` matches repeated words: "hello hello" or "foo foo", but not "hello world". The `(\w+)` captures the word; `\1` requires that exact same word to appear again.

Use case: finding duplicates, validating symmetry (like HTML tags `<(\w+)>.*?</\1>`).
~~~

### chisle: 116 answer tokens, 287 billed, 7 lines

~~~
Backreference: a way to match the same text a regex already captured. Denoted `\1`, `\2`, etc. — refers back to the 1st, 2nd captured group.

Example: find doubled words.
```regex
(\w+)\s+\1
```
Captures a word in group 1, matches spaces, then `\1` requires the *exact same word* again. Matches "hello hello" or "the the", but not "hello world".
~~~

## default-s2 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 182 answer tokens, 426 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST** favors simplicity and caching: stateless endpoints, standard HTTP semantics, and straightforward browser/CDN caching, but clients suffer from over/under-fetching and need multiple requests to traverse relationships. **GraphQL** gives clients exactly what they ask for and resolves related data in a single request, but servers become more complex to implement securely and efficiently, and query caching is harder.

**Recommendation:** Choose REST if you're building a simple CRUD API with well-defined resources and predictable access patterns; choose GraphQL if your clients are diverse (mobile, web, third-party) or need fine-grained control over what they fetch. The tradeoff is implementation complexity vs. client efficiency.

What's your use case—are you serving a single frontend, or many different client types?
~~~

### caveman: 147 answer tokens, 515 billed, 7 lines

~~~
**REST**: Simple, cacheable (HTTP caching built-in), multiple endpoints. Over-fetches data, needs multiple requests for related data.

**GraphQL**: Fetch exact fields in one request, single endpoint. No HTTP caching (needs custom layer), steeper learning curve, complex query parsing on server.

**Main tradeoff**: REST is simpler to build and cache; GraphQL is more bandwidth-efficient and flexible for clients. Pick REST for straightforward, cacheable APIs with stable schemas. Pick GraphQL if clients need varied data shapes or you want to minimize requests and over-fetching.

What's your team familiar with? New project constraints?
~~~

### ponytail: 162 answer tokens, 496 billed, 3 lines

~~~
**REST** wins if your data is hierarchical and clients consume similar shapes (fewer versions needed, HTTP caching just works, dead simple). **GraphQL** wins if clients need wildly different data subsets from interconnected resources (one endpoint, no over-fetching, schema is the docs).

The real tradeoff: REST trades flexibility for simplicity; GraphQL trades operational simplicity (caching, monitoring, DOS surface) for client ergonomics. Pick REST unless you're shipping to many diverse clients or your domain is deeply relational (think social graph, e-commerce with variants/reviews/recommendations). If unsure, start REST—migration to GraphQL is cheap later, but over-engineering the API upfront is sunk cost.
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 275 answer tokens, 547 billed, 7 lines

~~~
**REST**: Simple, cacheable (HTTP layer), stateless. Each resource = endpoint. Related data → multiple requests (N+1 problem). Versioning friction (`/v1`, `/v2`). Mobile-friendly because you control the payload, but clients often get excess data.

**GraphQL**: One request → exactly what you asked for. No over-fetching, no under-fetching. Steep server complexity: needs a resolver per field, N+1queries lurk (fetch user, then 100 friends' profiles = 101 DB queries). HTTP caching breaks (always POST). Clientside learning curve. Payload inspection hard.

**Pick REST if**: API is simple, caching matters, team knows it, clients are diverse (web, mobile, IoT—each can request what it needs via query params or multiple calls). **Pick GraphQL if**: clients are tightly coupled (single web app, iOS app—you control both), data is deeply nested, mobile bandwidth is a constraint and you'll invest in resolver optimization + dataloader to dodge N+1.

Middle ground: REST + sparse fieldsets / query params for selective fields (GitHub's API does this well). Slower to build than REST, faster queries than naive GraphQL.
~~~

### chisle: 183 answer tokens, 443 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST:** Simple, cacheable, stateless. Client makes multiple requests → overfetching (extra fields you don't need) and underfetching (need a third endpoint). Server-side caching via HTTP headers works out of the box. Familiar to most teams.

**GraphQL:** One request, fetch exactly what you need → no overfetch, no underfetch. Steep learning curve, server complexity higher. HTTP caching breaks (POST requests, field-level queries don't map to cache keys). Needs database query cost awareness or risks N+1 bugs.

**Pick REST** if schema is stable, caching matters, team prefers simplicity. **Pick GraphQL** if clients have wildly different data needs (mobile vs desktop, tight bandwidth constraints) or you're building a platform many teams query.
~~~

## large-s1 / apidesign

> Design the REST API for a multi-tenant document store: resources, auth model, pagination, versioning and error format. Explain the decisions.

### vanilla: 2668 answer tokens, 2990 billed, 289 lines

~~~
I'll design a production-ready REST API for a multi-tenant document store with clear reasoning for each decision.

## Resources & Endpoints

```
# Tenant-scoped resources (tenant isolation enforced at every level)
GET    /api/v1/documents                    # List documents in tenant
POST   /api/v1/documents                    # Create document
GET    /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}           # Retrieve document
PATCH  /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}           # Update document
DELETE /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}           # Delete document

GET    /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}/versions  # Document version history
GET    /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}/versions/{version_id}  # Specific version

POST   /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}/share     # Grant access
DELETE /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}/share/{user_id}  # Revoke access

GET    /api/v1/collections                  # List collections (grouping)
POST   /api/v1/collections                  # Create collection
PATCH  /api/v1/collections/{coll_id}        # Update collection

# Tenant info
GET    /api/v1/tenant                       # Current tenant info
GET    /api/v1/tenant/members               # Tenant members
```

**Decision: Tenant in path vs header**

I chose **implicit tenant routing** (tenant extracted from auth token), not explicit path like `/api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents`. 

**Why:** 
- Prevents accidental cross-tenant access through API misuse
- Simpler client code (no tenant ID plumbing)
- If a user has access to multiple tenants, they switch via re-auth, not URL manipulation
- Standard SaaS pattern (Stripe, GitHub, Figma don't put org in every path)

---

## Authentication & Multi-Tenancy

```
# Request header
Authorization: Bearer <jwt_token>

# JWT payload example
{
  "sub": "user_12345",
  "tenant_id": "org_789",
  "email": "alice@company.com",
  "roles": ["editor"],           # tenant-scoped roles
  "permissions": ["read", "write", "share"],
  "iat": 1700000000,
  "exp": 1700003600
}
```

**Decision: JWT with tenant claim, not API keys**

**Why:**
- **Tenant isolation**: Every request is tagged with `tenant_id` from token; server rejects if `tenant_id` in token ≠ resource's tenant
- **User identity**: Clear audit trail (who did what, when)
- **Revocation**: Logout invalidates all tokens for that user
- **Stateless**: No server session storage needed for horizontal scaling

**Fallback for service-to-service:**
```
# API key with tenant binding (for integrations)
X-API-Key: sk_live_abc123
X-API-Tenant: org_789    # Required; API key is tenant-locked server-side
```

**Server-side check on every request:**
```
token.tenant_id == resource.tenant_id  // Enforce strict isolation
```

---

## Pagination

**Decision: Cursor-based**

```json
GET /api/v1/documents?limit=20&cursor=abc123def456

Response:
{
  "data": [
    {
      "id": "doc_1",
      "title": "Q4 Budget",
      "created_at": "2026-11-15T10:30:00Z",
      "updated_at": "2026-11-18T14:22:00Z",
      "version": 3
    }
    // ... 19 more
  ],
  "pagination": {
    "has_more": true,
    "next_cursor": "xyz789uvw012",
    "prev_cursor": "mno345pqr678",
    "total_count": null    // Not provided; see Why
  }
}
```

**Why cursor over offset:**
- **Consistency**: If documents are added/deleted mid-scroll, cursor-based pagination doesn't skip or duplicate rows
- **Performance**: No need to count total rows or skip large offsets (scales to billions of documents)
- **Infinite scroll**: Better UX for client apps
- Omit `total_count` to avoid expensive COUNT queries; clients don't need it for navigation

**Filtering & sorting:**
```
GET /api/v1/documents?cursor=abc&limit=20&sort=-updated_at&owner=user_123&status=active
```

---

## Versioning

**Decision: URL-based API version + document-level versioning**

```
# API versioning (breaking changes only)
/api/v1/documents         # Stable API; additive changes within
/api/v2/documents         # Only if breaking change needed (rare)

# Document versioning (automatic per update)
GET /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}
→ Returns latest version automatically

GET /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}/versions
→ Lists all versions with metadata:
{
  "data": [
    {
      "version_id": "v_5",
      "version_number": 5,
      "created_at": "2026-11-18T14:22:00Z",
      "created_by": "user_123",
      "change_summary": "Updated budget figures",
      "size_bytes": 2048
    }
  ]
}

GET /api/v1/documents/{doc_id}/versions/v_3
→ Retrieve specific version (read-only)
```

**Why incremental v1→v2 (not date-based):**
- Clients pin to `/v1` and upgrade deliberately
- Minimal versioning overhead (break only on truly incompatible changes)
- Clear communication of stability

**Why auto-version every document change:**
- Automatic audit trail (who changed what)
- Rollback capability (restore from version)
- Compare/diff endpoint (compare versions) could be added later

---

## Error Format

```json
// All errors follow this structure
{
  "error": {
    "code": "UNAUTHORIZED",
    "message": "User does not have 'write' permission for this document.",
    "details": {
      "required_permission": "write",
      "user_id": "user_123",
      "doc_id": "doc_456"
    },
    "request_id": "req_7f8a9b1c2d3e",
    "timestamp": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z"
  }
}

// HTTP Status + Code mapping
401 Unauthorized          → UNAUTHORIZED (token missing/invalid)
403 Forbidden             → FORBIDDEN (token valid, but no permission)
404 Not Found             → NOT_FOUND (resource doesn't exist for this tenant)
409 Conflict              → CONFLICT (version mismatch, stale update)
422 Unprocessable Entity  → INVALID_PAYLOAD (validation error)
429 Too Many Requests     → RATE_LIMITED (quota exceeded)
500 Internal Server Error → INTERNAL_ERROR (unexpected server failure)
```

**Decision: Semantic codes + request_id**

**Why:**
- **Clients act, not parse messages**: Code is machine-readable; message is human-readable
- **Request ID for debugging**: Ops team can correlate logs across services
- **Details object**: Extra context (which permission was denied, etc.) without overloading the message
- **Consistency**: Same structure for all errors; easy to handle in client middleware

**Validation error example:**
```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "INVALID_PAYLOAD",
    "message": "Request validation failed.",
    "details": {
      "fields": [
        {
          "field": "title",
          "issue": "required"
        },
        {
          "field": "metadata",
          "issue": "must be valid JSON object"
        }
      ]
    },
    "request_id": "req_abc123"
  }
}
```

---

## Securing Multi-Tenancy

**Critical checks:**
1. Every read/write enforces `token.tenant_id == resource.tenant_id`
2. Queries filter by tenant automatically:
   ```sql
   SELECT * FROM documents WHERE tenant_id = ? AND doc_id = ?
   ```
3. No cross-tenant API calls (service-to-service uses internal channels)
4. Rate limits per tenant (not global):
   ```
   X-RateLimit-Limit: 1000
   X-RateLimit-Remaining: 847
   X-RateLimit-Reset: 1700003600
   X-RateLimit-Key: org_789  // Tenant-scoped
   ```

**Example request/response:**

```
POST /api/v1/documents
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9...
Content-Type: application/json

{
  "title": "2026 Strategic Plan",
  "collection_id": "coll_12",
  "content": "...",
  "metadata": {
    "category": "planning",
    "confidential": true
  }
}

201 Created
{
  "data": {
    "id": "doc_789",
    "tenant_id": "org_456",
    "title": "2026 Strategic Plan",
    "version": 1,
    "created_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
    "updated_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
    "created_by": "user_123",
    "owner": "user_123"
  }
}
```

---

## Summary of Decisions

| Aspect | Choice | Why |
|--------|--------|-----|
| **Tenant routing** | Implicit (via auth token) | Prevents accidental cross-tenant access; simpler UX |
| **Auth** | JWT + tenant claim | Stateless, audit trail, tenant isolation enforced at every request |
| **Pagination** | Cursor-based | Scales to large datasets; consistent with additions/deletions |
| **API versioning** | URL path (`/v1`, `/v2`) | Explicit opt-in to breaking changes; rare and deliberate |
| **Document versioning** | Automatic per update | Audit trail + rollback; no manual burden |
| **Error format** | Semantic code + request_id | Machine-readable actions + debuggability |
| **Errors** | Separate 403 Forbidden from 401 Unauthorized | Clear permission vs. auth failure distinction |

This design balances **security** (strict tenant isolation), **scalability** (cursor pagination, stateless auth), and **developer experience** (consistent error handling, clear versioning). Would you like me to elaborate on any section or discuss trade-offs for specific scenarios?
~~~

### caveman: 1595 answer tokens, 1782 billed, 190 lines

~~~
# Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API

## Resources

```
Tenant-scoped:
  GET/POST   /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents
  GET/PUT/DELETE /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}
  GET        /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions
  GET        /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/{docId}/versions/{versionId}

  GET/POST   /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/folders
  GET/PUT/DELETE /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/folders/{folderId}

Shared/admin:
  POST       /api/v1/auth/token
  POST       /api/v1/tenants (admin only)
  GET        /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId} (self or admin)
```

**Decision:** Tenant ID in path enforces isolation at routing layer. No cross-tenant leaks via query mistakes. Single namespace for all endpoints.

---

## Authentication & Authorization

**Token-based with tenant binding:**

```json
POST /api/v1/auth/token
{
  "email": "user@company.com",
  "password": "..."
}

Response:
{
  "access_token": "eyJ...",
  "token_type": "Bearer",
  "expires_in": 3600,
  "tenant_id": "org_xyz"
}
```

**Header:**
```
Authorization: Bearer {access_token}
```

**Token claims include:**
- `sub` (user ID)
- `tenant_id` (bound tenant)
- `roles` ["viewer", "editor", "admin"]
- `iat`, `exp`

**Decision:**
- Bearer tokens (stateless, scalable). JWT includes tenant_id so each request is self-contained.
- Roles in token avoid per-request permission lookups.
- Tenant_id in claims means middleware can reject cross-tenant requests before controller runs.
- Password flow shown; swap in OAuth2 client credentials for service-to-service.

---

## Pagination

**Cursor-based (not offset):**

```
GET /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents?limit=20&cursor=abc123
```

**Response:**
```json
{
  "data": [...],
  "pagination": {
    "next_cursor": "def456",
    "has_more": true,
    "count": 20
  }
}
```

**Decision:**
- Cursor avoids offset problems (deletions mid-page, O(n) scans on large datasets).
- Encoded cursor hides implementation; can switch from timestamp to keyset without API change.
- `has_more` boolean cheap and clear.
- Limit capped server-side (e.g., max 100) to prevent DoS.

---

## Versioning

**Immutable versions + current pointer:**

```
POST /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents
{
  "title": "Q4 Budget",
  "content": "..."
}

Response 201:
{
  "id": "doc_xyz",
  "version_id": "v_1",
  "version_number": 1,
  "created_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
  "created_by": "user_123",
  "current": true
}

PUT /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/doc_xyz
{
  "content": "updated..."
}

Response 200: same structure, version_number: 2, new version_id
```

**Get version history:**
```
GET /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/doc_xyz/versions
→ lists all versions, newest first
```

**Get specific version:**
```
GET /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/documents/doc_xyz/versions/v_1
→ immutable snapshot
```

**Decision:**
- Each PUT is a new version. No PATCH (simpler: avoid partial updates, concurrent edit conflicts).
- Version ID + number both: ID for linking, number for UI ("version 3").
- Immutable versions prevent audit trail corruption.
- Metadata (created_by, created_at) on each version for compliance.
- No "rollback" endpoint; rollback = PUT the old version content (audit trail preserved).

---

## Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "RESOURCE_NOT_FOUND",
    "message": "Document doc_xyz not found in tenant org_abc",
    "status": 404,
    "timestamp": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
    "request_id": "req_def789",
    "details": {
      "resource_type": "document",
      "resource_id": "doc_xyz"
    }
  }
}
```

**Common codes:**
```
RESOURCE_NOT_FOUND (404)
FORBIDDEN (403) — no permission or cross-tenant access
INVALID_REQUEST (400) — schema validation, missing field
CONFLICT (409) — concurrent edit, optimistic lock failed
RATE_LIMITED (429) — quota exceeded
INTERNAL_ERROR (500) — unhandled exception
```

**Decision:**
- Consistent envelope (error.code, not error_code or type).
- Status code + symbolic code (clients read code, not status).
- request_id for logs (user→support→tracing).
- timestamp helps debugging timezone issues.
- details field optional, context-dependent (not all errors need it).
- message is human-readable, not error.id-only.

---

## Cross-Cutting Decisions

| Decision | Rationale |
|----------|-----------|
| **Path versioning** (`/v1/`) | Breaks client caches cleanly. v2 coexists during migration. |
| **No field filtering** | Simple. If needed later, use sparse fieldsets (`?fields=id,title`), don't add sub-endpoints. |
| **Timestamps in ISO 8601** | Standard, timezone-safe, sortable. |
| **Soft deletes** | Audit trail + recovery window. DELETE returns 204 (no body). Physical delete job runs async. |
| **No POST to fetch** | Use GET + query params, not `POST /documents/search`. Cacheable, RESTful. |

This design scales multi-tenant isolation, keeps token scope tight, avoids pagination cliff-edges, bakes versioning into mutation semantics, and gives ops visibility. Ready to implement?
~~~

### ponytail: 1742 answer tokens, 2086 billed, 161 lines

~~~
# Multi-Tenant Document Store API Design

## Resources & Endpoints

```
Tenant-scoped (all requests include tenant_id in URL or header):

POST   /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents
GET    /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents
GET    /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}
PUT    /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}
DELETE /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}

GET    /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions
GET    /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/{version_id}
POST   /api/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/restore

Auth (tenant-independent):
POST   /api/v1/auth/login
POST   /api/v1/auth/token/refresh
```

**Decision:** Tenant ID in URL path, not header. Harder to accidentally cross-tenant, easier to audit in logs and understand request scope at a glance.

## Authentication & Authorization

```
Bearer token (JWT):
- Issued at login with sub=user_id, tenant_id claim
- Short-lived (15min), refresh token for new ones
- Token claims: { sub, tenant_id, exp, iat, scopes: ["read", "write"] }

Middleware enforces:
1. Token valid & not expired
2. Requested tenant_id == token's tenant_id claim (every route)
3. Required scope present (read for GET, write for PUT/POST/DELETE)
```

**Decision:** Tenant ID in JWT claim (not extracted from path only) prevents token misuse across tenants. Scope-based approach scales to fine-grained perms later without redesign.

## Pagination

```
Query params: ?limit=50&offset=0
Response body includes:
{
  "data": [...],
  "meta": {
    "limit": 50,
    "offset": 0,
    "total": 1200,
    "has_next": true
  }
}

Defaults: limit=20, max=100 (enforced server-side)
```

**Decision:** Offset-based, not cursor, keeps API simple for small result sets. `total` count lets clients know if more exists without an extra query. If data churn becomes a problem, migrate to cursor then — YAGNI until it matters.

## Versioning Strategy

```
URL path: /api/v1/ (major version only)
No breaking changes within v1; instead:
- New fields added with defaults, never removed
- Enum values never change, only added
- If field behavior must change → new field + deprecation period + docs

Example: changing "status" enum after launch → add "status_v2", mark "status" deprecated 6 months before removal.

No version in Accept header; path is clearer.
```

**Decision:** Single major version in URL because true breaking changes are rare in a document store API. Field deprecation scales to v2 when needed without redesign. Avoids content-negotiation complexity.

## Document Versioning

```
Every PUT creates a new version automatically; GET returns current.

GET /documents/{doc_id}
→ { id, content, version: 3, updated_at, ... }

GET /documents/{doc_id}/versions
→ paginated list of all versions with created_at, created_by

PUT /documents/{doc_id}
→ Increments version, keeps history. Returns new version.

POST /documents/{doc_id}/restore?version=2
→ Creates new version with content from v2, marks as "restored_from"
```

**Decision:** Auto-versioning on write, not opt-in; lossy updates are almost never the intent. Restore as POST (not PUT on a version) keeps versions immutable.

## Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "INVALID_TENANT",
    "message": "Tenant not found or access denied",
    "status": 403,
    "request_id": "req_abc123xyz",
    "details": {
      "tenant_id": "tenant-999"
    }
  }
}
```

**Common codes:**
- `INVALID_TENANT` (403) — tenant mismatch or not found
- `UNAUTHORIZED` (401) — no token or invalid
- `FORBIDDEN` (403) — valid token, insufficient scope
- `NOT_FOUND` (404) — document doesn't exist
- `CONFLICT` (409) — version mismatch on update (optimistic lock)
- `UNPROCESSABLE_ENTITY` (422) — invalid input (missing/bad fields)
- `RATE_LIMITED` (429) — quota exceeded
- `INTERNAL_ERROR` (500) — server fault

**Decision:** `code` is machine-readable (not just status), `request_id` enables tracing, `details` contextualizes the error without overloading message.

---

## Key Decisions Explained

| Decision | Why |
|----------|-----|
| **Tenant in path** | Prevents accidental cross-tenant reads; auditable; mental model matches resource hierarchy |
| **JWT with tenant claim** | No N+1 token lookups; claim is source of truth (server-side token revocation catches edge cases) |
| **Offset pagination** | Simpler than cursors for most queries; total count is free; switch to cursors if keyset pagination becomes critical |
| **No breaking changes in v1** | Deprecation + field versioning handles 90% of change; full v2 only if truly unavoidable. Keeps clients on v1 longer |
| **Auto-versioning** | Explicit versioning per-request is a foot-gun; automatic capture catches intent |
| **Optimistic lock (version field)** | Prevents silent lost updates; clients aware of concurrency without distributed locks |
| **Structured error codes** | Log parsing + client handling both need machine-readable labels; `message` is for humans |

## Example Flow

```
1. POST /api/v1/auth/login
   ← token (JWT with tenant_id claim)

2. POST /api/v1/tenants/acme-inc/documents
   Headers: Authorization: Bearer <token>
   Body: { "title": "Q4 Budget", "content": "..." }
   ← { id, version: 1, created_at, ... }

3. PUT /api/v1/tenants/acme-inc/documents/doc-42
   Body: { "content": "updated..." }
   ← { id, version: 2, updated_at, ... }

4. GET /api/v1/tenants/acme-inc/documents/doc-42/versions
   ← [{ version: 1, ... }, { version: 2, ... }]

5. POST /api/v1/tenants/acme-inc/documents/doc-42/restore?version=1
   ← { id, version: 3, restored_from: 1, ... }
```

This design is production-ready: scales to 1M+ documents per tenant, handles concurrency, audit-trail-friendly, and extensible without breaking clients.
~~~

# Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API Design

**Core Resources**

```
GET    /api/v1/documents              - list (paginated)
POST   /api/v1/documents              - create
GET    /api/v1/documents/{id}         - read
PATCH  /api/v1/documents/{id}         - update (creates version)
DELETE /api/v1/documents/{id}         - soft delete
GET    /api/v1/documents/{id}/versions - version history
GET    /api/v1/documents/{id}/versions/{versionId} - rollback restore
```

**Auth Model: Tenant via Token Claims**

Bearer JWT in `Authorization: Bearer <token>`. Token contains `tenant_id`, `user_id`, scopes. Server extracts tenant → all queries filtered by that tenant. Never trust tenant from request body/query.

Why: eliminates URL-scoped tenancy (which leaks via referer headers, logs, caches). Single auth mechanism for all endpoints. Revocation is instant (invalidate token). Scales to service-to-service auth (service principals with tenant scope).

Tradeoff: revocation is eventual if using long-lived tokens; mitigate with short TTL + refresh tokens.

**Pagination: Cursor-Based**

```json
GET /api/v1/documents?limit=20&cursor=eyJpZCI6IjEwIiwic29ydCI6ImNyZWF0ZWRfYXQifQ==

{
  "data": [{...}, {...}],
  "pagination": {
    "limit": 20,
    "has_more": true,
    "next_cursor": "eyJpZCI6IjMwIiwic29ydCI6ImNyZWF0ZWRfYXQifQ==",
    "prev_cursor": "..."
  }
}
```

Why: offset breaks on insertion/deletion (page 2 shifts mid-request). Cursor points to a row, stable under concurrent writes. Encodes sort key + ID → deterministic ordering. No N+1 risk.

**Versioning: URL Path + Field**

API version in URL (`/api/v1/`), document version in response & query:
```json
GET /api/v1/documents/{id}
{
  "id": "doc-123",
  "title": "Report Q4",
  "version": 5,
  "content": "...",
  "created_at": "2026-11-01T10:00:00Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-11-19T14:30:00Z"
}

GET /api/v1/documents/{id}?version=3  // immutable snapshot
```

Why: API version in URL → breaking changes have explicit pathway (v2 endpoints coexist). Document version in field → history queryable, no extra endpoint. `?version=` lets clients fetch old state without separate version resource.

**Error Format: Consistent Structure**

```json
400 Bad Request
{
  "error": {
    "code": "INVALID_DOCUMENT_ID",
    "message": "Document ID must be UUID v4",
    "details": {
      "field": "id",
      "provided": "not-a-uuid",
      "expected": "UUID v4"
    }
  }
}

403 Forbidden
{
  "error": {
    "code": "TENANT_ISOLATION_VIOLATION",
    "message": "Cannot access document from another tenant",
    "details": {
      "requested_id": "doc-456",
      "your_tenant": "tenant-789"
    }
  }
}

409 Conflict (concurrent update)
{
  "error": {
    "code": "VERSION_CONFLICT",
    "message": "Document was modified by another user",
    "details": {
      "current_version": 6,
      "your_version": 5
    }
  }
}
```

Standard HTTP codes: 400 (bad input), 401 (auth failed), 403 (tenant mismatch/permission), 404 (not found), 409 (version conflict), 429 (rate limit), 500+ (server).

**Concurrency: Optimistic Locking**

```json
PATCH /api/v1/documents/{id}
{
  "title": "Updated",
  "version": 5  // must match current version
}

Response 409 if stale → client re-fetches, retries.
```

Why: distributed writes without locks. Prevents silent overwrites in concurrent edits.

**Key Decisions Summary**

| Concern | Choice | Why |
|---------|--------|-----|
| Tenant isolation | Auth token claims | No URL leakage, instant revocation |
| Pagination | Cursor | Stable under concurrent writes |
| API versioning | URL path | Explicit, coexistent versions |
| Doc versioning | Response field + query param | Immutable snapshots, queryable history |
| Concurrency | Optimistic locking | Scales, detects conflicts |
| Errors | Structured + code + details | Programmatically parseable, debuggable |
~~~
