# Multi-Tenant Document Store API Design

## Resources

**Core hierarchy:**
- `POST /orgs` → create tenant (org)
- `GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents` → list docs in tenant
- `POST /orgs/{org_id}/documents` → create doc
- `GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}` → retrieve
- `PUT /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}` → update
- `GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions` → history
- `GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/{version_id}` → specific version
- `DELETE /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}` → soft-delete (recoverable)

**Members & sharing:**
- `POST /orgs/{org_id}/members` → add tenant member
- `POST /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/shares` → grant external access

**Why:** Tenant ID in every path enforces isolation at routing layer. Versioning as subresource keeps history queryable. Shares as separate resource handles both internal (members) and external (links/tokens) access patterns.

## Auth Model

**Two-layer:**

```
Header: Authorization: Bearer {tenant_token}
Header: X-Tenant-ID: {org_id}
```

Tenant token is a long-lived credential (API key or OAuth token with `org:{org_id}` scope). X-Tenant-ID must match the token's org, enforced server-side. User identity extracted from token claims or separate session cookie for browser clients.

**Why:** Decouples tenant identity (which silo to use) from user identity (who is acting). Token expiry + tenant claim signature prevents token reuse across orgs. If a token leaks, damage is scoped to one tenant. Tenant header is redundant but caught typos early (token says org-123, header says org-124 → reject).

**Permission model:** Role-based within tenant (owner, editor, viewer). Explicit sharing for cross-tenant or public links uses signed tokens: `GET /documents/{doc_id}?share_token={signed_token}`.

## Pagination

**Cursor-based for scalability:**

```json
{
  "data": [...],
  "pagination": {
    "cursor": "eyJsYXN0X2lkIjogIm9kYy0xMjM0In0=",
    "has_next": true,
    "limit": 50
  }
}
```

Request: `GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents?limit=50&cursor={cursor}`

Cursor is base64-encoded JSON: `{last_id: "...", last_created_at: "..."}`. Avoids offset skew (concurrent deletes break offset), scales to millions of docs. Clients get `has_next` to know when to stop.

**Why:** Offset pagination fails under concurrent mutations (delete mid-scroll, users see duplicates or gaps). Cursors anchor to actual data. For small result sets (< 10k), offset-limit is fine; cursor adds zero cost here, scales when it matters.

## Versioning

**API versioning via header (not URL):**
```
Accept: application/vnd.example.com; version=2
```

Avoids `/v1/`, `/v2/` URL paths (harder to deprecate, pollutes routing). Version in Accept header + fallback to default for unspecified clients.

**Document versioning (snapshots):**
Each `PUT` creates a new version server-side. No explicit version body param.
```json
{
  "id": "doc-123",
  "version": 5,
  "created_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
  "data": {...}
}
```

Retrieve version: `GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/5`

Comparison: `GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/4/diff?to=5` → returns diff (not full snapshot diff for size).

**Why:** Immutable versions prevent accidental overwrites ("you changed what I was reading"). Snapshots are simpler than diff-based versioning for most cases; diff endpoint added when storage dominates cost. API versioning separate from document versioning — API changes infrequently, document schema evolves per-org.

## Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "TENANT_MISMATCH",
    "message": "Org ID in token does not match request path.",
    "details": {
      "token_org": "org-456",
      "request_org": "org-123"
    },
    "request_id": "req-789abc"
  }
}
```

**Standard codes:**
- `UNAUTHENTICATED` (401) → missing/invalid token
- `FORBIDDEN` (403) → auth OK, but no permission for this resource
- `TENANT_MISMATCH` (403) → token org ≠ path org
- `NOT_FOUND` (404) → resource doesn't exist (never leak whether resource exists across tenants)
- `CONFLICT` (409) → stale version (document was updated by someone else)
- `RATE_LIMIT` (429) → too many requests
- `INVALID_REQUEST` (400) → bad input (malformed JSON, missing fields)

**Why:** `code` is machine-parseable; clients route on it. `request_id` ties client error to server logs. `details` only included when actionable (don't leak internal state). Single error format, never multiple errors in one response (clients handle one problem at a time).

## Summary of tradeoffs

| Decision | Tradeoff |
|----------|----------|
| **Tenant in path** | Verbose URLs but bulletproof isolation, fails open on routing bugs. Alternative: tenant claim in token only → smaller URLs, bug in auth layer = breach. |
| **Cursors** | Stateless (server doesn't track position), scales. Can't jump to page 500. Use offset if you need that. |
| **Snapshots over diffs** | Storage overhead early, retrieval fast, diffs on-demand. Alternative: diffs only → saves space, diff computation always needed. |
| **Header versioning** | Cleaner URLs, requires clients read spec. URL versioning is noisier but discoverable. Pick based on your audience. |
| **Share tokens for external access** | External users never see internal auth. Alternative: add them as members → simpler but leaks org structure. |
~~~

# Migrating Monolith to Services: Incremental Approach

## Ordering: Extract from Outside-In

**Start at the edges**, not the core. This minimizes blast radius and lets you ship early.

1. **Stateless I/O services first** — logging, file uploads, external API integrations. These have clear boundaries and no internal dependencies.
2. **Domain services with weak coupling** — features that are mostly independent. Example: user preferences, notifications, analytics.
3. **High-traffic read-heavy services** — services where you can decouple reads without initially splitting writes. Keep reads local via caching/replication.
4. **Core domain services last** — the parts that touch everything. By then you have patterns proven.

The rule: **extract when separation is obvious, not when it's theoretically possible**. Premature extraction creates coordination overhead that makes everything slower.

## Data Ownership: Strangler + Dual Writes

Don't split the database in one move. Instead:

**Phase 1: Strangler at the boundary**
- New service owns its data schema (separate DB) but reads/writes initially go through the monolith
- Monolith acts as the source of truth; service builds its view asynchronously
- Example: user service writes to both monolith's `users` table and its own schema via async job

**Phase 2: Flip the write direction**
- Redirect writes to the new service's database
- Monolith now reads from the service (via API or replication)
- This creates a brief "dual-write" window where both are authoritative — keep it short (days, not weeks)

**Phase 3: Remove monolith's ownership**
- Delete redundant columns from monolith's schema
- Monolith becomes read-only for that entity, then client calls service directly
- Keep the dual-read layer as long as needed for safety

**Data sync safety**: use change data capture (CDC) or event logs, not dual writes in application code. If the app crashes between writes, you're inconsistent. Let the database or message queue guarantee ordering.

## Keeping It Shippable: Continuous Deployability

**Problem**: if you're in a half-migrated state for months, the monolith keeps changing and new services get stale. Solutions:

**1. Feature flags over branches**
- Migrate one feature at a time behind a flag
- Monolith calls the new service if the flag is on; otherwise uses old logic
- Revert by flipping a flag, not rolling back commits
- This lets you deploy to production, test with real traffic, and roll back instantly

**2. Shared integration tests**
- Tests run against monolith's API and new service's API, verifying they return the same result
- Catch data drift immediately
- These become your safety net as services proliferate

**3. Leave an anti-corruption layer**
- Keep a thin translation layer in the monolith (or at the boundary) that adapts old and new schemas
- Services speak their own language; translation happens in one place
- Makes it safe for the monolith and service to evolve independently

**4. Prioritize services that reduce monolith size**
- Extract the most frequently changed, least stable parts first (not the most important)
- Stability improves as you shrink the blast radius
- Freed engineers now have fewer things to hold in their head

## Practical Workflow

```
Week 1: Extract logging service (stateless)
 ├─ Deploy service
 ├─ Monolith calls it via feature flag
 └─ Ship to prod with flag off, gradually enable

Week 3: Feature flag on 100%, metrics green, remove flag

Week 4: Extract auth tokens (high-value, relatively isolated)
 ├─ Dual-write to new DB
 ├─ Build tests that verify both return same data
 └─ Flip reads to new service

Month 2: Extract user preferences (medium complexity)
 ├─ Repeat pattern
 └─ Now you have three services, monolith is 15% smaller

Month 6: Core features still in monolith, but team is unblocked
 ├─ Dependencies are clear
 └─ Next services are obvious
```

## The Hard Part: Organizational

- **Assign clear ownership** — one team per service. Prevents "whose bug is this?" paralysis.
- **Build observability first** — if you can't see latency/errors across service boundaries, you can't debug anything.
- **Define a synchronous protocol** — REST or gRPC, pick one. Don't let services invent their own.
- **Accept temporary slowness** — a monolith function call is nanoseconds; a service call is milliseconds. You will regress latency initially. That's the cost of decoupling.

The goal is **deployable continuously**, not fast. Speed comes later, after you stop coordinating deployments.
~~~

### caveman: 431 answer tokens, 582 billed, 23 lines

~~~
**Strangler pattern**: Wrap monolith endpoints with adapters. New requests → new service. Old requests → monolith. Routes flip gradually. No big bang.

**Order**:
1. **Seams first** — extract low-coupling, single-responsibility modules. Auth, logging, config: easy, used everywhere, payoff immediate.
2. **Leaf services next** — no dependencies on other services. Payment processing, email, notifications. Safe to fail.
3. **High-value flows last** — core business logic tied to data. Do these once pattern proven.
Avoid middle layers. Harder to untangle.

**Data ownership**:
- **Synchronous boundary** — monolith owns DB. New service queries via API during migration. Eventual consistency later.
- **Event log between** — monolith publishes events (user created, order placed). Services subscribe, build own read models. Monolith stays source of truth until services mature.
- **Dual write period** — service writes → monolith DB *and* own DB. Flip reads to new DB once caught up. Delete dual-write once confident.
Never split DB schema until services own their data fully. Cross-service transactions stay in monolith logic during transition.

**Shippable throughout**:
- Feature flags. Route % of traffic to new service. Canary → ramp → full cutover.
- Circuit breaker on service calls. Monolith falls back if service slow/down. 
- Versioned APIs. Old clients talk to shims until migrated.
- Kill switch. Flip route back instantly if broken.

**Validation**: Deploy, measure, ship incrementally. If feature works in new service + falls back to monolith, deploy. Next week, expand traffic. Prevents batch risk.

What phase you in — still monolith-only, started extraction, or running services alongside?
~~~

### ponytail: 936 answer tokens, 1428 billed, 47 lines

~~~
# Monolith-to-Services Without Big Bang

## Ordering: Start at the edges

Migrate boundary-by-boundary, not randomly. The order matters:

1. **Leaf services first** — Things that nothing else depends on (email sender, PDF generator, analytics). They're forgiving: if they're slightly out of sync or slow, the core doesn't break.
2. **Low-coupling services next** — Features that touch few tables, have clear inputs/outputs, don't participate in multi-service transactions.
3. **Core domain last** — The tangled heart of your app. You need experience and infrastructure working before you touch it.

**Don't freeze the monolith.** Keep shipping to it while you extract. Monolith stagnation kills migrations.

## Data ownership: The real bottleneck

You can't extract a service without clear data boundaries. This is harder than the code:

1. **Each service owns its schema** — The service writes its own tables, reads its own data. If service B needs data from service A's tables, service A exposes an API, not a shared DB connection.

2. **Establish ownership *before* the cutover** — While still monolithic, refactor so the code paths for "email sending" live in one place and touch one schema. This prepares the boundary. When you extract to a service, it's just moving a module, not surgery.

3. **Duplicate data when needed** — If two services need the same data, they each own a copy. Service A writes to its table; a synchronizer (event handler, batch job, CDC pipeline) keeps service B's copy fresh. Accept eventual consistency, or don't extract yet.

4. **Avoid cross-service foreign keys** — If service A's orders table needs to ref service B's customer ID, don't use a FK. Store the ID as plain data; validate it at the boundary. FKs across services are a hidden coupling bomb.

## Keeping it shippable

1. **Deploy the new service alongside old code** — Write both. The monolith still handles the request, but calls the new service internally (or via a seam/adapter). Feature-flag the routing. If the new service breaks, flip the flag and keep running.

2. **Dual-write during transition** — For a time, the monolith writes to both old and new storage. When a user hits the old code path, it syncs both DBs. This gives you a window to verify the new service works before you delete the old path. Dual-write is temporary, not forever.

3. **Route gradually** — Don't flip 100% of traffic at once. Route 5% → 25% → 50% → 100%. Or by account: trusted customers first, then wider.

4. **Keep the monolith API stable** — The monolith's callers (web frontend, mobile app) don't see the service boundary. They call the same endpoint. The monolith orchestrates: "this request goes to the new service now, but I pretend it came from me." Hides the extraction.

5. **Revert must be fast** — If the new service goes sideways at 3am, you need to flip a flag and go back to the old code path in seconds, not hours. This means the old code stays in the monolith, unused but present, for weeks or months after cutover.

## The cadence

**Week 1–2:** Extract code for one leaf service. Set up the new service, the dual-write, the feature flag.  
**Week 3:** 50% traffic on new service, 50% on old. Watch logs.  
**Week 4:** Flip to 100%. Keep old code in monolith for now.  
**Week 5:** Delete old code, remove dual-write. Ship.  
**Week 6+:** Repeat with next service.

This isn't clever. It's tedious. That's the point—tedium is safer than speed. You ship every week, so a mistake costs one week, not three months of blocked work.

The monolith gets smaller, the services grow, and nothing breaks in between.
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 454 answer tokens, 652 billed, 9 lines

~~~
**Ordering:** Start with services that touch few other modules and carry traffic that won't tank the system if the extraction fails. High-pain, low-coupling services go first—not the most popular one. Example: a reporting service that reads stale data is safer than authentication. Work backward from dependencies: extract things others need before extracting things that need others.

**Data ownership:** Dual writes → split reads → eventual deletion. Early on, the monolith keeps writing; the new service writes in parallel to its own store (and reads only from itself). Once that's stable, flip reads in the monolith to the service. Eventually retire the monolith's copy. This avoids the "where does the truth live?" knot and keeps both sides in sync while trusting neither fully. Use versioned APIs and feature flags to route traffic gradually—don't flip all reads at once.

**Shippability:** Treat the monolith and services as a coupled system until they're not. Monolith changes to call the service via an internal RPC layer, not bare HTTP. The service is behind that adapter; swapping implementations doesn't break the monolith. Lock the monolith's version when shipping the service so deploys don't race each other. Ship the service as a sidecar or container the monolith's tests already know about—not as an external system to wire later.

**The trap:** Starting with the "cleanest" architecture (perfect separation, async everything, event sourcing). That delays shipping. Start with the simplest plumbing—sync RPC, shared DB, coarse feature flags—and add sophistication only when the first extraction works end-to-end.

**Gotchas:** Transactions across service + monolith fail atomically. Use saga pattern or accept eventual consistency. Shared DB needs coordination (lock columns, sequence IDs)—it works but is a crutch; don't stay there. Canary new services on 5% of traffic, not 50%, while monolith handles 95% and falls back gracefully.
~~~

### chisle: 457 answer tokens, 709 billed, 9 lines

~~~
**Ordering:** Start with leaf services—high dependency cost, low dependents. Work toward the core. Example: payment service → inventory → orders → cart API. Each extracted service gets its own DB (data ownership boundary). Never extract the top layer first (UI, orchestration); that stays glued to whatever runs last.

**Data ownership:** The strangler fig pattern: new service gets its own DB copy, reads from monolith's tables via shared schema initially, then gradually owns writes. During migration, both live—monolith writes to its schema, service writes to its own, with event-based sync or dual-write in between (read from service, fall back to monolith). This avoids the "move schema, everything breaks" cliff. Once the service owns a table fully, monolith stops touching it. Shared auth/user tables stay longest; they're dependencies everywhere.

**Keeping it shippable:** Always ship both old code and new code running in prod. Use feature flags (percentage rollout, gradual canary) to route traffic, not deploy toggles. If the extracted service crashes, flag flips traffic back—no emergency rollback. Database: run migrations on the monolith's schema *before* the service expects them, so old and new code coexist through deployments. Schema changes: add column → backfill on monolith → new service reads it → old code ignores it (never delete/rename until service takes over).

**The hard part:** Transactions that span old and new code. Saga pattern (compensating transactions if one half fails) is the common fix, but it's eventual consistency—must accept that mid-transaction crashes leave orphaned data. For high-value txns, add a reconciliation job. Ordering matters: pick services where txn boundaries naturally align with your domain, not arbitrary splits.

**Momentum:** Ship one service fully (extracted, owning its data, handling its traffic) before starting the next. Proves the pattern works, unblocks parallel work. Each extracted service *reduces* monolith load → makes the next extraction faster.
~~~

    app=app,
    key_func=get_remote_address,
    default_limits=["200 per day", "50 per hour"],
    storage_uri="memory://"
)

DB_PATH = 'auth.db'

def init_db():
    conn = sqlite3.connect(DB_PATH)
    c = conn.cursor()
    
    c.execute('''
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
            id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
            email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
            password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
            created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
        )
    ''')
    
    c.execute('''
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS login_attempts (
            id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
            email TEXT NOT NULL,
            ip_address TEXT NOT NULL,
            timestamp TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
            success BOOLEAN NOT NULL
        )
    ''')
    
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()

def get_db():
    conn = sqlite3.connect(DB_PATH)
    conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    return conn

def record_login_attempt(email, ip_address, success):
    conn = get_db()
    c = conn.cursor()
    c.execute(
        'INSERT INTO login_attempts (email, ip_address, success) VALUES (?, ?, ?)',
        (email, ip_address, success)
    )
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()

def is_account_locked(email, ip_address, max_attempts=5, window_minutes=15):
    conn = get_db()
    c = conn.cursor()
    cutoff_time = datetime.now() - timedelta(minutes=window_minutes)
    
    c.execute(
        '''SELECT COUNT(*) as failed_count FROM login_attempts 
           WHERE email = ? AND ip_address = ? AND success = 0 
           AND timestamp > ?''',
        (email, ip_address, cutoff_time.isoformat())
    )
    
    result = c.fetchone()
    conn.close()
    
    return result['failed_count'] >= max_attempts

def require_login(f):
    @wraps(f)
    def decorated_function(*args, **kwargs):
        if 'user_id' not in session:
            return jsonify({'error': 'Unauthorized'}), 401
        return f(*args, **kwargs)
    return decorated_function

@app.route('/signup', methods=['POST'])
@limiter.limit("5 per hour")
def signup():
    data = request.get_json()
    
    if not data or not data.get('email') or not data.get('password'):
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    email = data['email'].strip().lower()
    password = data['password']
    
    if len(password) < 8:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Password must be at least 8 characters'}), 400
    
    if '@' not in email or '.' not in email:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid email format'}), 400
    
    password_hash = generate_password_hash(password, method='pbkdf2:sha256')
    
    conn = get_db()
    c = conn.cursor()
    
    try:
        c.execute(
            'INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash) VALUES (?, ?)',
            (email, password_hash)
        )
        conn.commit()
        user_id = c.lastrowid
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError:
        conn.close()
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email already registered'}), 409
    finally:
        conn.close()
    
    return jsonify({'message': 'Signup successful', 'user_id': user_id}), 201

@app.route('/login', methods=['POST'])
@limiter.limit("10 per hour")
def login():
    data = request.get_json()
    
    if not data or not data.get('email') or not data.get('password'):
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    email = data['email'].strip().lower()
    ip_address = get_remote_address()
    
    if is_account_locked(email, ip_address):
        return jsonify({'error': 'Too many failed attempts. Try again later.'}), 429
    
    conn = get_db()
    c = conn.cursor()
    c.execute('SELECT id, password_hash FROM users WHERE email = ?', (email,))
    user = c.fetchone()
    conn.close()
    
    if user and check_password_hash(user['password_hash'], data['password']):
        record_login_attempt(email, ip_address, True)
        session.permanent = True
        session['user_id'] = user['id']
        session['email'] = email
        return jsonify({'message': 'Login successful', 'user_id': user['id']}), 200
    else:
        record_login_attempt(email, ip_address, False)
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid email or password'}), 401

@app.route('/logout', methods=['POST'])
@require_login
def logout():
    session.clear()
    return jsonify({'message': 'Logged out successfully'}), 200

@app.route('/profile', methods=['GET'])
@require_login
def profile():
    return jsonify({
        'user_id': session['user_id'],
        'email': session['email']
    }), 200

@app.route('/change-password', methods=['POST'])
@require_login
def change_password():
    data = request.get_json()
    
    if not data or not data.get('old_password') or not data.get('new_password'):
        return jsonify({'error': 'Old and new passwords required'}), 400
    
    if len(data['new_password']) < 8:
        return jsonify({'error': 'New password must be at least 8 characters'}), 400
    
    conn = get_db()
    c = conn.cursor()
    c.execute('SELECT password_hash FROM users WHERE id = ?', (session['user_id'],))
    user = c.fetchone()
    
    if not user or not check_password_hash(user['password_hash'], data['old_password']):
        conn.close()
        return jsonify({'error': 'Old password is incorrect'}), 401
    
    new_hash = generate_password_hash(data['new_password'], method='pbkdf2:sha256')
    c.execute('UPDATE users SET password_hash = ? WHERE id = ?', 
              (new_hash, session['user_id']))
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()
    
    return jsonify({'message': 'Password changed successfully'}), 200

if __name__ == '__main__':
    init_db()
    app.run(debug=False, host='127.0.0.1', port=5000)

## Overview

I've created a complete authentication system with these features:

**Core Features:**
- **Signup** (`/signup`): Email validation, password strength (8+ chars), duplicate prevention
- **Login** (`/login`): Credential verification with rate limiting
- **Sessions**: Secure HTTP-only cookies with 24-hour expiry
- **Password Hashing**: PBKDF2-SHA256 via Werkzeug (industry standard)
- **Rate Limiting**: 
  - Signup: 5 per hour per IP
  - Login: 10 per hour per IP
  - Account lockout: 5 failed attempts per 15 minutes per email+IP
- **Protected Routes**: Profile, logout, and password change endpoints
- **Password Change**: Requires old password verification

**Security Features:**
- Passwords never logged or exposed
- Secure session cookies (HttpOnly, Secure flags)
- IP-based and email-based rate limiting
- Login attempt tracking with success/failure records
- Constant-time password comparison (prevents timing attacks)

**To run:**
```bash
pip install -r requirements.txt
python auth_app.py
```

Then test with:
```bash
python test_auth.py
```

The system uses SQLite for simplicity; for production, upgrade to PostgreSQL and add CSRF protection, email verification, and secure password reset flows.

<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/rateLimiter.js</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">const MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5;
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000; // 15 minutes
const ATTEMPT_TIMEOUT = 60 * 1000; // 1 minute after last attempt

class RateLimiter {
  constructor() {
    this.attempts = new Map();
  }

  isBlocked(email) {
    if (!this.attempts.has(email)) return false;

    const record = this.attempts.get(email);
    const now = Date.now();

    // Clear old attempts outside window
    if (now - record.firstAttempt > WINDOW_MS) {
      this.attempts.delete(email);
      return false;
    }

    // Block if max attempts reached and within timeout
    if (record.count >= MAX_ATTEMPTS && now - record.lastAttempt < ATTEMPT_TIMEOUT) {
      return true;
    }

    // Reset if attempts window expired
    if (now - record.lastAttempt > WINDOW_MS) {
      this.attempts.delete(email);
      return false;
    }

    return false;
  }

  recordAttempt(email) {
    const now = Date.now();
    
    if (!this.attempts.has(email)) {
      this.attempts.set(email, {
        count: 1,
        firstAttempt: now,
        lastAttempt: now
      });
    } else {
      const record = this.attempts.get(email);
      record.count++;
      record.lastAttempt = now;
      
      // Reset window if old
      if (now - record.firstAttempt > WINDOW_MS) {
        record.count = 1;
        record.firstAttempt = now;
      }
    }
  }

  reset(email) {
    this.attempts.delete(email);
  }
}

export const rateLimiter = new RateLimiter();

<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/auth.js</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">import bcrypt from 'bcrypt';
import jwt from 'jsonwebtoken';

const JWT_SECRET = 'dev-secret-change-in-production';
const JWT_EXPIRY = '24h';

export async function hashPassword(password) {
  return bcrypt.hash(password, 10);
}

export async function verifyPassword(password, hash) {
  return bcrypt.compare(password, hash);
}

export function issueSession(userId, email) {
  return jwt.sign(
    { userId, email },
    JWT_SECRET,
    { expiresIn: JWT_EXPIRY }
  );
}

export function verifySession(token) {
  try {
    return jwt.verify(token, JWT_SECRET);
  } catch (err) {
    return null;
  }
}

export function validateEmail(email) {
  const re = /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/;
  return re.test(email) && email.length <= 254;
}

export function validatePassword(password) {
  return password.length >= 8;
}
</parameter>

<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/routes.js</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">import express from 'express';
import { getDb } from './db.js';
import { 
  hashPassword, 
  verifyPassword, 
  issueSession, 
  verifySession,
  validateEmail,
  validatePassword
} from './auth.js';
import { rateLimiter } from './rateLimiter.js';

const router = express.Router();

// Signup
router.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  // Validate input
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }

  if (!validateEmail(email)) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Invalid email format' });
  }

  if (!validatePassword(password)) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Password must be at least 8 characters' });
  }

  try {
    const db = getDb();
    const passwordHash = await hashPassword(password);

    const result = await db.run(
      'INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash) VALUES (?, ?)',
      [email, passwordHash]
    );

    const token = issueSession(result.lastID, email);

    res.status(201).json({
      userId: result.lastID,
      email,
      token
    });
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.message.includes('UNIQUE constraint failed')) {
      return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already registered' });
    }
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Signup failed' });
  }
});

// Login
router.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }

  // Check rate limit
  if (rateLimiter.isBlocked(email)) {
    return res.status(429).json({ 
      error: 'Too many failed attempts. Try again in 1 minute.' 
    });
  }

  try {
    const db = getDb();
    const user = await db.get(
      'SELECT id, email, password_hash FROM users WHERE email = ?',
      [email]
    );

    if (!user) {
      rateLimiter.recordAttempt(email);
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
    }

    const passwordMatch = await verifyPassword(password, user.password_hash);

    if (!passwordMatch) {
      rateLimiter.recordAttempt(email);
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
    }

    // Clear rate limiter on success
    rateLimiter.reset(email);

    const token = issueSession(user.id, user.email);

    res.json({
      userId: user.id,
      email: user.email,
      token
    });
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Login failed' });
  }
});

// Verify token (protected route example)
router.get('/me', (req, res) => {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];

  if (!token) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'No token provided' });
  }

  const payload = verifySession(token);

  if (!payload) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid or expired token' });
  }

  res.json({ userId: payload.userId, email: payload.email });
});

export default router;

```python
# app.py
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
from werkzeug.security import generate_password_hash, check_password_hash
import secrets
import time
from datetime import datetime, timedelta

app = Flask(__name__)
app.config['SECRET_KEY'] = secrets.token_hex(32)

# In-memory storage (replace with real DB)
users = {}  # {email: {password_hash, created_at}}
sessions = {}  # {token: {email, created_at}}
rate_limit = {}  # {email: {attempts: int, reset_at: timestamp}}

RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS = 5
RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW = 900  # 15 minutes
SESSION_EXPIRY = 86400  # 24 hours

def get_client_ip():
    return request.remote_addr

def check_rate_limit(email):
    now = time.time()
    if email not in rate_limit:
        rate_limit[email] = {'attempts': 0, 'reset_at': now + RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW}
    
    record = rate_limit[email]
    if now > record['reset_at']:
        record['attempts'] = 0
        record['reset_at'] = now + RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW
    
    if record['attempts'] >= RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS:
        return False
    
    return True

def increment_rate_limit(email):
    if email not in rate_limit:
        rate_limit[email] = {'attempts': 0, 'reset_at': time.time() + RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW}
    rate_limit[email]['attempts'] += 1

def cleanup_sessions():
    now = time.time()
    expired = [token for token, data in sessions.items() 
               if now - data['created_at'] > SESSION_EXPIRY]
    for token in expired:
        del sessions[token]

@app.route('/signup', methods=['POST'])
def signup():
    data = request.get_json()
    email = data.get('email', '').strip()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    if not email or not password:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    if email in users:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email already registered'}), 409
    
    if len(password) < 8:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Password must be at least 8 characters'}), 400
    
    users[email] = {
        'password_hash': generate_password_hash(password),
        'created_at': datetime.utcnow().isoformat()
    }
    
    return jsonify({'message': 'Signup successful'}), 201

@app.route('/login', methods=['POST'])
def login():
    cleanup_sessions()
    
    data = request.get_json()
    email = data.get('email', '').strip()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    if not email or not password:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    if not check_rate_limit(email):
        return jsonify({'error': 'Too many failed attempts. Try again later'}), 429
    
    if email not in users:
        increment_rate_limit(email)
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid credentials'}), 401
    
    if not check_password_hash(users[email]['password_hash'], password):
        increment_rate_limit(email)
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid credentials'}), 401
    
    # Reset rate limit on successful login
    if email in rate_limit:
        del rate_limit[email]
    
    # Create session
    token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)
    sessions[token] = {'email': email, 'created_at': time.time()}
    
    return jsonify({'token': token, 'email': email}), 200

@app.route('/protected', methods=['GET'])
def protected():
    cleanup_sessions()
    
    token = request.headers.get('Authorization', '').replace('Bearer ', '')
    
    if not token or token not in sessions:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Unauthorized'}), 401
    
    return jsonify({'message': 'Success', 'email': sessions[token]['email']}), 200

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=False)
```

**Usage:**

I'll build a Node.js/Express auth system with bcrypt hashing, JWT sessions, and login rate limiting.

**server.js** — Express API with signup/login/protected routes:

```javascript
const express = require('express');
const bcrypt = require('bcrypt');
const jwt = require('jsonwebtoken');
const app = express();

const SECRET_KEY = process.env.SECRET_KEY || 'your-secret-key-change-in-prod';
const users = new Map();
const rateLimitStore = new Map();

app.use(express.json());

const checkRateLimit = (identifier, maxAttempts = 5, windowMs = 15 * 60 * 1000) => {
  const now = Date.now();
  if (!rateLimitStore.has(identifier)) {
    rateLimitStore.set(identifier, { attempts: 0, resetTime: now + windowMs });
  }
  const record = rateLimitStore.get(identifier);
  if (now > record.resetTime) {
    record.attempts = 0;
    record.resetTime = now + windowMs;
  }
  return record.attempts < maxAttempts;
};

const recordFailedAttempt = (identifier) => {
  const now = Date.now();
  if (!rateLimitStore.has(identifier)) {
    rateLimitStore.set(identifier, { attempts: 0, resetTime: now + 15 * 60 * 1000 });
  }
  const record = rateLimitStore.get(identifier);
  if (now > record.resetTime) {
    record.attempts = 0;
    record.resetTime = now + 15 * 60 * 1000;
  }
  record.attempts++;
};

const clearRateLimit = (identifier) => {
  rateLimitStore.delete(identifier);
};

app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }
  if (users.has(email)) {
    return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already registered' });
  }
  try {
    const hashed = await bcrypt.hash(password, 10);
    users.set(email, { email, password: hashed });
    res.status(201).json({ message: 'User registered' });
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Signup failed' });
  }
});

app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }
  if (!checkRateLimit(email)) {
    return res.status(429).json({ error: 'Too many failed attempts. Try again in 15 minutes.' });
  }
  const user = users.get(email);
  if (!user) {
    recordFailedAttempt(email);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid email or password' });
  }
  try {
    const match = await bcrypt.compare(password, user.password);
    if (!match) {
      recordFailedAttempt(email);
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid email or password' });
    }
    clearRateLimit(email);
    const token = jwt.sign({ email: user.email }, SECRET_KEY, { expiresIn: '7d' });
    res.json({ token });
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Login failed' });
  }
});

app.get('/profile', (req, res) => {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: 'No token' });
  try {
    const decoded = jwt.verify(token, SECRET_KEY);
    res.json({ user: decoded });
  } catch {
    res.status(403).json({ error: 'Invalid token' });
  }
});

app.listen(3000, () => console.log('Running on :3000'));
```

**package.json**:

```json
{
  "name": "auth-server",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "main": "server.js",
  "scripts": { "start": "node server.js" },
  "dependencies": {
    "express": "^4.18.2",
    "bcrypt": "^5.1.1",
    "jsonwebtoken": "^9.1.2"
  }
}
```

**How it works:**

- `POST /signup`: Hash password with bcrypt (salt 10), store user
- `POST /login`: Compare input against hash; rate limit to 5 failed attempts per 15-min window per email; issue 7-day JWT on success
- `GET /profile`: Validate JWT, return decoded payload (protected route example)
- Successful login clears the rate-limit counter

Install: `npm install` → `npm start` → test with curl or Postman.

For production: replace in-memory user Map with a database, use a Redis-backed session store for rate limiting, and move `SECRET_KEY` to env vars.

<parameter name="contents">const express = require('express');
const sqlite3 = require('sqlite3').verbose();
const bcrypt = require('bcrypt');
const { v4: uuid } = require('uuid');

const app = express();
app.use(express.json());

// Database setup
const db = new sqlite3.Database(':memory:');
db.run(`
  CREATE TABLE users (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
    password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at INTEGER
  )
`);

db.run(`
  CREATE TABLE sessions (
    token TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    user_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at INTEGER,
    FOREIGN KEY (user_id) REFERENCES users(id)
  )
`);

// Rate limiting: track failed login attempts per email
const failedAttempts = new Map();
const RATE_LIMIT = { maxAttempts: 5, windowMs: 15 * 60 * 1000 }; // 5 attempts per 15 min

function checkRateLimit(email) {
  if (!failedAttempts.has(email)) {
    failedAttempts.set(email, []);
  }
  
  const attempts = failedAttempts.get(email);
  const now = Date.now();
  const recent = attempts.filter(t => now - t < RATE_LIMIT.windowMs);
  
  if (recent.length >= RATE_LIMIT.maxAttempts) {
    return { allowed: false, retryAfter: Math.ceil((recent[0] + RATE_LIMIT.windowMs - now) / 1000) };
  }
  
  failedAttempts.set(email, recent);
  return { allowed: true };
}

function recordFailedAttempt(email) {
  const attempts = failedAttempts.get(email) || [];
  attempts.push(Date.now());
  failedAttempts.set(email, attempts);
}

function clearFailedAttempts(email) {
  failedAttempts.delete(email);
}

// Routes
app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }

  if (password.length < 8) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Password must be at least 8 characters' });
  }

  try {
    const hash = await bcrypt.hash(password, 10);
    const userId = uuid();
    
    db.run(
      'INSERT INTO users (id, email, password_hash, created_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?)',
      [userId, email, hash, Date.now()],
      (err) => {
        if (err) {
          if (err.message.includes('UNIQUE')) {
            return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already registered' });
          }
          return res.status(500).json({ error: 'Database error' });
        }
        res.status(201).json({ userId, email });
      }
    );
  } catch (err) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Signup failed' });
  }
});

app.post('/login', (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }

  const rateLimit = checkRateLimit(email);
  if (!rateLimit.allowed) {
    return res.status(429).json({ 
      error: 'Too many failed attempts',
      retryAfter: rateLimit.retryAfter
    });
  }

  db.get(
    'SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = ?',
    [email],
    async (err, user) => {
      if (err || !user) {
        recordFailedAttempt(email);
        return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
      }

      try {
        const validPassword = await bcrypt.compare(password, user.password_hash);
        
        if (!validPassword) {
          recordFailedAttempt(email);
          return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
        }

        clearFailedAttempts(email);
        const sessionToken = uuid();
        
        db.run(
          'INSERT INTO sessions (token, user_id, created_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?)',
          [sessionToken, user.id, Date.now()],
          (err) => {
            if (err) return res.status(500).json({ error: 'Session creation failed' });
            res.json({ sessionToken, userId: user.id, email: user.email });
          }
        );
      } catch (err) {
        recordFailedAttempt(email);
        res.status(500).json({ error: 'Login failed' });
      }
    }
  );
});

app.post('/logout', (req, res) => {
  const { sessionToken } = req.body;
  
  if (!sessionToken) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Session token required' });
  }

  db.run(
    'DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token = ?',
    [sessionToken],
    (err) => {
      if (err) return res.status(500).json({ error: 'Logout failed' });
      res.json({ success: true });
    }
  );
});

app.get('/me', (req, res) => {
  const sessionToken = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];
  
  if (!sessionToken) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Missing session token' });
  }

  db.get(
    'SELECT u.* FROM users u JOIN sessions s ON u.id = s.user_id WHERE s.token = ?',
    [sessionToken],
    (err, user) => {
      if (err || !user) {
        return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid session' });
      }
      res.json({ id: user.id, email: user.email, createdAt: user.created_at });
    }
  );
});

const PORT = process.env.PORT || 3000;
app.listen(PORT, () => {
  console.log(`Auth server running on port ${PORT}`);
});

**Setup & run:**

```bash
npm start
```

**API endpoints:**

**POST /signup** — Register user

```json
{"email": "user@example.com", "password": "securepass123"}
```

**POST /login** — Authenticate and get session
```json

```
Returns: `{sessionToken, userId, email}`

**POST /logout** — Invalidate session
```json
{"sessionToken": "..."}
```

> Write a script that reads a large CSV of transactions, validates each row, aggregates totals per customer, and writes a report, handling malformed rows without dying. Show the code.

I'll create a Python script that robustly handles CSV processing with validation and error handling.
<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<path>/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/transaction_processor.py</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">#!/usr/bin/env python3
import csv
import sys
from pathlib import Path
from collections import defaultdict
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation
from datetime import datetime

def validate_row(row, row_num, headers):
    """Validate a transaction row. Returns (is_valid, customer_id, amount, error_msg)"""
    errors = []
    
    # Check required fields exist
    if 'customer_id' not in row or not row['customer_id']:
        errors.append("missing or empty customer_id")
    
    if 'amount' not in row or not row['amount']:
        errors.append("missing or empty amount")
    
    if errors:
        return False, None, None, "; ".join(errors)
    
    customer_id = row['customer_id'].strip()
    amount_str = row['amount'].strip()
    
    # Validate customer_id format (non-empty string)
    if not customer_id or len(customer_id) > 100:
        return False, None, None, "customer_id invalid length"
    
    # Validate amount is a valid decimal
    try:
        amount = Decimal(amount_str)
        if amount < 0:
            return False, None, None, "amount must be non-negative"
    except InvalidOperation:
        return False, None, None, f"invalid amount: {amount_str}"
    
    return True, customer_id, amount, None


def process_transactions(csv_path, output_path):
    """Process CSV transactions, aggregate by customer, and write report."""
    
    totals = defaultdict(Decimal)
    transaction_count = defaultdict(int)
    errors = []
    valid_count = 0
    
    # Read CSV
    try:
        with open(csv_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as infile:
            reader = csv.DictReader(infile)
            
            if not reader.fieldnames:
                print("Error: CSV has no headers", file=sys.stderr)
                return False
            
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):  # Start at 2 (line 1 is header)
                is_valid, customer_id, amount, error_msg = validate_row(row, row_num, reader.fieldnames)
                
                if is_valid:
                    totals[customer_id] += amount
                    transaction_count[customer_id] += 1
                    valid_count += 1
                else:
                    errors.append(f"Row {row_num}: {error_msg}")
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: File not found: {csv_path}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error reading CSV: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    # Write report
    try:
        with open(output_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as outfile:
            outfile.write("Transaction Report\n")
            outfile.write(f"Generated: {datetime.now().isoformat()}\n")
            outfile.write(f"Valid transactions: {valid_count}\n")
            outfile.write(f"Malformed rows: {len(errors)}\n")
            outfile.write("\n" + "="*60 + "\n\n")
            
            outfile.write("Customer Totals:\n")
            outfile.write(f"{'Customer ID':<30} {'Count':<10} {'Total':>15}\n")
            outfile.write("-"*60 + "\n")
            
            for customer_id in sorted(totals.keys()):
                total = totals[customer_id]
                count = transaction_count[customer_id]
                outfile.write(f"{customer_id:<30} {count:<10} ${total:>14.2f}\n")
            
            if errors:
                outfile.write("\n" + "="*60 + "\n\n")
                outfile.write("Malformed Rows (first 100):\n")
                for error in errors[:100]:
                    outfile.write(f"  {error}\n")
                if len(errors) > 100:
                    outfile.write(f"  ... and {len(errors) - 100} more errors\n")
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error writing report: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    # Print summary to stdout
    print(f"✓ Processed {valid_count} valid transactions from {len(totals)} customers")
    if errors:
        print(f"⚠ Skipped {len(errors)} malformed rows (see report for details)")
    print(f"✓ Report written to {output_path}")
    
    return True


if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        print("Usage: python transaction_processor.py <input.csv> <output.txt>")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    csv_path = sys.argv[1]
    output_path = sys.argv[2]
    
    success = process_transactions(csv_path, output_path)
    sys.exit(0 if success else 1)

### caveman: 1175 answer tokens, 1335 billed, 125 lines

Building CSV processor script. Reads transactions, validates, aggregates by customer, logs errors.

```python
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path

def process_transactions(csv_path, output_path):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate totals per customer, write report."""
    
    customers = defaultdict(lambda: {"total": 0, "count": 0})
    errors = []
    processed = 0
    
    try:
        with open(csv_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as infile:
            reader = csv.DictReader(infile)
            
            # Validate header
            if not reader.fieldnames or 'customer_id' not in reader.fieldnames or 'amount' not in reader.fieldnames:
                print("ERROR: CSV must have 'customer_id' and 'amount' columns")
                sys.exit(1)
            
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):  # start=2 (header is 1)
                try:
                    # Extract fields
                    customer_id = row.get('customer_id', '').strip()
                    amount_str = row.get('amount', '').strip()
                    
                    # Validate customer_id
                    if not customer_id:
                        raise ValueError("customer_id missing or empty")
                    
                    # Validate amount
                    if not amount_str:
                        raise ValueError("amount missing or empty")
                    
                    try:
                        amount = float(amount_str)
                    except ValueError:
                        raise ValueError(f"amount '{amount_str}' is not a valid number")
                    
                    if amount < 0:
                        raise ValueError(f"amount {amount} cannot be negative")
                    
                    # Aggregate
                    customers[customer_id]["total"] += amount
                    customers[customer_id]["count"] += 1
                    processed += 1
                    
                except (ValueError, KeyError) as e:
                    errors.append(f"Row {row_num}: {str(e)} | {row}")
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"ERROR: File not found: {csv_path}")
        sys.exit(1)
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"ERROR: Failed to read CSV: {e}")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    # Write report
    try:
        with open(output_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8', newline='') as outfile:
            writer = csv.writer(outfile)
            writer.writerow(['customer_id', 'transaction_count', 'total_amount'])
            
            for customer_id in sorted(customers.keys()):
                data = customers[customer_id]
                writer.writerow([customer_id, data['count'], f"{data['total']:.2f}"])
    
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"ERROR: Failed to write report: {e}")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    # Print summary
    print(f"Processed: {processed} rows")
    print(f"Customers: {len(customers)}")
    print(f"Errors: {len(errors)}")
    print(f"Report written: {output_path}")
    
    if errors:
        error_log = output_path.replace('.csv', '_errors.log')
        with open(error_log, 'w') as f:
            f.write('\n'.join(errors))
        print(f"Error log: {error_log}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        print(f"Usage: {sys.argv[0]} <input_csv> <output_csv>")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    process_transactions(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2])
```

**Usage:**
```bash
python process_transactions.py transactions.csv report.csv
```

**Script handles:**
- Missing/empty fields
- Non-numeric amounts
- Negative amounts
- Malformed rows (logs, continues)
- Large files (streams, no full load)
- Encoding issues
- File not found

**Output:**
- `report.csv`: customer_id, transaction_count, total_amount (sorted)
- `report_errors.log`: malformed rows with row number + reason

```python
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path

def process_transactions(csv_path, report_path):
    totals = defaultdict(float)
    skipped = 0
    processed = 0
    
    with open(csv_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='replace') as f:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        
        for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):  # start at 2 (after header)
            try:
                # Validate required fields exist and are non-empty
                customer = row.get('customer', '').strip()
                amount_str = row.get('amount', '').strip()
                
                if not customer or not amount_str:
                    print(f"Row {row_num}: missing customer or amount", file=sys.stderr)
                    skipped += 1
                    continue
                
                # Try to parse amount
                try:
                    amount = float(amount_str)
                except ValueError:
                    print(f"Row {row_num}: invalid amount '{amount_str}'", file=sys.stderr)
                    skipped += 1
                    continue
                
                totals[customer] += amount
                processed += 1
                
            except Exception as e:
                print(f"Row {row_num}: unexpected error: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
                skipped += 1
    
    # Write report
    with open(report_path, 'w') as f:
        f.write("Customer,Total\n")
        for customer in sorted(totals.keys()):
            f.write(f"{customer},{totals[customer]:.2f}\n")
    
    print(f"Processed: {processed}, Skipped: {skipped}", file=sys.stderr)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    csv_path = sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else 'transactions.csv'
    report_path = sys.argv[2] if len(sys.argv) > 2 else 'report.csv'
    process_transactions(csv_path, report_path)
```

**Usage:**
```bash
python script.py input.csv output.csv
```

**What it does:**
- Reads CSV with `DictReader` (handles headers automatically)
- Skips rows with missing/empty customer or amount
- Catches float parse errors, logs them, keeps going
- Aggregates totals in a dict
- Writes sorted report with 2 decimal places
- Logs skipped/processed counts to stderr

**Skipped:** fancy validation logic, logging config, progress bars. Add when you have specific validation rules or need to process millions of rows and want progress tracking.

```python
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path

def validate_row(row, row_num):
    """Return (is_valid, error_msg)"""
    required = {'customer_id', 'amount', 'date'}
    if not required.issubset(row.keys()):
        return False, f"Missing fields: {required - set(row.keys())}"
    
    if not row['customer_id'].strip():
        return False, "Empty customer_id"
    
    try:
        float(row['amount'])
    except ValueError:
        return False, f"Invalid amount: {row['amount']}"
    
    if not row['date'].strip():
        return False, "Empty date"
    
    return True, None

def process_transactions(input_csv, output_report):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate by customer, write report."""
    totals = defaultdict(float)
    skipped = []
    processed = 0
    
    try:
        with open(input_csv, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
            reader = csv.DictReader(f)
            if not reader.fieldnames:
                print("Error: Empty CSV or no header row", file=sys.stderr)
                return
            
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):  # start=2 (after header)
                valid, error = validate_row(row, row_num)
                
                if not valid:
                    skipped.append((row_num, error, dict(row)))
                    continue
                
                try:
                    customer = row['customer_id'].strip()
                    amount = float(row['amount'])
                    totals[customer] += amount
                    processed += 1
                except Exception as e:
                    skipped.append((row_num, f"Processing error: {e}", dict(row)))
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: File not found: {input_csv}", file=sys.stderr)
        return
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error reading CSV: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return
    
    # Write report
    try:
        with open(output_report, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:
            f.write("TRANSACTION REPORT\n")
            f.write(f"{'='*50}\n")
            f.write(f"Processed: {processed} rows\n")
            f.write(f"Skipped: {len(skipped)} rows\n\n")
            
            if skipped:
                f.write("SKIPPED ROWS:\n")
                for row_num, error, row in skipped:
                    f.write(f"  Row {row_num}: {error}\n")
                    if row:
                        f.write(f"    Data: {row}\n")
                f.write("\n")
            
            f.write("CUSTOMER TOTALS:\n")
            f.write(f"{'-'*50}\n")
            f.write(f"{'Customer ID':<20} {'Total':>15}\n")
            f.write(f"{'-'*50}\n")
            
            for customer in sorted(totals.keys()):
                f.write(f"{customer:<20} {totals[customer]:>15.2f}\n")
            
            f.write(f"{'-'*50}\n")
            grand_total = sum(totals.values())
            f.write(f"{'GRAND TOTAL':<20} {grand_total:>15.2f}\n")
        
        print(f"Report written to {output_report}")
        print(f"Processed: {processed}, Skipped: {len(skipped)}")
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error writing report: {e}", file=sys.stderr)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
        print(f"Usage: {sys.argv[0]} <input.csv> <output_report.txt>")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    process_transactions(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2])
```

**Usage:**
```bash
python transactions.py transactions.csv report.txt
```

**What it does:**
- Streams rows (doesn't load full CSV in memory)
- Validates: required fields, non-empty customer_id, numeric amount
- Logs malformed rows with error details → continues processing
- Aggregates totals per customer
- Writes report with summary + customer breakdown

Skipped rows show row number, error reason, and original data for debugging.
~~~

### chisle: 1099 answer tokens, 1368 billed, 103 lines

```python
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path

def validate_row(row, row_num):
    """Return (is_valid, customer_id, amount, error_msg)"""
    if len(row) < 2:
        return False, None, None, f"Row {row_num}: insufficient fields (got {len(row)})"
    
    customer_id, amount_str = row[0].strip(), row[1].strip()
    
    if not customer_id:
        return False, None, None, f"Row {row_num}: empty customer_id"
    
    try:
        amount = float(amount_str)
    except ValueError:
        return False, None, None, f"Row {row_num}: invalid amount '{amount_str}'"
    
    return True, customer_id, amount, None

def process_transactions(csv_path, output_path, error_log_path=None):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate by customer, write report."""
    totals = defaultdict(float)
    valid_count = 0
    error_count = 0
    errors = []
    
    try:
        with open(csv_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='replace') as infile:
            reader = csv.reader(infile)
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=1):
                is_valid, cid, amount, error_msg = validate_row(row, row_num)
                
                if is_valid:
                    totals[cid] += amount
                    valid_count += 1
                else:
                    errors.append(error_msg)
                    error_count += 1
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Fatal error reading {csv_path}: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    # Write report
    try:
        with open(output_path, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as outfile:
            writer = csv.writer(outfile)
            writer.writerow(['customer_id', 'total_amount'])
            for cid in sorted(totals.keys()):
                writer.writerow([cid, f"{totals[cid]:.2f}"])
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error writing report to {output_path}: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    # Write error log if requested
    if error_log_path and errors:
        try:
            with open(error_log_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as errfile:
                errfile.write('\n'.join(errors))
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error writing error log to {error_log_path}: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
    
    # Summary to stdout
    total_rows = valid_count + error_count
    print(f"Processed {total_rows} rows: {valid_count} valid, {error_count} errors")
    print(f"Report written to {output_path}")
    if error_count > 0:
        print(f"Error details: {error_log_path or 'none (use --errors flag)'}")
    
    return True

if __name__ == '__main__':
    import argparse
    
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description='Process transaction CSV')
    parser.add_argument('input', help='Input CSV file')
    parser.add_argument('-o', '--output', default='report.csv', help='Output report file')
    parser.add_argument('-e', '--errors', help='Error log file')
    args = parser.parse_args()
    
    success = process_transactions(args.input, args.output, args.errors)
    sys.exit(0 if success else 1)
```

**Usage:**
```bash
python script.py transactions.csv -o report.csv -e errors.log
```

**Features:**
- Reads CSV line-by-line (memory-efficient for large files)
- Validates: non-empty customer_id, parseable float amount
- Logs malformed rows with line numbers → continues
- Aggregates totals per customer
- Writes sorted report (customer_id, total_amount)
- Optional error log with details
- Handles encoding errors gracefully (`errors='replace'`)
- Exits 0 on success, 1 on fatal I/O errors

Malformed rows skip silently, counted in summary. Set `--errors` to see details.

## large-s1 / migration

> Write a database migration script that moves a users table from a single full_name column to first_name/last_name, backfills existing rows, and is safely re-runnable. Show the code.

### vanilla: 918 answer tokens, 1263 billed, 88 lines

~~~
I'll create a PostgreSQL migration script that safely handles the schema change and data migration in a re-runnable way.

```sql
-- migration: split_full_name_to_first_last.sql
-- Description: Move users.full_name to first_name/last_name columns
-- This migration is idempotent and safe to run multiple times

BEGIN;

-- Step 1: Add new columns if they don't exist
ALTER TABLE users
ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name VARCHAR(255),
ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name VARCHAR(255);

-- Step 2: Backfill data from full_name, only if first_name is still NULL
-- This handles re-runs by checking if data was already migrated
UPDATE users
SET
  first_name = TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 1)),
  last_name = TRIM(SUBSTRING(full_name FROM POSITION(' ' IN full_name) + 1))
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL;

-- Handle edge case: names with no space (use full_name as first_name)
UPDATE users
SET
  first_name = TRIM(full_name),
  last_name = ''
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL AND POSITION(' ' IN full_name) = 0;

-- Step 3: Add NOT NULL constraints only if data is populated
-- (This runs idempotently since the constraint may already exist)
ALTER TABLE users
ALTER COLUMN first_name SET NOT NULL;

-- Note: You may want to keep last_name nullable if some users have only one name
-- ALTER TABLE users
-- ALTER COLUMN last_name SET NOT NULL;

-- Step 4: Drop the old column (optional - comment out if you want to keep it for safety)
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;

COMMIT;
```

**Key features for safe re-runnability:**

1. **`IF NOT EXISTS`** — Adding columns twice is a no-op
2. **NULL checks in UPDATE** — Skips backfill if data already migrated
3. **Edge case handling** — Names without spaces get handled gracefully
4. **Constraint safety** — The final `ALTER COLUMN SET NOT NULL` is a no-op if already applied
5. **Transaction wrapper** — All-or-nothing atomicity
6. **Optional cleanup** — The column drop is commented out; uncomment once you've verified the migration

**For other databases:**

**SQLite** (adjust for single quotes and `SUBSTR`):
```sql
BEGIN;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN first_name TEXT;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN last_name TEXT;

UPDATE users
SET first_name = TRIM(SUBSTR(full_name, 1, INSTR(full_name, ' ') - 1)),
    last_name = TRIM(SUBSTR(full_name, INSTR(full_name, ' ') + 1))
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND INSTR(full_name, ' ') > 0;

UPDATE users
SET first_name = TRIM(full_name), last_name = ''
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL;

COMMIT;
```

**MySQL** (uses `SUBSTRING_INDEX`):
```sql
START TRANSACTION;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN first_name VARCHAR(255);
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN last_name VARCHAR(255);

UPDATE users
SET first_name = TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', 1)),
    last_name = TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', -1))
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL;

COMMIT;
```

Run the script with your database client, test on a staging environment first, then apply to production. The idempotency means you can safely re-run it if needed.

Assuming PostgreSQL. Build with idempotency checks so re-runs don't error or corrupt data.

```sql
BEGIN;

-- Step 1: Create new columns if not exist
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'first_name'
  ) THEN
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN first_name VARCHAR(255);
  END IF;
  
  IF NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'last_name'
  ) THEN
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN last_name VARCHAR(255);
  END IF;
END $$;

-- Step 2: Backfill data (only unpopulated rows, only if full_name exists)
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    UPDATE users
    SET 
      first_name = TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 1)),
      last_name = NULLIF(TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 2)), '')
    WHERE (first_name IS NULL OR first_name = '') 
      AND full_name IS NOT NULL 
      AND full_name != '';
  END IF;
END $$;

-- Step 3: Drop old column (after backfill verified safe)
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;

COMMIT;
```

**Why safe:**
- Checks column existence before ALTER—skips if already migrated
- Backfill only touches NULL/empty first_name—won't overwrite manual edits
- Checks full_name column exists before UPDATE—handles partial migrations
- Commented DROP—verify data first, then run separate transaction if confident
- Wrapped in transaction—all-or-nothing, no half-states

**To use:**
1. Run as-is. Safe to run multiple times.
2. Verify data looks right: `SELECT first_name, last_name FROM users LIMIT 5;`
3. Uncomment DROP line, run again to drop full_name.

For MySQL, swap `SPLIT_PART` → `SUBSTRING_INDEX`, `information_schema.columns` same but different syntax. Need specific DB?

```sql
  last_name = COALESCE(last_name,

      ELSE ''
    END)

  AND (first_name IS NULL OR last_name IS NULL);

COMMIT;
```

## large-s1 / postmortem

> Write an incident postmortem for a four-hour outage caused by a connection pool exhausted by a slow downstream dependency, including timeline, root cause and remediations.

### vanilla: 2183 answer tokens, 2372 billed, 191 lines

~~~

<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/incident_postmortem.md</parameter>
<parameter name="contents"># Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion Outage

**Incident ID:** INC-2026-1119-001  
**Date:** 2026-11-18  
**Duration:** 4 hours (14:30–18:30 UTC)  
**Severity:** SEV-1 (Critical)  
**Status:** Resolved

---

## Executive Summary

A four-hour outage affecting all API requests was caused by connection pool exhaustion in our backend service. The root cause was a downstream dependency (payment processor) experiencing elevated latency, which caused database connection acquisition timeouts. Connections were held for longer than expected, depleting the available pool and causing cascading failures. The incident was resolved by restarting the affected service and implementing connection timeouts.

---

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|-----------|-------|
| 14:30 | Monitoring alert triggered: API error rate spike (>10%) |
| 14:32 | On-call engineer acknowledged alert; began investigation |
| 14:35 | Confirmed widespread 503 errors; application logs showed connection pool exhaustion |
| 14:38 | Identified slow requests to payment processor API; response times increased from 200ms to 5–8s |
| 14:45 | Attempted to scale up API servers; no improvement observed (bottleneck was connection pool, not compute) |
| 14:52 | Escalated to database team; payment processor status page showed no reported outage |
| 15:10 | Decided to restart affected service to clear connection pool |
| 15:12 | Service restart completed; error rate returned to normal |
| 15:15 | Confirmed API health restored; monitoring normalized |
| 16:30 | Root cause analysis identified payment processor latency as trigger |
| 18:30 | Temporary fix deployed: added connection acquisition timeout to prevent indefinite blocking |

---

## Root Cause Analysis

### Primary Cause
The payment processor API experienced degraded performance, increasing response latency from ~200ms to 5–8 seconds. Our service did not have a timeout configured for database connection acquisition.

### Contributing Factors

1. **Missing connection acquisition timeout**  
   Database pool was configured with a maximum of 20 connections. During normal operation, most connections were returned quickly. When the downstream dependency slowed, threads waited indefinitely for available connections, eventually exhausting the pool.

2. **Slow downstream dependency without circuit breaker**  
   Requests to the payment processor were not protected by a circuit breaker. Even when responses took 5–8 seconds, we continued retrying and opening new connections, accelerating pool exhaustion.

3. **Insufficient alerting on connection pool metrics**  
   Monitoring tracked error rates but not connection pool utilization. Pool exhaustion was detected only after cascading to user-facing errors.

4. **No queue or backpressure mechanism**  
   Incoming requests were accepted without checking downstream dependency health, allowing the queue to grow and exacerbating connection pressure.

### Why It Happened

The payment processor experienced a temporary issue (later identified as a misconfigured load balancer on their infrastructure). Our service's lack of defensive programming—timeouts, circuit breakers, and pool monitoring—meant we amplified their problem into a complete outage for our users.

---

## Impact

- **User Blast Radius:** 100% of API endpoints affected
- **Duration:** 4 hours
- **Estimated User Impact:** ~50,000 failed requests
- **Business Impact:** Payment processing was blocked; estimated revenue impact $12K
- **SLA Breach:** Yes (99.9% SLA violated)

---

## Remediations

### Immediate Actions (Completed)
1. ✅ Restarted affected service to clear connection pool (15:12 UTC)
2. ✅ Deployed connection acquisition timeout (default 5 seconds) to prevent indefinite blocking

### Short-term Actions (In Progress)
1. 🔄 Add circuit breaker to payment processor client (deadline: 2026-11-21)
2. 🔄 Increase alerting sensitivity on connection pool utilization >75% (deadline: 2026-11-20)
3. 🔄 Implement request queue with backpressure mechanism (deadline: 2026-11-25)

### Long-term Actions (Planned)
1. 📋 Implement bulkhead pattern to isolate payment processor traffic from other services
2. 📋 Add adaptive timeout tuning based on downstream latency metrics
3. 📋 Establish SLO targets for downstream dependencies and add monitoring dashboard
4. 📋 Conduct chaos engineering exercises to test behavior under degraded downstream conditions
5. 📋 Document connection pool configuration best practices in runbooks

---

## Prevention Measures

### Code Changes
```
Database Configuration:
- connectionTimeout: 5000ms (from unlimited)
- maxPoolSize: 20 (unchanged, but now bounded by timeout)
- idleTimeout: 15000ms (to reclaim stale connections faster)

Payment Processor Client:
- Add circuit breaker (failure threshold: 50%, reset timeout: 60s)
- Add timeout: 2s (vs. current unlimited)
- Implement exponential backoff for retries
```

### Monitoring
- Alert on connection pool utilization >75%
- Alert on payment processor p99 latency >3s
- Dashboard showing connection pool trends and downstream dependency health

### Process
- Add "connection pool health" to daily metrics review
- Include downstream dependency resilience in architecture reviews
- Quarterly chaos engineering exercises targeting connection pool scenarios

---

## Lessons Learned

| What Went Well | What Could Be Better |
|----------------|---------------------|
| Fast detection via error rate monitoring | Should have monitored connection pool directly |
| Quick escalation and decision-making | Lack of circuit breaker amplified downstream issue |
| Effective communication with stakeholders | No runbook for connection pool exhaustion |
| Root cause identified within 2 hours | Timeout configuration missing despite known best practice |

---

## Action Items

| Priority | Owner | Action | Due Date |
|----------|-------|--------|----------|
| P0 | @backend-team | Implement circuit breaker for payment processor | 2026-11-21 |
| P0 | @platform-team | Add connection pool utilization alerting | 2026-11-20 |
| P1 | @backend-team | Implement request queue with backpressure | 2026-11-25 |
| P2 | @devops-team | Update runbooks with connection pool troubleshooting | 2026-11-22 |
| P2 | @backend-team | Chaos engineering exercise: downstream latency | 2026-12-05 |

---

## Appendices

### A. Configuration Before Incident
```yaml
database:
  maxPoolSize: 20
  connectionTimeout: unlimited
  idleTimeout: 30000ms
  
payment_processor:
  timeout: unlimited
  retries: 3
  backoff: linear
```

### B. Logs from 14:30–14:40 UTC
```
2026-11-18T14:35:22Z ERROR: Unable to acquire connection from pool (timeout=0ms)
2026-11-18T14:35:23Z WARN: Payment processor response time: 6250ms
2026-11-18T14:35:24Z ERROR: Thread pool exhausted; rejecting requests
```

### C. Payment Processor Status
Timeline from their postmortem:
- 14:25–14:50 UTC: Load balancer misconfiguration increased response latency to 5–8s
- 14:50 UTC: Issue identified and resolved; latency returned to normal
- Our service did not recover until restart due to pool exhaustion

---

**Reviewed By:** Engineering Manager  
**Date:** 2026-11-19  
**Follow-up Review:** 2026-12-19 (30-day check-in on action items)
</parameter>
</invoke>
