# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion Outage

**Incident ID:** INC-2026-1119-001  
**Date:** 2026-11-19  
**Duration:** 4 hours (14:30–18:30 UTC)  
**Severity:** SEV-1 (Complete Service Unavailability)  
**Author:** Incident Response Team

---

## Executive Summary

A downstream payment processing service degraded, responding to requests in 30–60+ seconds instead of <100ms. Connection pool threads waiting for responses accumulated until exhaustion, blocking all new requests. Service became unresponsive to customers for 4 hours until manual intervention and downstream recovery.

---

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|-----------|-------|
| 14:28 | Payment service latency spike begins (root cause unknown to external observers) |
| 14:30 | First alerts fire: connection pool utilization >90% |
| 14:35 | On-call pages. Team assumes connection leak; begins log review |
| 14:45 | Customer reports: API endpoints return 503 / timeout errors |
| 15:10 | Thread dump confirms all 50 pool threads blocked on payment service calls |
| 15:15 | On-call escalates to downstream team for support |
| 15:30 | Payment service team reports they are investigating their own latency |
| 16:45 | Payment service deploys fix for query timeout in their database layer |
| 17:00 | Payment service latency recovers to <100ms |
| 17:05 | Connection pool drains; threads unblock |
| 17:15 | Service stability confirmed; customer traffic normalized |
| 18:30 | On-call stands down |

---

## Root Cause

**Primary:** Payment processing service (external dependency) entered a degraded state, with p99 latency reaching 45–90+ seconds for transaction validation endpoints.

**Secondary:** No connection timeout or circuit breaker in place. HTTP client was configured with `connectionTimeout=5s` but no `readTimeout`. Requests hung indefinitely (or for minutes) waiting for responses.

**Tertiary:** Connection pool size (50 threads) was not scaled relative to expected downstream latency. No monitoring on per-thread request duration or downstream latency contributed to late detection.

---

## Impact

- **Availability:** 100% request failure rate (all API endpoints routed through payment validation)
- **Duration:** 4 hours, 2 minutes
- **Affected Users:** ~8,500 active users during incident window
- **Failed Transactions:** ~12,000 blocked transaction attempts (customers retried manually later)
- **Revenue Impact:** Estimated $45K in lost transaction fees + reputational cost

---

## Remediations

### Immediate (Completed by 17:30 UTC)
1. **Manual pool drain:** On-call reset connection pool via JMX to unblock threads.
2. **Customer comms:** Sent incident notification to affected users.

### Short-term (Complete within 48 hours)
1. **Add read timeout:** Configure HTTP client with `readTimeout=10s` to fail fast rather than hang.
   - Implementation: Update `PaymentClient` configuration in `config/clients.yml`
   - Verify in staging before production deploy
2. **Add circuit breaker:** Implement exponential backoff + circuit breaker for payment service.
   - Use resilience4j library; threshold: 5 consecutive timeouts → open circuit for 30s
   - Add metrics: open/half-open/closed state exported to monitoring
3. **Increase alerting sensitivity:** Alert on connection pool >70% (reduce threshold from 90%).

### Medium-term (Complete within 2 weeks)
1. **Pool size analysis:** Model expected downstream latency variance; right-size pool.
   - Current: 50 threads, ~100ms avg latency → adequate if no degradation
   - Proposed: 100 threads if downstream latency can reach 500ms+
   - Decision: validate with payment service SLA
2. **Dependency health dashboard:** Add real-time latency percentiles for each downstream service.
   - Alert on p99 >500ms or p95 >250ms to catch degradation before pool exhaustion
3. **Graceful degradation:** Implement fallback for payment validation (e.g., async validation with eventual consistency).

### Long-term (Complete within 1 quarter)
1. **Chaos testing:** Add integration tests simulating downstream latency (100ms→1s→10s ramps).
   - Validate circuit breaker activates and customer experience degrades gracefully (not crash).
2. **Bulkhead isolation:** Separate connection pool for payment service from general HTTP pool.
   - Prevents single-service degradation from blocking other APIs.
3. **SLO review:** Establish and document SLO for payment service dependency; define escalation path and fallback.

---

## Action Items

| ID | Task | Owner | Due | Priority |
|----|------|-------|-----|----------|
| A1 | Add HTTP read timeout to payment client | Backend | 2026-11-20 | P0 |
| A2 | Implement circuit breaker with resilience4j | Backend | 2026-11-21 | P0 |
| A3 | Lower pool exhaustion alert threshold to 70% | SRE | 2026-11-20 | P1 |
| A4 | Build dependency latency dashboard | SRE | 2026-11-26 | P2 |
| A5 | Chaos testing for downstream degradation | QA/SRE | 2026-12-03 | P2 |
| A6 | Connection pool right-sizing analysis | Backend + SRE | 2026-12-03 | P2 |

---

## Lessons Learned

**What went well:**
- Alert fired quickly (14:30, within 2 minutes of degradation start).
- Escalation to downstream team was swift; they had context and acted.

**What went poorly:**
- No timeout on HTTP reads; threads waited indefinitely.
- Pool size not validated against realistic downstream latency.
- Lack of per-dependency latency visibility delayed root cause identification.

**What we'll do differently:**
- All external service clients must have both connection and read timeouts.
- Connection pool sizing must account for worst-case downstream latency + percentile targets.
- Real-time dashboards for critical dependencies (latency, error rate, saturation).

---

**Postmortem conducted:** 2026-11-19 16:00 UTC  
**Reviewed by:** Engineering Manager, SRE Lead, Product

<parameter name="contents"># Incident Postmortem: API Service Outage

**Date:** 2026-11-19  
**Duration:** 4 hours (14:32 UTC – 18:32 UTC)  
**Severity:** SEV1 (all API endpoints unavailable)  
**Authors:** Incident Commander, On-Call Engineer  

---

## Executive Summary

The API service became unresponsive for 4 hours due to connection pool exhaustion. The root cause was a 100x increase in query latency to the user-service downstream dependency, which blocked all available database connections while waiting for responses that never completed. No data was lost; full service recovery occurred after restart.

---

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|-----------|-------|
| 14:32 | First customer-facing errors reported; alert fires for elevated API latency |
| 14:35 | On-call engineer acknowledges; API request latency visible at ~8s (normal: 100ms) |
| 14:42 | Database connection pool at 100% capacity; new requests start queuing |
| 14:50 | Concluded: downstream user-service experiencing issues; requests timeout after 30s |
| 15:10 | Attempted workaround: reduced pool size, increased timeouts; no recovery |
| 15:25 | Contacted user-service oncall; they report normal metrics but acknowledge receiving unusual request patterns |
| 15:40 | Restarted API service; connections drained, service recovered immediately |
| 16:00 | Confirmed user-service latency returned to baseline (12ms avg); API fully healthy |
| 18:32 | RTO met; all downstream systems verified stable |

---

## Root Cause

**Primary:** User-service latency degradation (baseline 12ms → 100ms+) caused by a database schema change deployed at 14:15 UTC. A new index was missing on the query path hit by our API calls, forcing a full table scan on a 2GB table.

**Contributing:** 
- API service connection pool configured for single-dependency assumption (max 20 connections, 30s timeout)
- No circuit breaker or timeout guard between API and user-service
- No alerting on connection pool saturation; latency alert alone insufficient for diagnosis
- No staged traffic or query plan validation in user-service deployment

**Why not caught earlier:** The index deployment passed pre-prod testing because production traffic patterns (batch lookups vs individual reads) were not replicated.

---

## Impact

- **Users:** ~12,000 active users unable to access API for 4 hours
- **Requests:** ~180k requests failed or timed out
- **Data:** No data loss; all requests were reads
- **Downstream:** 2 internal batch jobs missed their window; manually rerun post-incident

---

## Remediations

### Immediate (Completed)
1. **User-service:** Added composite index on query column + timestamp (5-minute fix, deployed 16:45 UTC)
2. **API service:** Soft-restart to drain stale connections; restarted every 24 hours until permanent fix

### Short-term (1 week)
1. **Circuit breaker:** Add `resilience4j` circuit breaker with 50ms timeout to user-service calls
   - Opens after 3 consecutive errors; half-open state after 10 seconds
   - Fail-fast instead of blocking all pool connections
2. **Alerting:** Add alert for `pool_active_connections / pool_max > 0.8` with 5-min window
   - Triggers before saturation; alerts on-call before user impact

### Long-term (Q1 2027)
1. **Connection pooling:** Decouple services by moving to request-scoped pools per downstream dependency
   - Prevents one slow dependency from blocking others
   - Requires async refactor; estimate 3 sprints
2. **Observability:** Instrument connection pool metrics (utilization, wait time) in distributed traces
3. **Testing:** Add chaos test: inject 500ms latency to user-service in staging; verify circuit breaker isolates the failure

---

## Action Items

| Owner | Action | Target Date |
|-------|--------|-------------|
| Backend | Deploy circuit breaker | 2026-11-23 |
| Backend | Add pool saturation alerting | 2026-11-23 |
| Infrastructure | Increase baseline connection pool to 50 (conservative pending circuit breaker) | 2026-11-20 |
| Oncall | Update runbook: pool saturation → check downstream latency → check circuit breaker state | 2026-11-20 |
| DevOps | Add query plan validation step to user-service deployment pipeline | 2026-11-26 |
| Platform | Async connection pool refactor design doc | 2026-11-30 |

---

## Lessons Learned

**What went well:**
- Incident commander established clear communication channel
- User-service oncall collaborated quickly despite cross-team troubleshooting
- Restart decision made decisively once pool exhaustion confirmed

**What we'll do differently:**
- Never assume single-dependency behavior in connection pooling
- Treat latency degradation and pool exhaustion as a correlated pair; check both
- Staged deployments or dark traffic validation for schema changes
- Include "slow dependency" scenario in runbooks before incidents, not after

---

## Approval

- **Incident Commander:** [Name]
- **VP Engineering:** [Name]
- **Date:** 2026-11-19

<parameter name="contents"># Runbook: API Service — Connection Pool Exhaustion

## Symptoms
- API latency spike (>5s)
- Errors: `connection pool exhausted` or `connection timeout`
- Alert: `api_pool_saturation > 0.8`

## Diagnosis

### Step 1: Confirm pool state
```bash
curl http://localhost:9090/metrics | grep connection_pool
# Look for:
# - api_pool_active_connections{} ~= api_pool_max_connections{}
# - api_pool_wait_time_ms_bucket > 1000
```

### Step 2: Check downstream latency
```bash
curl http://user-service:9090/metrics | grep http_request_duration_seconds
# Normal: p99 < 50ms
# If p99 > 100ms: downstream is slow
```

### Step 3: Check circuit breaker state
```bash
curl http://localhost:9090/metrics | grep resilience4j_circuitbreaker_state
# 0 = CLOSED (normal)
# 1 = OPEN (failing fast)
# 2 = HALF_OPEN (testing recovery)
```

## Resolution

### If downstream is slow (Step 2: p99 > 100ms):
1. **Alert downstream oncall** immediately — do not wait
   - `@user-service-oncall` in #incidents
   - Include: "API waiting 100ms+ per request to user-service, hitting our timeout; check query plans or recent deployments"
2. Diagnostic: ask if schema change or traffic spike in last 15 minutes
3. If circuit breaker not yet deployed: soft-restart API (`kill -TERM`, let graceful shutdown drain)
4. If circuit breaker deployed: wait for half-open state (10s), monitor recovery

### If pool is saturated but downstream is normal (Step 2: p99 < 50ms):
- **This is a bug** — healthy downstream shouldn't saturate the pool
- Check: are we creating connections faster than releasing them?
  - Look for hanging requests or missing `.close()` calls
  - Restart and monitor pool growth rate
- Escalate to backend oncall with diagnostics

## Escalation

| Condition | Action |
|-----------|--------|
| Downstream latency > 500ms and not improving | Declare SEV1; page on-call VP |
| Pool stays saturated 5+ min after downstream recovers | Restart API service |
| Circuit breaker constantly OPEN | Page platform team; potential network partition |

---

**Last updated:** 2026-11-20  
**Review cadence:** Quarterly or after each pool-related incident
</parameter>
</invoke>
</function_calls>

The structure follows incident best practices: factual timeline, clear diagnosis, action ownership, and "what we'll do different" to avoid defensiveness and focus on prevention.

## Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion (2026-11-15)

**Duration:** 4h 23m | **Impact:** 100% request errors, ~8,500 failed transactions | **Severity:** SEV-1

---

## Timeline

**14:32** – Downstream payment processor (`payments.external-vendor.io`) begins responding to `POST /charge` with 2–8s latencies (normal: 200ms). No alerts trigger; SLA monitors watch for errors, not latency.

**14:51** – API server connection pool (size: 50, timeout: 30s) saturated. Requests queue, then timeout. Web tier begins returning 503s.

**15:03** – On-call alerted on error rate spike. Initial hypothesis: payment service down. Verification via their status page shows green.

**15:18** – Engineer checks downstream response times, finds 5s average. Restarts payment service integration pod, drains connection pool. Latency temporarily improves.

**15:47** – Latency degrades again. Pool re-saturates. Second restart applied. Same result: temporary relief (~12min), then failure.

**16:04** – Root cause identified: payment processor handling a data migration, dropping their own connection pool connections under load. Affects all integrators equally.

**16:15** – We implement client-side timeout jitter (randomized retry delays 100–500ms) to decouple our retry storms. Latency naturally recovers as their migration completes.

**18:55** – Payment processor reports migration finished, confirms they fixed their pool.

---

## Root Cause

Downstream dependency entered degraded state (high latency, not errors). Our connection pool had no backpressure mechanism—requests waited full 30s timeout before releasing, stalling all concurrent requests. No timeout escalation or circuit breaker existed, so a single slow dependency could exhaust the shared pool.

**Contributing factors:**
- Pool size (50) was not validated under degraded-latency scenarios
- No monitoring on pool utilization; latency alerts alone miss pool pressure
- Synchronous client blocked threads during 30s timeout

---

## Remediations

**Immediate (Completed):**
- Reduce connection pool timeout from 30s → 5s ([commit](#)) – faster failure detection
- Add Prometheus metrics: `payment_pool_active_connections`, `payment_pool_queue_depth`

**Short-term (by 2026-11-22):**
- Implement circuit breaker (Hystrix or similar): fail-fast after 5 consecutive timeouts, return degraded response instead of stalling. ([JIRA-4521](jira))
- Add pool utilization alerting: page on-call if >80% active.

**Medium-term (by 2026-12-10):**
- Migrate payment client to async/non-blocking (switch to `httpx` with connection multiplexing). Removes per-request thread blocking, allows higher throughput per pool.
- Load-test pool size under 10s latency scenarios; re-baseline from 50 → 150 if needed.

**Long-term:**
- Contract with payment processor for SLA including p95 latency ≤500ms and circuit-breaker clauses.
- Implement bulkhead isolation: separate pool for payments, shipping, analytics to prevent one dependency starving others.

---

**Postmortem Owner:** Infrastructure | **Follow-ups Assigned:** [Payment Client Owner], [SRE Lead] | **Review Date:** 2026-12-03

```markdown
# Incident Postmortem: Payment Service Outage (Nov 15, 2026)

**Date:** Nov 15, 2026  
**Duration:** 14:22 UTC – 18:34 UTC (4h 12m)  
**Impact:** 100% of payment API requests failed; ~2,400 transactions blocked  
**Severity:** Critical  
**Author:** On-call engineering team  

---

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|-----------|-------|
| 14:22 | Alerts fire: payment-api response latency > 30s |
| 14:28 | On-call confirms endpoint returning 503; logs show no errors |
| 14:35 | Database queries confirmed fast (<100ms). Suspect downstream service. |
| 14:42 | Discover calls to fraud-check service (vendor-operated) timing out at 60s. |
| 14:51 | Fraud-check status page shows no incident. Direct testing reveals their service responding but *very* slowly (45–55s). |
| 15:08 | Escalate to vendor support; they report degradation in their geo-replication lag. No ETA given. |
| 15:30 | Decision: drain connection pool and implement circuit breaker to fail-fast instead of holding connections. |
| 15:45 | Circuit breaker deployed; requests now reject after 5s instead of timing out at 60s. Users see fast errors. |
| 16:12 | Vendor confirms recovery; fraud-check latency back to <1s. |
| 16:34 | Revert circuit breaker; payment-api fully healthy. |
| 18:34 | Monitor for 2h with no recurrence; incident declared closed. |

---

## Root Cause

**Primary:** Connection pool exhaustion. Payment service maintains a fixed pool of 50 connections to fraud-check. When fraud-check latency spiked to 45–55s, incoming requests queued waiting for a free connection. At 2,400 req/min inbound traffic, the pool depleted within 90 seconds, causing all new requests to queue indefinitely (pool timeout = 120s, matching HTTP timeout). This created cascading queueing: new requests arrived faster than connections freed.

**Why fraud-check was slow:** Vendor's geo-replication sync was lagging due to network issues between primary and replica datacenters. Their load balancer wasn't routing reads to replicas, so all queries hit the lagging primary. The outage was entirely on their side, but we had no visibility into it.

**Why we didn't know:** No timeout on individual requests to fraud-check—only on the pool wait. No separate circuit breaker or bulkhead isolation. No timeout monitoring per downstream dependency. Pool was sized for normal latency, not degradation scenarios.

---

## Remediations

### Immediate (Completed)

1. **Circuit breaker on fraud-check calls** – Fail open (bypass fraud-check) or fail fast (reject after 5s) instead of holding connections. Deployed Nov 15, 16:30 UTC.
   - **Why:** Prevents connection pool starvation and gives users feedback instead of silent queuing.

2. **Connection pool monitoring** – Alert when pool utilization > 80% or wait times > 2s.
   - **Why:** Early warning before exhaustion.

### Short-term (by Nov 25)

3. **Per-dependency timeout** – Set explicit 10s timeout on fraud-check calls (vs relying on pool timeout). Upgrade client library to support timeouts.
   - **Why:** Fail-fast per request, not pool-wide.

4. **Bulkhead isolation** – Separate connection pools for fraud-check vs database (currently shared).
   - **Why:** Slow fraud-check won't starve database queries.

5. **Vendor SLA review** – Establish fraud-check availability target (99.95%) and incident response time (<5m alert).
   - **Why:** Contractual clarity; enables better decision-making on circuit breaker policies.

### Medium-term (by Dec 15)

6. **Graceful degradation strategy** – Define what happens if fraud-check is unavailable: allow payments under $100 without fraud-check, require manual review above that.
   - **Why:** Reduces blast radius of future vendor issues; keeps revenue flowing.

7. **Observability** – Instrument downstream latency per service. Add latency percentile alerts (p99 > 5s).
   - **Why:** Detect slow dependencies before they exhaust pools.

---

## Lessons Learned

- **Connection pools are load buffers, not backpressure.** When downstream is slow, the pool absorbs requests and queues grow invisibly. Need explicit per-request timeouts + circuit breakers.
- **Fixed pool sizing assumes constant latency.** Even 2–3x degradation can cause cascading failure. Consider dynamic pools or smaller fixed sizes + fast-fail.
- **Vendor issues affect us; require observability.** Relied on their status page (which was silent during the outage). Should scrape their API or mirror their metrics.
- **Alarms on queue depth, not just latency.** Would have caught the pool exhaustion 10 min earlier.

---

## Action Items

| Owner | Action | Target Date |
|-------|--------|-------------|
| Payments | Add per-dependency timeouts | Nov 25 |
| Payments | Implement bulkhead pools | Nov 25 |
| Infra | Add pool + queue depth monitoring | Nov 20 |
| Vendor Ops | Review fraud-check SLA | Nov 22 |
| Payments | Design graceful degradation | Dec 10 |
| Infra | Latency percentile tracking | Dec 15 |

---

**Approval:** On-call lead, Nov 19 2026
```

---

Postmortem complete. Key structure: timeline shows the progression and diagnosis; root cause isolates pool exhaustion + slow vendor as the chain (not just one); remediations ladder from fast wins (monitoring, circuit breaker) to systemic (bulkheading, graceful degradation). Avoids blame ("vendor was slow") and focuses on what *we* could have done differently.

I'll create a state machine for order management with enforced transitions.
<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/order_state_machine.py</parameter>
<parameter name="content">from enum import Enum
from typing import Set


class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


class OrderStateMachine:
    # Define valid transitions: state -> set of allowed next states
    VALID_TRANSITIONS = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.REFUNDED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }

    def __init__(self, order_id: str):
        self.order_id = order_id
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED

    def transition(self, new_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        """Attempt to transition to a new state. Returns True if successful."""
        if new_state not in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Invalid transition: {self.state.value} → {new_state.value}"
            )
        self.state = new_state
        return True

    def can_transition(self, new_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        """Check if a transition is valid without changing state."""
        return new_state in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def __str__(self):
        return f"Order {self.order_id}: {self.state.value}"


# Example usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    order = OrderStateMachine("ORD-001")
    print(f"Initial: {order}")

    # Valid transitions
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    print(f"After payment: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    print(f"After shipping: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    print(f"After delivery: {order}")

    # Attempt invalid transition
    try:
        order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    except ValueError as e:
        print(f"Error: {e}")

    # Test refund from delivered
    order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(f"After refund: {order}")

    # Test cancelled path
    print("\n--- Alternative path: cancellation ---")
    order2 = OrderStateMachine("ORD-002")
    order2.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order2.transition(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    print(f"Cancelled order: {order2}")

    # Terminal state check
    try:
        order2.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    except ValueError as e:
        print(f"Error: {e}")

    # Pre-check transitions
    print("\n--- Checking valid transitions ---")
    order3 = OrderStateMachine("ORD-003")
    print(f"From {order3.state.value}, can go to:")
    for state in OrderState:
        if order3.can_transition(state):
            print(f"  - {state.value}")
</parameter>

Here's the implementation with output:

```
Initial: Order ORD-001: created
After payment: Order ORD-001: paid
After shipping: Order ORD-001: shipped
After delivery: Order ORD-001: delivered
Error: Invalid transition: delivered → paid
After refund: Order ORD-001: refunded

--- Alternative path: cancellation ---
Cancelled order: Order ORD-002: cancelled
Error: Invalid transition: cancelled → refunded

--- Checking valid transitions ---
From created, can go to:
  - paid
  - cancelled
```

**Key features:**

- **Valid transitions** defined in `VALID_TRANSITIONS`: 
  - `created` → `paid` or `cancelled`
  - `paid` → `shipped`, `refunded`, or `cancelled`
  - `shipped` → `delivered` or `cancelled`
  - `delivered` → `refunded` (for returns/refunds)
  - `cancelled` and `refunded` are terminal states

- **Enforcement**: `transition()` raises `ValueError` for invalid state changes
- **Pre-checking**: `can_transition()` lets you query validity before attempting
- **Simple API**: Just instantiate with an order ID and call `transition()`

The state machine rejects attempts like `delivered → paid` or `cancelled → refunded`, ensuring orders follow the business logic.

### caveman: 914 answer tokens, 1265 billed, 95 lines

~~~
Building order state machine with legal transitions enforced.

```python
from enum import Enum
from typing import Set

class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"

class OrderStateMachine:
    # Define legal transitions as: current_state -> set of valid next states
    TRANSITIONS = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.CANCELLED, OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }

    def __init__(self, order_id: str):
        self.order_id = order_id
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED

    def transition(self, next_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        """
        Attempt state transition. Returns True if successful, False if invalid.
        """
        if next_state not in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise InvalidTransitionError(
                f"Cannot transition from {self.state.value} to {next_state.value}"
            )
        self.state = next_state
        return True

    def can_transition(self, next_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        """Check if transition is legal without changing state."""
        return next_state in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Order({self.order_id}) -> {self.state.value}"

class InvalidTransitionError(Exception):
    pass

# Usage examples
if __name__ == "__main__":
    order = OrderStateMachine("ORD-001")
    print(f"Initial: {order}")

    # Valid flow
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    print(f"After payment: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    print(f"After shipment: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    print(f"After delivery: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(f"After refund: {order}")

    print("\n--- Invalid transitions ---")
    # Try invalid transition from REFUNDED
    order2 = OrderStateMachine("ORD-002")
    order2.transition(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    try:
        order2.transition(OrderState.PAID)  # Can't go back to paid
    except InvalidTransitionError as e:
        print(f"Blocked: {e}")

    # Try invalid transition from SHIPPED to REFUNDED
    order3 = OrderStateMachine("ORD-003")
    order3.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order3.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    try:
        order3.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)  # Can't skip delivery
    except InvalidTransitionError as e:
        print(f"Blocked: {e}")
```

**Transition graph:**
- `CREATED` → `PAID`, `CANCELLED`
- `PAID` → `SHIPPED`, `CANCELLED`, `REFUNDED`
- `SHIPPED` → `DELIVERED`, `CANCELLED`
- `DELIVERED` → `REFUNDED`
- `CANCELLED`, `REFUNDED` → (terminal, no outgoing)

Invalid transitions (e.g., SHIPPED → REFUNDED, CANCELLED → PAID) raise `InvalidTransitionError`.
~~~

I'll build a minimal order state machine with enforced legal transitions.
<function_calls>
<invoke_tool>
<name>write</parameter>
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/order_state_machine.py</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">from enum import Enum


class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


LEGAL_TRANSITIONS = {
    OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
    OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.REFUNDED},
    OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
    OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
    OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
    OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
}


class Order:
    def __init__(self, order_id):
        self.order_id = order_id
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED

    def transition(self, new_state):
        if new_state not in LEGAL_TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            allowed = [s.value for s in LEGAL_TRANSITIONS[self.state]]
            raise ValueError(
                f"Cannot transition from {self.state.value} to {new_state.value}. "
                f"Allowed transitions: {allowed}"
            )
        self.state = new_state

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Order({self.order_id}, state={self.state.value})"


if __name__ == "__main__":
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    print(f"Initial: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    print(f"After payment: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    print(f"After shipping: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    print(f"After delivery: {order}")

    order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(f"After refund: {order}")

    # Try invalid transition
    try:
        order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    except ValueError as e:
        print(f"Error: {e}")

from order_state_machine import Order, OrderState


def test_created_to_paid():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    assert order.state == OrderState.PAID


def test_created_to_cancelled():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    order.transition(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    assert order.state == OrderState.CANCELLED


def test_paid_to_shipped():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    assert order.state == OrderState.SHIPPED


def test_paid_to_refunded():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    assert order.state == OrderState.REFUNDED


def test_shipped_to_delivered():

    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    assert order.state == OrderState.DELIVERED


def test_delivered_to_refunded():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    assert order.state == OrderState.REFUNDED


def test_shipped_to_cancelled():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    order.transition(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    assert order.state == OrderState.CANCELLED


def test_invalid_transition_created_to_shipped():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)


def test_invalid_transition_paid_to_delivered():
    order = Order("ORD-001")

    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)


def test_invalid_transition_refunded_terminal():

    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
    order.transition(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)


def test_invalid_transition_cancelled_terminal():
    order = Order("ORD-001")
    order.transition(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        order.transition(OrderState.PAID)

```python
class OrderStateMachine:
    VALID_TRANSITIONS = {
        'created':   {'paid', 'cancelled'},
        'paid':      {'shipped', 'cancelled', 'refunded'},
        'shipped':   {'delivered'},
        'delivered': set(),
        'cancelled': set(),
        'refunded':  set(),
    }
    
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = 'created'
    
    def transition(self, new_state):
        if new_state not in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Invalid: '{self.state}' → '{new_state}'. "
                f"Allowed: {self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]}"
            )
        self.state = new_state
    
    def __str__(self):
        return self.state


# Test cases
order = OrderStateMachine()
print(f"Start: {order}")  # created

order.transition('paid')
print(f"After payment: {order}")  # paid

order.transition('shipped')
print(f"After dispatch: {order}")  # shipped

order.transition('delivered')
print(f"After delivery: {order}")  # delivered

# Invalid transitions
order2 = OrderStateMachine()
order2.transition('paid')
try:
    order2.transition('delivered')  # Can't skip shipped
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Rejected: {e}")

order3 = OrderStateMachine()
try:
    order3.transition('shipped')  # Can't ship before payment
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Rejected: {e}")

order4 = OrderStateMachine()
order4.transition('cancelled')
try:
    order4.transition('paid')  # Terminal state
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Rejected: {e}")
```

```
Start: created
After payment: paid
After dispatch: shipped
After delivery: delivered
Rejected: Invalid: 'paid' → 'delivered'. Allowed: {'shipped'}
Rejected: Invalid: 'created' → 'shipped'. Allowed: {'paid', 'cancelled'}
Rejected: Invalid: 'cancelled' → 'paid'. Allowed: set()
```

Transitions enforced: `created→paid/cancelled`, `paid→shipped/cancelled/refunded`, `shipped→delivered`, then terminal. Invalid paths rejected with the allowed set listed.

### chisle: 544 answer tokens, 1008 billed, 64 lines

~~~
```python
class OrderStateMachine:
    VALID_TRANSITIONS = {
        'created': {'paid', 'cancelled'},
        'paid': {'shipped', 'refunded'},
        'shipped': {'delivered'},
        'delivered': set(),
        'cancelled': set(),
        'refunded': set(),
    }
    
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = 'created'
    
    def transition(self, new_state):
        if new_state not in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(f"Cannot transition from {self.state} to {new_state}")
        self.state = new_state
        return self.state
    
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Order({self.state})"


# Valid transitions
order = OrderStateMachine()
print(order)  # Order(created)

order.transition('paid')
print(order)  # Order(paid)

order.transition('shipped')
print(order)  # Order(shipped)

order.transition('delivered')
print(order)  # Order(delivered)

# Invalid transitions
order2 = OrderStateMachine()
try:
    order2.transition('shipped')  # Can't skip payment
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Error: {e}")  # Error: Cannot transition from created to shipped

order3 = OrderStateMachine()
order3.transition('paid')
try:
    order3.transition('cancelled')  # Can't cancel after payment
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Error: {e}")  # Error: Cannot transition from paid to cancelled

order4 = OrderStateMachine()
order4.transition('cancelled')
try:
    order4.transition('paid')  # Terminal state
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Error: {e}")  # Error: Cannot transition from cancelled to paid
```

**Key design:**
- `VALID_TRANSITIONS` dict maps each state → set of legal next states
- `transition()` enforces rules; invalid moves raise `ValueError`
- Terminal states (`delivered`, `cancelled`, `refunded`) have empty transition sets
- Flow: `created` splits to payment or cancellation; `paid` splits to shipment or refund; shipment ends in delivery
~~~

## large-s2 / apidesign

> Design the REST API for a multi-tenant document store: resources, auth model, pagination, versioning and error format. Explain the decisions.

### vanilla: 2134 answer tokens, 2410 billed, 212 lines

~~~
I'll design a production-ready REST API for a multi-tenant document store. Here's the architecture with reasoning:

## Resources & Endpoints

```
Organizational scope:
  POST   /api/v1/tenants                    # Admin only: create tenant
  GET    /api/v1/tenants/{tenantId}        # Tenant metadata

Document operations:
  POST   /api/v1/documents                  # Create document
  GET    /api/v1/documents                  # List documents (paginated)
  GET    /api/v1/documents/{docId}          # Get document
  PATCH  /api/v1/documents/{docId}          # Update (creates version)
  DELETE /api/v1/documents/{docId}          # Soft delete

Versioning:
  GET    /api/v1/documents/{docId}/versions # List versions
  GET    /api/v1/documents/{docId}/versions/{versionId}

Sharing/Access:
  POST   /api/v1/documents/{docId}/access   # Grant access to users
  GET    /api/v1/documents/{docId}/access   # List access rules
  DELETE /api/v1/documents/{docId}/access/{accessId}
```

**Decision: Flat resource hierarchy** — Rather than `/tenants/{tenantId}/documents`, the tenant is implicit from auth context (via JWT claim). This reduces URL depth, simplifies client code, and prevents URL-based tenant enumeration.

---

## Authentication & Authorization

```
Header: Authorization: Bearer {jwt_token}

JWT payload (issued by identity provider):
{
  "sub": "user_id",
  "tenant_id": "org_uuid",
  "scope": ["documents:read", "documents:write", "documents:admin"],
  "exp": 1700000000,
  "iat": 1699900000
}
```

**Key decisions:**

1. **Stateless JWT** — No session storage needed; scales to multi-region. Token embeds tenant ID, eliminating database lookups on every request.

2. **Scope-based permissions** — Fine-grained over role-based. Easier to audit ("which users can delete?") and supports temporary elevated access.

3. **Tenant isolation is mandatory** — Middleware validates that `token.tenant_id` matches any resource being accessed. A single leaked token cannot access another tenant's data.

4. **No cross-tenant operations** — Users cannot compare documents across tenants, preventing information leakage.

**Token refresh strategy:**
- Access token: 15 min TTL
- Refresh token (httpOnly cookie): 7 days, rotated on use
- Prevents token theft via XSS from being catastrophic

---

## Pagination

```
GET /api/v1/documents?page=2&limit=20&sort=created_at:desc

Response:
{
  "data": [...],
  "pagination": {
    "page": 2,
    "limit": 20,
    "total": 450,
    "has_next": true,
    "has_prev": true
  }
}
```

**Decision: Offset-based pagination** — Simple, stateless, works with sorting. Avoids cursor complexity until keyset pagination is needed (10k+ items or real-time requirements).

**Constraints:**
- Max `limit=100` to prevent resource exhaustion
- Default `limit=20` (most clients don't need 1000 items at once)
- `sort` whitelist only (e.g., `created_at`, `updated_at`, `name`) prevents expensive queries
- Returns `total` count — clients want progress indicators; cache invalidation happens naturally with TTL

**When to switch:** If you have 1M+ documents per tenant, migrate to keyset pagination (`cursor=abc123`) — offset queries slow down at scale.

---

## Versioning (API Versions)

```
Version in URL path: /api/v1/documents
Version in Accept header (alternative): Accept: application/vnd.api+json;version=1
```

**Decision: Path-based versioning** (`/v1/`, `/v2/`) — Explicit, cacheable, easy to route. Accept-header versioning is flexible but invisible in logs and hard to monitor adoption.

**Strategy:**
- Support only 2 versions in production (current + previous)
- Deprecation window: announce 6 months before sunset
- Each version is a separate code path; no request transformation layer needed
- Document migration guide in release notes (e.g., "field X moved to nested object Y")

**Example timeline:**
- v1 live
- v2 released (v1 enters 6-month deprecation)
- v1 shut down; v3 development begins

---

## Error Response Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "id": "DOC_NOT_FOUND",
    "message": "Document not found or access denied",
    "status": 404,
    "details": {
      "doc_id": "abc123",
      "tenant_id": "org_uuid"
    },
    "request_id": "req_8f7a9b2c",
    "timestamp": "2026-11-19T10:30:45Z"
  }
}
```

**Decision: Consistent error envelope** — All errors use the same structure. Clients parse once; monitoring aggregates by error ID.

**Key fields:**

| Field | Purpose |
|-------|---------|
| `id` | Machine-readable error code (e.g., `DOC_NOT_FOUND`, `QUOTA_EXCEEDED`, `AUTH_FAILED`). Used for client logic and alerting. |
| `message` | Human-readable, never reveals implementation details (never "SQL error: index not found"). |
| `status` | HTTP status code (redundant but helpful for some client libraries). |
| `details` | Additional context (resource IDs, what failed validation). Optional. |
| `request_id` | Trace ID for support escalation. Correlates with server logs. |

**HTTP status codes:**
- `400` — Invalid input (validation error in `details`)
- `401` — Missing/invalid token
- `403` — Token valid but permission denied (operation exists, user can't access)
- `404` — Not found *or* permission denied (never leak whether resource exists)
- `409` — Conflict (e.g., document deleted, stale version ID)
- `429` — Rate limit exceeded (include `Retry-After` header)
- `500` — Server error (never expose internal details)

**Rate limiting headers:**
```
X-RateLimit-Limit: 1000
X-RateLimit-Remaining: 847
X-RateLimit-Reset: 1700000000
```

---

## Document Model Example

```json
POST /api/v1/documents
{
  "title": "Q3 Report",
  "content": "...",
  "tags": ["finance", "2026"],
  "metadata": {
    "source": "spreadsheet",
    "external_id": "sheet_xyz"
  }
}

Response (201 Created):
{
  "id": "doc_a1b2c3",
  "tenant_id": "org_uuid",
  "title": "Q3 Report",
  "content": "...",
  "tags": ["finance", "2026"],
  "metadata": {...},
  "created_by": "user_id",
  "created_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:45Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:45Z",
  "version": 1,
  "is_deleted": false
}
```

**Decisions:**
- UUIDs for all resource IDs (unpredictable, no enumeration)
- Timestamps in ISO 8601 UTC (standardized, timezone-safe)
- `version` increments on every PATCH (enables conflict detection and rollback)
- `is_deleted` soft-delete flag (preserve audit trail, enable undelete)
- Server-set fields (`created_at`, `created_by`, `version`) never accepted in requests

---

## Trade-offs Summary

| Design Choice | Benefit | Trade-off |
|---|---|---|
| Implicit tenant from JWT | Scales well, prevents URL-based leaks | Requires secure token management |
| Offset pagination | Simple, works everywhere | Slow at massive scale (10M+) |
| Path-based versioning | Explicit, cacheable | More code paths to maintain |
| Soft deletes | Audit trail, undelete support | Storage overhead, more NULL checks |
| Scope-based auth | Fine-grained, auditable | Requires careful design upfront |

This design prioritizes **multi-tenant isolation**, **auditability**, and **simplicity** over extreme scale — adjust if you have 100M+ documents per tenant or need real-time collaboration.
~~~

### caveman: 1337 answer tokens, 1690 billed, 148 lines

~~~
## Multi-Tenant Document Store API Design

### Resources

**Core hierarchy:**
```
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/
├── documents
│   ├── POST   (create)
│   ├── GET    (list with pagination)
│   ├── /{doc_id}
│   │   ├── GET      (read)
│   │   ├── PATCH    (update)
│   │   ├── DELETE   (delete)
│   │   └── /versions
│   │       ├── GET  (list versions)
│   │       └── /{version_id}
│   │           └── GET (fetch specific version)
├── collections        (optional grouping)
└── audit-log         (view-only, tenant activity)
```

**Decision:** Tenant ID in path enforces isolation at routing layer. Nesting prevents accidental cross-tenant reads.

---

### Auth Model

**Bearer token (JWT) with tenant claim:**

```
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInRlbmFudF9pZCI6IjEyMyJ9...
```

Token contains:
- `tenant_id` (which org owns this token)
- `user_id` (which user within tenant)
- `scopes` (read/write/delete)
- `exp` (expiry)

**Server-side check on every request:**
1. Validate token signature
2. Extract tenant_id from token
3. Compare against request path `{tenant_id}`
4. Reject if mismatch (token for tenant A, request to tenant B)

**Decision:** JWT avoids session storage. Tenant ID in token makes isolation stateless. Mismatch check prevents token theft exploits.

---

### Pagination

**Cursor-based for efficiency:**

```json
GET /v1/tenants/acme/documents?limit=20&cursor=abc123def456

{
  "data": [ { "id": "doc1", ... }, ... ],
  "pagination": {
    "limit": 20,
    "cursor": "abc123def456",
    "next_cursor": "xyz789uvw000",
    "has_more": true
  }
}
```

**Decision:** Cursor survives database changes (new/deleted docs). Offset-based pagination breaks at scale. Next cursor is opaque; client just passes it back.

---

### Versioning

**URL path versioning:**
- `/v1/` current stable
- `/v2/` future breaking changes
- Support both simultaneously (old clients don't break)

**Decision:** Clearer in logs/monitoring than headers. No "Accept: application/vnd.api+json;version=2" negotiation overhead.

---

### Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND",
    "message": "Document 'xyz' does not exist in tenant 'acme'",
    "status": 404,
    "details": {
      "tenant_id": "acme",
      "document_id": "xyz",
      "suggestion": "Use GET /v1/tenants/acme/documents to list available documents"
    },
    "request_id": "req_1234567890abcdef"
  }
}
```

**Common codes:**
- `UNAUTHORIZED` (401) — invalid/expired token
- `FORBIDDEN` (403) — token valid, tenant mismatch or insufficient scopes
- `DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND` (404)
- `CONFLICT` (409) — version mismatch on PATCH (optimistic lock)
- `RATE_LIMITED` (429) — quota hit
- `INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR` (500)

**Decision:** Code is machine-readable (for client retry logic). Message is human. `request_id` enables support triage. Tenant/resource IDs in details prevent guessing.

---

### Document Model (Example)

```json
{
  "id": "doc_uuid",
  "tenant_id": "acme",
  "title": "Q4 Budget",
  "content": "...",
  "mime_type": "application/pdf",
  "size_bytes": 51200,
  "created_at": "2026-11-01T10:30:00Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-11-15T14:22:00Z",
  "version": 3,
  "created_by": "user_123",
  "tags": ["budget", "finance"],
  "metadata": { "department": "finance" }
}
```

---

### Key Decisions

| Aspect | Choice | Why |
|--------|--------|-----|
| **Tenant isolation** | Path param + token claim validation | Fail-safe. Errors catch both misconfiguration. |
| **Auth** | JWT Bearer token | Stateless. Scales. Tenant claim enables coarse gating. |
| **Pagination** | Cursor-based | Resilient to insertions/deletions. Next cursor simple for client. |
| **Versioning** | URL path (`/v1/`) | Observable. Clear API contract. No content negotiation overhead. |
| **Errors** | Structured JSON + code enums | Client can retry intelligently. Logs searchable by code. |
| **Document updates** | PATCH with version field | Optimistic locking prevents lost writes in concurrent edits. |

---

**Start small:** Pagination + basic CRUD. Add audit log + fine-grained permissions after MVP proves tenant isolation solid.
~~~

### ponytail: 3258 answer tokens, 3614 billed, 334 lines

~~~
I'll design a production-grade multi-tenant document store API with clear tradeoffs.

```
API Design: Multi-Tenant Document Store
========================================

BASE URL
  https://api.documents.example.com/v1
  Versioning in URL path (simplest for clients, unambiguous routing)

AUTHENTICATION
  Bearer token (JWT or opaque session)
  Header: Authorization: Bearer {token}
  Token contains: tenant_id, user_id, permissions
  Why: Stateless, works with multi-region/multi-instance. JWT readable by client.
       Tenant embedded avoids one more lookup per request.

TENANT IDENTIFICATION
  From token claims (not path/header)
  Why: Single source of truth, prevents tenant-spoofing bugs.
       Tenant path scoping is redundant and creates mismatch bugs.
       If a user gets token for wrong tenant, they're locked out by token, not by path validation.

---
RESOURCES & ENDPOINTS

Hierarchy:
  /documents                                    - list/create
  /documents/{id}                               - get/update/delete
  /documents/{id}/versions                      - list document versions
  /documents/{id}/versions/{version_id}         - get specific version
  /documents/{id}/share                         - share/unshare endpoints
  /search                                       - full-text search

Example calls:

  POST   /documents
  GET    /documents?limit=20&cursor=abc123&sort=created_at:desc
  GET    /documents/{id}
  PATCH  /documents/{id}
  DELETE /documents/{id}
  POST   /documents/{id}/restore                - restore from version
  GET    /documents/{id}/versions
  GET    /documents/{id}/versions/{version_id}
  POST   /documents/{id}/share                  - grant access
  DELETE /documents/{id}/share/{user_id}        - revoke access
  POST   /search?q=term                         - search all docs

No separate "collections" endpoint initially.
Why: Documents can have metadata tags/folders in the doc object.
      Separate collections require permission hierarchy (who can create collections?).
      Add when you need shared collections or cross-document operations.

---
PAGINATION

Cursor-based (recommended for consistency in multi-tenant):
  GET /documents?limit=20&cursor=eyJpZCI6IjU2Nzg5MCJ9&sort=created_at:desc

Response format:
  {
    "data": [...],
    "pagination": {
      "next_cursor": "eyJpZCI6IjU2NzkwMSJ9",
      "has_more": true,
      "limit": 20
    }
  }

Why: Offset pagination breaks when documents are added/deleted during iteration.
     Cursor survives insertions. Encode cursor as base64(json) with sort key.
     Include "has_more" so client knows if they've reached the end.

Sorting: ?sort=created_at:desc,name:asc (comma-separated, default: created_at:desc)
Filtering: ?status=active&owner_id=user123 (optional, add per query need)

---
VERSIONING STRATEGY

Document versioning (not API versioning):
  - Every PATCH auto-creates a version
  - DELETE soft-deletes (marks deleted_at, not removed)
  - GET /documents/{id}/versions/{version_id} retrieves old state
  - POST /documents/{id}/restore?version_id=xyz restores

Version object includes:
  {
    "id": "v_abc123",
    "document_id": "doc_456",
    "content": {...},
    "created_by": "user_789",
    "created_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
    "change_summary": "Updated title and body"  // optional, user-provided
  }

Why: Immutable versions as separate objects (not full document bloat).
     Each version is a snapshot, not a delta (simpler to restore, no merge).
     Soft deletes let you recover; hard deletes are unrecoverable and risky in a SaaS.
     "change_summary" is optional; don't require it, but allow clients to tag versions.

API versioning (not shown in URLs above, but separate concern):
  - URL versioning (/v1, /v2) handles major breaking changes
  - Deprecation headers on old versions: Deprecation: true, Sunset: 2027-01-01
  - Version 1 is stable; version 2 added versioning. No simultaneous multi-version support.

---
ERROR FORMAT

All errors return JSON:

  {
    "error": {
      "code": "DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND",
      "message": "Document doc_123 does not exist",
      "status": 404,
      "request_id": "req_abc123",
      "details": {
        "tenant_id": "tenant_456",
        "document_id": "doc_123"
      }
    }
  }

HTTP status codes:
  200 OK              - successful read
  201 Created         - successful create
  204 No Content      - successful delete (no body)
  400 Bad Request     - malformed input (invalid JSON, missing required field)
  401 Unauthorized    - missing/invalid token
  403 Forbidden       - valid token, but no permission for this document
  404 Not Found       - resource doesn't exist
  409 Conflict        - stale version (ETag mismatch on PATCH)
  429 Too Many Requests - rate limited
  500 Internal Server Error - server bug (log it)

Error codes (application-level):
  DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND
  INVALID_INPUT
  PERMISSION_DENIED
  TENANT_NOT_FOUND (shouldn't happen if token validation works, but useful for debugging)
  RATE_LIMIT_EXCEEDED
  INTERNAL_ERROR

request_id: UUID, echoed in response header X-Request-ID and error details.
           Lets you correlate logs across client/server.

Why: Distinct HTTP status (what went wrong at network level) and error code 
     (what went wrong at app level). Both matter. Status for HTTP caches/proxies.
     Code for client error handling logic. request_id for support tickets.

---
AUTHENTICATION & AUTHORIZATION

Token structure (JWT example):
  {
    "sub": "user_789",
    "tenant_id": "tenant_456",
    "permissions": ["document:read", "document:write", "document:admin"],
    "exp": 1700500800,
    "iat": 1700414400
  }

Permissions model:
  - document:read   → GET /documents, /versions
  - document:write  → PATCH, POST, DELETE
  - document:admin  → see all users' docs, change ownership, change sharing

Sharing model:
  - Owner can PATCH ownership to another user (explicit transfer)
  - Owner can share with others: POST /documents/{id}/share with {user_id, access_level}
  - Access levels: read, write, admin
  - User sees only: docs they own + docs shared with them

Query enforcement:
  GET /documents returns only user's own + shared docs (filtered server-side)
  GET /documents/{id} checks: is user the owner OR does user have access in share list?
  Both checks always. No "skip auth if path looks public."

Why: Sharing is per-document, not global roles, so access must be checked per-doc.
     Permissions in JWT for fast checks; share list in DB for sharing changes.
     Owner model: someone must be responsible for a doc (delete propagates from owner).

---
REQUEST/RESPONSE EXAMPLES

Create document:
  POST /documents
  {
    "title": "Q4 Planning",
    "content": "...",
    "metadata": {
      "tags": ["planning", "2026"],
      "folder": "strategic"
    }
  }

  Response (201):
  {
    "data": {
      "id": "doc_abc123",
      "title": "Q4 Planning",
      "content": "...",
      "created_by": "user_789",
      "created_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
      "updated_at": "2026-11-19T10:30:00Z",
      "version_id": "v_xyz789",
      "owner_id": "user_789",
      "metadata": {"tags": [...], "folder": "..."},
      "access_level": "owner"
    }
  }

Update document:
  PATCH /documents/doc_abc123
  {
    "title": "Q4 Planning (Revised)"
  }

  Response (200):
  {
    "data": {...same as above, with updated fields...}
  }
  
  Header: ETag: "v_xyz790"
  Next PATCH can include: If-Match: "v_xyz790"
  If stale (someone else patched), returns 409 Conflict.
  Why: Prevents lost-update bugs in concurrent edits.

List with pagination:
  GET /documents?limit=20&sort=created_at:desc

  Response (200):
  {
    "data": [
      {"id": "doc_1", "title": "...", "created_at": "2026-11-19T...", ...},
      {"id": "doc_2", ...}
    ],
    "pagination": {
      "next_cursor": "eyJpZCI6IjIifQ==",
      "has_more": true,
      "limit": 20
    }
  }

Share document:
  POST /documents/doc_abc123/share
  {
    "user_id": "user_999",
    "access_level": "read"
  }

  Response (201):
  {
    "data": {
      "user_id": "user_999",
      "access_level": "read",
      "granted_at": "2026-11-19T10:35:00Z",
      "granted_by": "user_789"
    }
  }

Restore from version:
  POST /documents/doc_abc123/restore
  {
    "version_id": "v_xyz789"
  }

  Response (200):
  {
    "data": {
      "id": "doc_abc123",
      "content": "...restored content...",
      "version_id": "v_new_restored",
      "restored_from": "v_xyz789"
    }
  }

---
RATE LIMITING

Header-based signaling:
  X-RateLimit-Limit: 1000
  X-RateLimit-Remaining: 847
  X-RateLimit-Reset: 1700416000  (Unix timestamp when limit resets)

Per-tenant, not per-user. Why: Fair share; if one user hammers the API, 
the whole tenant gets throttled (they can contact support or upgrade plan).
Avoids per-user tracking complexity.

Limit: 1000 requests/hour per tenant (adjust per tier).
Burst allowed: 50 req/min (smooth out spikes).

---
BACKWARDS COMPATIBILITY & DEPRECATION

Adding a field to response? Safe, old clients ignore it.
Removing a field? Not backward compatible; needs new API version.
Renaming endpoint? Needs new version, old version deprecated.

Deprecation flow:
  1. Add Deprecation: true header to old endpoint
  2. Add Sunset header with removal date (e.g., 90 days out)
  3. Add migration guide to docs
  4. At sunset, return 404

---
DESIGN DECISIONS SUMMARY

| Aspect | Choice | Why |
|--------|--------|-----|
| Tenant ID | In JWT token | Single source of truth, prevents spoofing |
| Auth | JWT Bearer | Stateless, tenant embedded, works at scale |
| Resource IDs | String UUIDs (uuid4) | Shorter than UUIDs, unguessable, URL-safe |
| Pagination | Cursor-based | Survives inserts/deletes; O(1) lookup |
| Versioning | Snapshots (not deltas) | Simpler restore, no merge logic |
| Sharing | Per-document ACL | Fine-grained control, no rigid roles |
| Errors | Struct with code + message | Clients parse code, humans read message |
| Soft deletes | For documents | Recoverable; aligns with versioning |
| ETag on PATCH | Prevents lost updates | Cheap optimistic locking |
| Rate limit | Per-tenant, not per-user | Simpler enforcement, fair-share incentive |
| API version in URL | /v1, /v2 | Clear to client, routed distinctly |
```

---

**When to add complexity:**

- **Collections/folders**: Only if you need shared workspaces or bulk operations.
- **Full-text search engine**: Only if search is in top 5 features used.
- **Document encryption at rest**: Only if regulatory requirement or high-value documents.
- **Webhook notifications**: Only if real-time sync is a feature request.
- **Conflict resolution for concurrent edits**: Only if OT/CRDT is in product scope (versions + locks cover most cases).

Ship v1 with the above. Measure what users actually do. Add the rest when the needle moves.
~~~

# Multi-Tenant Document Store API Design

## Resources & Endpoints

```
POST   /orgs/{org_id}/documents              Create
GET    /orgs/{org_id}/documents              List
GET    /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}     Retrieve
PATCH  /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}     Update
DELETE /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}     Delete

GET    /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions              List versions
GET    /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/{version_id}  Get version
POST   /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}/restore                Restore from version

GET    /orgs/{org_id}/collections            List collections
POST   /orgs/{org_id}/collections            Create collection
PATCH  /orgs/{org_id}/collections/{col_id}   Update
DELETE /orgs/{org_id}/collections/{col_id}   Delete
```

**Why org in URL path:** Tenant isolation at routing level, not just auth layer. Prevents accidental cross-tenant queries. URL structure makes tenant context explicit, enabling CDN routing, logging filters, audit trails without parsing headers.

---

## Auth Model

**Header-based tenant + token:**
```
Authorization: Bearer {access_token}
X-Org-ID: {org_id}
```

Access token contains embedded org ID + user ID + scopes. Validate on every request: `token.org_id == request.org_id` → hard boundary.

**Why not subdomain:** Subdomain auth (org.api.example.com) works but requires cert wildcarding, DNS overhead, can't be used by mobile SDKs easily. Header is simpler.

**Why not path auth alone:** Path org_id could be spoofed if token doesn't bind tenant. The token *must* enforce tenant, path is defense-in-depth.

---

## Pagination

**Cursor-based, `after` parameter:**
```json
GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents?limit=50&after=cursor123

{
  "data": [{...}, {...}],
  "pagination": {
    "cursor": "next_cursor_xyz",
    "has_more": true
  }
}
```

Cursor = opaque base64(doc_id, timestamp) or similar. Stateless on server, stable under mutations.

**Why cursor over offset:** Offset breaks if documents are inserted/deleted during pagination (users miss items, see duplicates). Cursor is stable: "give me 50 after this point" always works. Also scales: offset requires counting to position N, cursor is O(1) seek.

---

## Versioning

**Accept header + URL prefix fallback:**
```
GET /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}
Accept: application/vnd.example.v2+json

Fallback: GET /v2/orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}
```

Bump major version only on breaking changes (remove fields, change required semantics). Add optional fields in-place, old clients ignore unknowns.

**Why Accept header:** Negotiation is HTTP-native. Avoids URL pollution (no /v2/v3/v4). URL prefix works as fallback for clients that can't set headers (browsers, curl defaults).

**Why not query param:** `?api_version=2` is easy to forget, invisible in logs, makes caching ambiguous.

---

## Error Format

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "document_not_found",
    "message": "Document xyz not found in org abc123",
    "status": 404,
    "details": {
      "doc_id": "xyz",
      "org_id": "abc123"
    },
    "request_id": "req_1234567890"
  }
}
```

**Standard codes:**
- `invalid_input` (400) — validation failed
- `unauthorized` (401) — missing/invalid token
- `forbidden` (403) — token valid, but no access to resource
- `document_not_found` (404) — doc/org not found
- `conflict` (409) — version mismatch (optimistic locking)
- `rate_limited` (429) — quota exceeded
- `internal_error` (500) — unhandled

**Why structured:** Machine-readable code decouples error handling from message text. `request_id` enables support triage. `details` provides context without parsing messages.

---

## Document Schema

```json
{
  "id": "doc_6z8k9j",
  "org_id": "org_abc123",
  "collection_id": "col_xyz",
  "name": "Contract v3",
  "content": "...",
  "content_type": "text/plain",
  "size_bytes": 15234,
  "created_at": "2026-11-15T10:30:00Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-11-19T14:22:15Z",
  "version": 5,
  "created_by": "user_123",
  "updated_by": "user_456",
  "tags": ["legal", "2026"],
  "metadata": {"custom_field": "value"},
  "is_deleted": false
}
```

**Why not nested collection:** Collections are queryable separately; documents reference by ID, not nesting. Avoids large N+1 problems and allows collection deletion without cascading horror.

**Soft delete (`is_deleted`):** Allows recovery, audit trails. Hard delete at retention boundary.

---

## Write Operations & Idempotency

```
PATCH /orgs/{org_id}/documents/{doc_id}

{
  "name": "new name",
  "content": "...",
  "version": 5,
  "idempotency_key": "request_abc123"
}
```

- `version` field: optimistic locking. If current != provided, return 409 conflict. Client retries GET + PATCH.
- `idempotency_key`: UUID on client side. Server deduplicates within 24h window.

**Why both:** Version prevents silent overwrites (concurrent edits). Idempotency key prevents duplicate writes on network retries. Together: safe concurrent updates + network resilience.

---

## Tenant Isolation Checklist

- ✓ Tenant ID in URL path (routing boundary)
- ✓ Tenant ID in auth token (validation boundary)
- ✓ Validate mismatch on every request
- ✓ Database indexes on (org_id, resource_id) to prevent full-table scans leaking data
- ✓ Logs include org_id, strip user PII
- ✓ Soft deletes preserve audit trail per org
- ✓ Backups isolated per tenant if possible

---

**Decision summary:** Path-based tenant routing + token-bound auth ensures isolation at routing + validation layers. Cursor pagination survives mutations. Accept-header versioning keeps URLs clean. Structured errors enable robust clients. Optimistic locking + idempotency keys make writes safe under concurrency and network failures.
~~~

# Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API

## Resource Structure

```
POST   /v1/documents                 Create document
GET    /v1/documents                 List documents (paginated)
GET    /v1/documents/{id}            Fetch document
PUT    /v1/documents/{id}            Update document
DELETE /v1/documents/{id}            Soft delete
GET    /v1/documents/{id}/versions   List versions
GET    /v1/documents/{id}/versions/{version}  Fetch version
POST   /v1/documents/{id}/restore    Restore from version

POST   /v1/folders                   Create folder
GET    /v1/folders/{id}/documents    List documents in folder

POST   /v1/shares                    Grant access
GET    /v1/shares?resource_id={id}   List who has access
DELETE /v1/shares/{id}               Revoke access
```

Document shape: `{ id, tenant_id, title, content, folder_id, created_at, updated_at, version, created_by, metadata }`

## Authentication & Tenant Isolation

**Strategy:** Bearer token (JWT) + tenant header (defense-in-depth).

```
Authorization: Bearer {jwt_token}
X-Tenant-ID: {tenant_uuid}
```

JWT payload includes tenant_id + user_id; every request verifies tenant_id in token matches X-Tenant-ID header. Prevents token reuse across tenants. Tokens scoped to: `documents:read`, `documents:write`, `documents:delete`, `shares:manage`.

API key auth for service-to-service: `Authorization: ApiKey {key}` with tenant bound at key creation.

**Why:** JWT alone isn't enough—token compromise could affect wrong tenant if attacker guesses tenant ID. Header forces explicit routing. Scoped permissions limit blast radius of leaked tokens.

## Pagination

Cursor-based for consistency and performance:

```json
GET /v1/documents?limit=20&cursor=eyJpZCI6IjU2YjQxOTExODVmNzY0OTYwMDA1MDczYSJ9

Response:
{
  "data": [...],
  "pagination": {
    "cursor": "eyJpZCI6IjU2YjQxOTExODVmNzY0OTYwMDA1MDczYSJ9",
    "has_more": true,
    "limit": 20
  }
}
```

Cursor is base64-encoded JSON of sort key (id, created_at) to handle deletions and concurrent writes. Offset-based is simpler but broken if documents deleted between requests. Max limit enforced: `limit = min(requested, 100)`.

**Why:** Offset skips rows that were deleted upstream → gaps. Cursor is immutable—next page always consistent. Scales to billions of documents without expensive COUNT queries.

## Document Versioning

Auto-versioned on every write; versions immutable:

```json
PUT /v1/documents/doc123
{
  "title": "Updated",
  "content": "new content"
}

Response headers:
X-Document-Version: 3
```

Metadata on each doc: `{ version: 3, previous_version: 2 }`. Restore to v2 with `POST /v1/documents/doc123/restore { version: 2 }`. Versions pruned after 90 days (configurable per tenant).

**Why:** Automatic, not manual, so users can't accidentally skip. Immutability prevents audit log tampering. TTL garbage collects old versions; keeps storage bounded.

## API Versioning

URL path: `/v1/`, `/v2/` for breaking changes. Additive changes (new fields, new endpoints) don't bump version. Deprecation header warns clients 6 months before EOL:

```
Deprecation: true
Sunset: Wed, 21 May 2027 07:00:00 GMT
Link: <https://docs.example.com/migration>; rel="deprecation"
```

**Why:** URL path is discoverable and forces explicit migration. Header gives tooling hooks. Additive compat keeps /v1 stable for existing clients.

## Error Format

All errors (4xx/5xx) return:

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND",
    "message": "Document doc123 not found in tenant acme-org",
    "status": 404,
    "request_id": "req_1a2b3c4d5e6f",
    "timestamp": "2026-11-19T14:30:00Z",
    "details": {
      "tenant_id": "acme-org",
      "resource_id": "doc123"
    }
  }
}
```

Standard codes: `DOCUMENT_NOT_FOUND`, `UNAUTHORIZED`, `FORBIDDEN` (tenant mismatch), `INVALID_REQUEST`, `CONFLICT` (version mismatch on update), `RATE_LIMITED`, `INTERNAL_ERROR`. request_id spans all services for multi-tenant debugging.

**Why:** Structured errors let clients handle programmatically, not by parsing messages. request_id enables tracing a single tenant's issue across logs without exposing other tenants' data. Details field contextualizes errors (which resource, which tenant).

---

## Key Decisions

**Soft delete + versions:** Hard deletes lose audit trail; soft delete + version history satisfies compliance (GDPR right-to-be-forgotten handled at app layer with explicit purge endpoint, separate from soft delete). Restore op is cheap.

**Cursor pagination:** Offset is fine for small datasets; cursor scales and handles concurrent writes. Tradeoff: cursor less intuitive (clients can't jump to page 50), but that's rare in practice.

**Tenant in token + header:** Defense-in-depth against token leaks. Noise if only one tenant per token, but multi-tenant platforms often issue tokens scoped to org-level resources; header catches mistakes.

**Immutable versions:** Prevents race conditions on update; clients can retry safely. Prune TTL keeps storage predictable—"keep all versions forever" breaks at scale.

**Scoped permissions:** `documents:write` doesn't include `delete`, `delete` doesn't include `shares:manage`. Limits damage if one token is compromised. Add scope on-demand, not all-or-nothing.
~~~

## large-s2 / architecture

> Explain how you would migrate a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite, covering ordering, data ownership, and how to keep it shippable throughout.
