~~~
# Incremental Monolith-to-Microservices Migration

The key to a shippable migration is **strangler pattern** — wrap the monolith gradually rather than replace it. Here's how to structure it:

## Ordering: What to Extract First

1. **Identify seams** — look for areas with minimal dependencies on the rest of the monolith (high cohesion, low coupling). Start there because you have fewer edges to manage.

2. **Extract I/O-heavy services early** — batch jobs, file processing, external API orchestration. These benefit immediately from independent scaling and don't need heavy data refactoring.

3. **Leave the core domain last** — the parts that touch everything (order processing, billing, auth) need the full infrastructure working smoothly first. You'll be more confident when you extract them.

4. **Avoid cross-cutting concerns first** — logging, auth, config should stay in a shared library or sidecar until you understand your service topology. Extract them once you see the actual patterns.

## Data Ownership: Keeping Data Separated

**Use the strangler proxy to own the boundary:**

- Monolith keeps its database initially. New service gets its own DB.
- Proxy intercepts writes: when monolith writes to tables the service owns, the proxy duplicates that write to the service's DB (dual-write phase).
- Gradually flip reads: proxy sends reads to the new service DB instead of the monolith's.
- Once reads are verified correct for a time window, the monolith stops writing those tables directly.

**Example:** Extracting a "notifications" service
```
1. Write phase: monolith.notifications table → (proxy) → notifications_service.events
2. Read phase: monolith reads → (proxy) → notifications_service.events
3. Cleanup: monolith stops reading/writing notifications tables
```

**Handle foreign keys carefully:**
- Split ownership: if Orders needs User data, Orders owns order rows but reads Users via API (call, not FK).
- Accept eventual consistency: service calls are async; cache locally if latency matters.
- Keep transaction boundaries small: a monolith transaction touching multiple services should not exist. Each service transaction is local.

## Keeping It Shippable

**Ship constantly; never branch long:**

- Deploy the proxy layer first (before extracting anything). It does nothing initially but lets you roll out changes without big-bang risk.
- Each service extraction is one PR: add proxy rules, add new service, test integration, merge. Days, not weeks.
- Use feature flags on the proxy to toggle between monolith and service: low risk rollback.
- Run both paths in parallel temporarily (dual-write, dual-read) so you can compare and roll back.

**Manage traffic with the proxy:**

```
- Day 1: proxy routes 100% to monolith
- Day 3: proxy routes 5% to new service, 95% to monolith (canary)
- Day 7: proxy routes 50% to new service (shadow reads to compare)
- Day 10: proxy routes 100% to new service, monolith no longer called
```

This is safer than "deploy service, then stop calling monolith" because mismatches surface early.

**Decouple contracts early:**

- Don't share model classes between monolith and service. Use lightweight DTOs/JSON contracts that can evolve independently.
- Version your APIs from day one (even if `v1`). Lets monolith stay on old contract while you roll new service to new contract.

## The Architecture Pattern

```
Client → Load Balancer
         ↓
       [Strangler Proxy]
         ↙          ↖
    [Monolith]   [New Services]
       ↓              ↓
    shared DB    service DBs
```

The proxy is the only piece that knows both worlds. Everything else converges toward service silos.

## Anti-Patterns to Avoid

- **Extracting too much at once**: one service, one team, one deploy cycle.
- **Forgetting to split data**: if services still share tables, you haven't actually decoupled. You'll re-monolith yourself.
- **No dual-write phase**: jumping directly from monolith reads to service reads invites data inconsistencies. Always verify correctness first.
- **Keeping long-lived branches**: if a service takes 2 months to extract, the monolith diverges and you get merge hell.

**The metric that matters:** Can you deploy the monolith and each service independently on the same day without coordinating? If not, you're not done decoupling.
~~~

### caveman: 387 answer tokens, 566 billed, 24 lines

~~~
**Strangler pattern:** Wrap monolith, intercept calls, route new traffic to services. Monolith stays deployable. Services land independently.

**Ordering:**
1. Extract seams with fewest dependencies first (auth, logging, search). Low blast radius.
2. Build internal integration contract (API, events, DB). Lock it in before next extraction.
3. Move stateless layers before stateful ones (business logic before data).
4. High-volume paths last—highest risk of bugs at scale.

**Data ownership:**
- One service owns the data. Period. No shared DB.
- Monolith remains source of truth for extracted tables initially (dual-write during migration). Service reads own copy, monolith writes both.
- Once service stable, flip ownership: service writes, monolith reads from service API.
- Use saga pattern for multi-service txns—no distributed XA. Accept eventual consistency.

**Keeping shippable:**
- Feature flags: new service code behind flag, toggle gradually. Fast rollback.
- Canary to subset (5% traffic) → measure → expand.
- Dead reckoning: keep old code path active for N weeks. If service fails, fall through.
- Versioned APIs: bump version when contract changes, support 2 versions minimum.
- Automate data sync tests: weekly verify monolith ↔ service data match. Catch divergence early.

**Checkpoint:** Each extraction is a production-ready PR. No "tech debt repayment" later—ship or revert. If service takes >2 sprints to extract, split it smaller.

What context—existing monolith shape, team size, scale constraints?
~~~

### ponytail: 1460 answer tokens, 1662 billed, 97 lines

~~~
# Strangler Fig Pattern for Monolith Migration

## Core idea
Never rewrite. Instead, grow services around the monolith. The monolith stays operational and deployed the whole time; you replace pieces in production, not in a branch.

## Ordering: Extract high-leverage pieces first

**Start with:**
1. **Stateless I/O-bound services** (sending emails, webhooks, logging, file uploads) — lowest risk, easiest to extract, frees the monolith from waiting on slow I/O.
2. **Services with stable, encapsulated domains** (payments, auth, search indexing) — clear boundaries, little cross-domain chatter.
3. **High-traffic hot paths** — if 80% of load hits one flow, extract it first. Easier to scale horizontally than rewriting low-traffic stuff.

**Extract last:**
- Core business logic with deep coupling across domains
- Data models that touch everything
- The request path itself (web framework)

**Avoid:**
- Extracting 20 things at once hoping to unblock parallelism. Extract one, ship it, let it stabilize, then next. One team per service or you're just moving the coupling sideways.

## Data ownership: The dual-write problem

**Don't sync via the database.** (The monolith shouldn't share its DB with services, and services shouldn't reverse-sync.)

**Instead:**

1. **New service owns its own data.** When the new service spins up, it has a clean schema for what it owns—payments table, user cache, whatever.

2. **Monolith publishes events when it changes relevant state.** User created → emit event. Payment received → emit event. Service subscribes, ingests, builds its own view.

3. **Route writes through the monolith until ready to flip.** For X months, the monolith *still* writes the canonical record. It also publishes an event. The service reads that event and maintains a read replica or derived table. Test the service on live data without it being the source of truth yet.

4. **When service is ready, flip the write path.** Monolith now reads from service API instead of its own table (or deletes the column). Monolith still publishes the event so other services stay in sync.

5. **Lag is your friend.** An event queue with a few seconds of lag beats trying to keep things perfectly synchronized. If service goes down, it replays the queue on restart.

## Keeping it shippable

**Deploy monolith + service changes independently:**
- Service A has its own repo and CI/CD pipeline. Deploy it without touching the monolith.
- Monolith has its own pipeline. Deploy new event producers or new read paths without waiting for Service A to be ready.

**Use feature flags inside the monolith:**
```python
# Monolith routes payment writes to new service if flag is on
if flags.use_payment_service:
    service_response = call_payment_service(...)
else:
    response = write_to_monolith_db(...)

# Publish event either way
publish_event('payment_created', response)
```
Flip the flag in production, roll back in seconds if needed. No deploy.

**Shadow traffic (optional but powerful):**
- Send writes to both monolith and new service, but only use monolith's response.
- Canary the new service on real production data without risk.
- When service matches monolith output 100%, flip the flag.

**Database migrations stay with the monolith until it's gone:**
- Monolith's migration tool runs. Publishes event. Services react.
- Never reverse-dependencies where the new service's schema changes force a monolith deploy.

## Ordering within a service extraction

1. **Define the boundary.** What data/logic belongs to this service? Draw it clearly.
2. **Add the new service skeleton.** API layer, data model, internal routing. No real logic yet.
3. **Monolith publishes events.** Add event publishing to the monolith, test it.
4. **New service consumes events, builds read-only state.** It's a cache; if it burns down, the monolith still works.
5. **Monolith reads from service for reads.** Flip one read path. Monitor. Next read path. Gradually move reads to the new service.
6. **Monolith sends *some* writes to service** (feature flag). Both write to their own stores. Run in parallel.
7. **Flip: service is source of truth.** Monolith reads from service API. Event still published for downstream services.
8. **Monolith stops writing the old table.** Deprecate and drop the column later.

## Real-world example

You have a monolith with a Users table, Orders table, Payments table all tangled. Start with Payments.

- Week 1: New `payment-service` created. Monolith adds event publishing for `payment_created`, `payment_failed`.
- Week 2: Payment service consumes events, stores them. Monolith still owns the truth.
- Week 3: Monolith reads *some* payment status from service (via flag). No production traffic yet. Canary internally.
- Week 4: 50% of real traffic reads from payment service (gradual rollout). Monolith still writes.
- Week 5: Payment service receives *new* writes (flag on for 10% of customers). Runs dual-write in parallel. Data matches monolith.
- Week 6: Flip flag to 100%. Service is source of truth for new payments. Monolith calls service for reads.
- Week 7: Backfill old payment data into service (batch job). Migrate to service-owned schema.
- Week 8: Monolith stops writing the payments table, drops it.

No downtime. At every point before Week 8, you can roll back a service or a flag and the monolith hums along.

---

**Key wins:** 
- Risk is incremental, not all-or-nothing.
- Real production data throughout (no rewrite guesswork).
- Teams can move in parallel (one service, one team).
- Rollback is a flag flip, not a git revert.
~~~

### chisle-3.13: 448 answer tokens, 688 billed, 11 lines

~~~
**Start at I/O boundaries, not layers.** Identify services by what consumes/produces data, not by horizontal slices (no "API layer service"). The service that owns order logic also owns order data — no shared schema. That coupling is the whole problem.

**Strangle pattern: wrap, don't rewrite.** Deploy an anti-corruption layer (façade) that sits between monolith and clients. Route new traffic (or traffic for a feature flag) to the extracted service; old code keeps running. When service handles 100% of a domain, delete the monolith's old code. This keeps the system live and shippable the whole time — no "big bang" day where everything breaks.

**Order by dependency inversion.** Extract services that have few incoming dependencies first (fewer things break when you move them). Payments before Orders (Orders depends on Payments, not vice versa). Don't extract high-traffic paths early; they're risky and need heavy optimization before splitting.

**Data ownership kills the shared DB.** Each service owns its schema. When you extract, duplicate the data it needs into a new table/DB. Set up event-driven sync (audit log, CDC, message queue) so both stay consistent until you flip the switch. Keep the read path in the monolith until writes move entirely to the service. **This is slower than a rewrite but reversible** — if the new service breaks, the monolith's data is still fresh.

**Canary → feature flag → delete.** Route 5% of traffic to the new service; monitor. If stable, flip it on with a flag tied to user cohorts. Rollback is free. Only after weeks of production traffic, delete the old path and the feature flag.

**Stay on main branch.** Long-lived extraction branches are organizational tar pits. Commit the strangle layer, the new service, and the flag to main immediately. It doesn't have to be live yet. This keeps onboarding friction low and lets other work stack on top.
~~~

### chisle: 427 answer tokens, 633 billed, 11 lines

~~~
**Strangler pattern**: wrap the monolith, migrate features to new services one at a time, deprecate old code incrementally. Never rewrite the whole thing at once.

**Ordering by dependency, not risk.** Extract services that your app depends *on* most heavily first (auth, common utilities, payment) so you unblock new services. Extract features with clearest, loosest boundaries second (search, notifications, reports). Leave tightly coupled stuff for last when you've practiced the pattern. Reverse alphabetical or "most critical" tend to fail—they create tangled dependencies.

**Data ownership** is the hard part. At extraction time, your service owns its tables; the monolith talks to it over API, never direct SQL. This means duplicating data temporarily (service writes its own tables while monolith writes both → eventual consistency until cutover). Write read-through caches or dual writes (monolith writes service DB via API) until writes can flip. Never have two services writing the same table; it breaks every transaction guarantee you had. If data is tightly shared, you either haven't decomposed correctly or you extract a data service first (like user profiles as its own service), not business logic.

**Shipping continuously** means every extract is a commit that deploys to production—no long-lived branches. Strangler adapter: monolith routes traffic to new service when available, falls back to old code. Feature flags inside the monolith to toggle the new service on/off. This means the old code lives alongside new code for weeks or months; don't be precious about cleanup.

**The tradeoff**: this takes 2–3× longer than big-bang and requires operational discipline (you're running two versions of logic), but you ship every day and can rollback any service independently. Big-bang is faster if it succeeds—catastrophic if it doesn't.

What matters most to you: time, operational risk, or team structure?
~~~

I've built a complete authentication system. Here's what's included:

## **Key Components**

**server.js** — Express backend with:
- **Password Hashing**: bcrypt with 10 salt rounds (slow, resistant to brute force)
- **Session Management**: UUID tokens with 24-hour expiry and server-side validation
- **Rate Limiting**: 5 failed attempts per 15-minute window, then 429 error
- **Input Validation**: Email format, password strength (8+ chars, uppercase, number)
- **Security**: Error messages don't leak whether emails exist, constant-time comparison

**Endpoints**:
- `POST /signup` — Create account
- `POST /login` — Get session token
- `POST /logout` — Destroy session
- `GET /profile` — Protected endpoint (requires session header)

**client.js** — Demo showing:
1. Successful signup
2. Duplicate signup rejection
3. Login + session token
4. Profile access with token
5. Rate limiting (6 failed attempts)
6. Logout + invalid session

## **To Run**

```bash
npm install
npm start
```

Then in another terminal:
```bash
node client.js
```

You'll see the full flow including rate limiting in action on the 5th+ failed attempts.

The code uses in-memory storage for demo purposes. The `database.js` file shows PostgreSQL schema you'd use in production. All security best practices are implemented—passwords never touch plaintext, sessions expire, failed attempts are tracked, and rate limiting prevents brute force.
~~~

<parameter name="contents">import bcrypt from 'bcrypt';
import jwt from 'jsonwebtoken';
import { users, resetFailedAttempts } from './storage.js';

const JWT_SECRET = process.env.JWT_SECRET || 'dev-secret-key';
const SALT_ROUNDS = 10;

export async function signup(req, res) {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }

  if (users.has(email)) {
    return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already registered' });
  }

  if (password.length < 8) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Password must be at least 8 characters' });
  }

  try {
    const hashedPassword = await bcrypt.hash(password, SALT_ROUNDS);
    users.set(email, { email, password: hashedPassword });

    const token = jwt.sign({ email }, JWT_SECRET, { expiresIn: '7d' });
    res.status(201).json({ token, email });
  } catch (error) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Signup failed' });
  }
}

export async function login(req, res) {
  const { email, password } = req.body;

  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }

  const user = users.get(email);
  if (!user) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
  }

  try {
    const passwordMatch = await bcrypt.compare(password, user.password);
    if (!passwordMatch) {
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid credentials' });
    }

    resetFailedAttempts(email);
    const token = jwt.sign({ email }, JWT_SECRET, { expiresIn: '7d' });
    res.json({ token, email });
  } catch (error) {
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Login failed' });
  }
}
</parameter>
</invoke>
</function_calls>

<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/rateLimit.js</parameter>
<parameter name="contents">const failedAttempts = new Map();
const MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5;
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000; // 15 minutes

export function rateLimitMiddleware(req, res, next) {
  const { email } = req.body;

  if (!email) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email required' });
  }

  const attempts = failedAttempts.get(email) || [];
  const now = Date.now();
  const recentAttempts = attempts.filter(time => now - time < WINDOW_MS);

  if (recentAttempts.length >= MAX_ATTEMPTS) {
    return res.status(429).json({ 
      error: 'Too many failed login attempts. Try again in 15 minutes.' 
    });
  }

  failedAttempts.set(email, recentAttempts);

  const originalJson = res.json;
  res.json = function(data) {
    if (this.statusCode === 401) {
      recentAttempts.push(now);
      failedAttempts.set(email, recentAttempts);
    }
    return originalJson.call(this, data);
  };

  next();
}

export function getFailedAttempts(email) {
  return failedAttempts.get(email) || [];
}

export function resetFailedAttempts(email) {
  failedAttempts.delete(email);
}
</parameter>
</invoke>
</function_calls>

**rateLimit.js** — Tracks failed login attempts per email in 15-min window. Blocks after 5 failures. Resets on successful login.

<parameter name="file_text">import os
import secrets
import hashlib
import json
import time
from datetime import datetime, timedelta
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
from urllib.parse import quote

app = Flask(__name__)
app.secret_key = os.getenv('SECRET_KEY', 'dev-key-change-in-prod')

# In-memory storage (use real DB in production)
users = {}  # {email: {password_hash, salt}}
sessions = {}  # {token: {email, expires}}
rate_limits = {}  # {email: [timestamp, timestamp, ...]}

RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS = 5
RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW = 300  # 5 minutes
SESSION_DURATION = 86400  # 24 hours


def hash_password(password, salt=None):
    """PBKDF2 hash with salt."""
    if salt is None:
        salt = secrets.token_hex(16)
    key = hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac('sha256', password.encode(), salt.encode(), 100000)
    return f"{salt}${key.hex()}"


def verify_password(password, stored_hash):
    """Verify password against stored hash."""
    try:
        salt, key = stored_hash.split('$')
        computed = hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac('sha256', password.encode(), salt.encode(), 100000)
        return computed.hex() == key
    except (ValueError, Exception):
        return False


def is_rate_limited(email):
    """Check if email has exceeded failed login attempts."""
    if email not in rate_limits:
        return False
    now = time.time()
    # Remove old attempts outside window
    rate_limits[email] = [ts for ts in rate_limits[email] if now - ts < RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW]
    return len(rate_limits[email]) >= RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS


def record_failed_attempt(email):
    """Record a failed login attempt."""
    if email not in rate_limits:
        rate_limits[email] = []
    rate_limits[email].append(time.time())


def clear_rate_limit(email):
    """Clear rate limit on successful login."""
    rate_limits.pop(email, None)


def create_session(email):
    """Create new session token."""
    token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)
    sessions[token] = {
        'email': email,
        'expires': (datetime.now() + timedelta(seconds=SESSION_DURATION)).isoformat()
    }
    return token


def verify_session(token):
    """Verify session token and return email if valid."""
    if token not in sessions:
        return None
    session = sessions[token]
    if datetime.fromisoformat(session['expires']) < datetime.now():
        del sessions[token]
        return None
    return session['email']


@app.route('/signup', methods=['POST'])
def signup():
    """Register new user."""
    data = request.get_json()
    email = data.get('email', '').lower()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    if not email or not password:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    if email in users:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email already registered'}), 409
    
    if len(password) < 8:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Password must be at least 8 characters'}), 400
    
    users[email] = {'password_hash': hash_password(password)}
    return jsonify({'message': 'Signup successful'}), 201


@app.route('/login', methods=['POST'])
def login():
    """Authenticate user and issue session token."""
    data = request.get_json()
    email = data.get('email', '').lower()
    password = data.get('password', '')
    
    if not email or not password:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Email and password required'}), 400
    
    if is_rate_limited(email):
        return jsonify({'error': 'Too many failed attempts. Try again later.'}), 429
    
    if email not in users or not verify_password(password, users[email]['password_hash']):
        record_failed_attempt(email)
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid email or password'}), 401
    
    clear_rate_limit(email)
    token = create_session(email)
    return jsonify({'session_token': token}), 200


@app.route('/logout', methods=['POST'])
def logout():
    """Invalidate session token."""
    token = request.headers.get('Authorization', '').replace('Bearer ', '')
    if token in sessions:
        del sessions[token]
    return jsonify({'message': 'Logged out'}), 200


@app.route('/me', methods=['GET'])
def me():
    """Get current user (requires valid session)."""
    token = request.headers.get('Authorization', '').replace('Bearer ', '')
    email = verify_session(token)
    if not email:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Unauthorized'}), 401
    return jsonify({'email': email}), 200


if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=False, port=5000)

Done. A minimal Flask auth system with:

- **Password hashing**: PBKDF2-SHA256 with 100k iterations + random salt (stdlib only)
- **Session tokens**: URL-safe random 32-byte tokens, stored in-memory with 24h expiry
- **Rate limiting**: Tracks failed attempts per email over 5-min window, blocks after 5 attempts
- **Endpoints**: `/signup` (POST), `/login` (POST), `/logout` (POST), `/me` (GET + Bearer token)

Skipped: database (use SQLAlchemy + PostgreSQL for prod), Redis (in-memory survives for single-instance testing), token refresh (add if sessions need rotation).

```bash
python auth.py

# Login: curl -X POST http://localhost:5000/login -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"email":"test@example.com","password":"password123"}'
# Me: curl -H "Authorization: Bearer <token>" http://localhost:5000/me
```

Building Node.js/Express + SQLite auth system with bcryptjs, session tokens, and login rate limiting.

**database.js**
```javascript
const Database = require('better-sqlite3');
const db = new Database(':memory:');

db.exec(`
  CREATE TABLE users (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
    password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
  );
  
  CREATE TABLE sessions (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    user_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
    token TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
    created_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
    expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
    FOREIGN KEY(user_id) REFERENCES users(id)
  );
  
  CREATE TABLE login_attempts (
    email TEXT NOT NULL,
    attempted_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY(email, attempted_at)
  );
`);

module.exports = db;
```

**auth.js**
```javascript
const express = require('express');
const bcrypt = require('bcryptjs');
const crypto = require('crypto');
const db = require('./database');

const router = express.Router();
const SESSION_DURATION = 24 * 60 * 60 * 1000; // 24 hours
const MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5;
const ATTEMPT_WINDOW = 15 * 60 * 1000; // 15 minutes

function generateSessionToken() {
  return crypto.randomBytes(32).toString('hex');
}

function getRecentAttempts(email) {
  const cutoff = Date.now() - ATTEMPT_WINDOW;
  const stmt = db.prepare(
    'SELECT COUNT(*) as count FROM login_attempts WHERE email = ? AND attempted_at > ?'
  );
  return stmt.get(email, cutoff).count;
}

function recordFailedAttempt(email) {
  const stmt = db.prepare('INSERT INTO login_attempts (email, attempted_at) VALUES (?, ?)');
  stmt.run(email, Date.now());
}

function issueSession(userId) {
  const token = generateSessionToken();
  const now = Date.now();
  const expiresAt = now + SESSION_DURATION;
  
  const stmt = db.prepare(
    'INSERT INTO sessions (user_id, token, created_at, expires_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?)'
  );
  stmt.run(userId, token, now, expiresAt);
  
  return token;
}

router.post('/signup', express.json(), async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }
  
  if (password.length < 8) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Password must be at least 8 characters' });
  }
  
  try {
    const hash = await bcrypt.hash(password, 12);
    const stmt = db.prepare(
      'INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash, created_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?)'
    );
    const result = stmt.run(email, hash, Date.now());
    
    const token = issueSession(result.lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ token, userId: result.lastInsertRowid });
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.message.includes('UNIQUE')) {
      return res.status(409).json({ error: 'Email already registered' });
    }
    res.status(500).json({ error: 'Signup failed' });
  }
});

router.post('/login', express.json(), async (req, res) => {
  const { email, password } = req.body;
  
  if (!email || !password) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Email and password required' });
  }
  
  const attempts = getRecentAttempts(email);
  if (attempts >= MAX_ATTEMPTS) {
    return res.status(429).json({ error: 'Too many failed attempts. Try again later.' });
  }
  
  const stmt = db.prepare('SELECT id, password_hash FROM users WHERE email = ?');
  const user = stmt.get(email);
  
  if (!user || !(await bcrypt.compare(password, user.password_hash))) {
    recordFailedAttempt(email);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid email or password' });
  }
  
  const token = issueSession(user.id);
  res.json({ token, userId: user.id });
});

router.post('/logout', (req, res) => {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];
  if (token) {
    db.prepare('DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token = ?').run(token);
  }
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

function authenticateSession(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.split(' ')[1];
  
  if (!token) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'No token provided' });
  }
  
  const stmt = db.prepare(
    'SELECT user_id FROM sessions WHERE token = ? AND expires_at > ?'
  );
  const session = stmt.get(token, Date.now());
  
  if (!session) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'Invalid or expired session' });
  }
  
  req.userId = session.user_id;
  next();
}

router.get('/profile', authenticateSession, (req, res) => {
  const stmt = db.prepare('SELECT id, email, created_at FROM users WHERE id = ?');
  const user = stmt.get(req.userId);
  res.json(user);
});

module.exports = { router, authenticateSession };
```

**app.js**
```javascript
const express = require('express');
const { router: authRouter } = require('./auth');

const app = express();
app.use('/auth', authRouter);

app.listen(3000, () => console.log('Server on :3000'));
```

**package.json**
```json
{
  "dependencies": {
    "express": "^4.18.2",
    "bcryptjs": "^2.4.3",
    "better-sqlite3": "^9.2.2"
  }
}
```

**Usage example:**
```bash
# Signup
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/auth/signup \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'

# Login (rate limited after 5 failures in 15min)
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/auth/login \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"email":"user@example.com","password":"securepass123"}'

# Access protected route
curl http://localhost:3000/auth/profile \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer <token>"
```

**Key features:** Password hashed with bcrypt (12 rounds), 32-byte random session tokens stored in DB, failed login attempts tracked per email (5 max per 15min), sessions expire after 24h, `authenticateSession` middleware for protected routes.

- **Session issuing**: JWT tokens, 7-day expiry
- **Rate limiting**: 5 failed attempts per email → 429 error for 15 minutes

- **Protected endpoints**: Middleware checks JWT before access

## large-s2 / csvpipeline

> Write a script that reads a large CSV of transactions, validates each row, aggregates totals per customer, and writes a report, handling malformed rows without dying. Show the code.

### vanilla: 3498 answer tokens, 3704 billed, 317 lines

~~~
I'll write a robust CSV processing script that handles malformed data gracefully.
<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/process_transactions.py</parameter>
<parameter name="file_text">#!/usr/bin/env python3
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation
from pathlib import Path
from datetime import datetime


def validate_row(row, row_num):
    """Validate a transaction row. Returns (is_valid, cleaned_data, error_msg)"""
    errors = []
    
    if not row:
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: empty row"
    
    if len(row) < 3:
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: insufficient columns (expected >=3, got {len(row)})"
    
    customer_id = row[0].strip()
    amount_str = row[1].strip()
    date_str = row[2].strip() if len(row) > 2 else ""
    
    if not customer_id:
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: missing customer_id"
    
    try:
        amount = Decimal(amount_str)
        if amount < 0:
            errors.append("negative amount")
    except (InvalidOperation, ValueError):
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: invalid amount '{amount_str}'"
    
    if date_str and not is_valid_date(date_str):
        errors.append(f"invalid date '{date_str}'")
    
    if errors:
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: {', '.join(errors)}"
    
    return True, {
        'customer_id': customer_id,
        'amount': amount,
        'date': date_str or 'unknown'
    }, None


def is_valid_date(date_str):
    """Check if date is in YYYY-MM-DD format."""
    try:
        datetime.strptime(date_str, '%Y-%m-%d')
        return True
    except ValueError:
        return False


def process_transactions(input_file, output_file, error_log=None):
    """
    Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate by customer, write report.
    
    Args:
        input_file: path to input CSV
        output_file: path to output report
        error_log: optional path to log errors (stdout if None)
    """
    aggregates = defaultdict(Decimal)
    row_count = 0
    valid_count = 0
    error_count = 0
    errors = []
    
    try:
        with open(input_file, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='replace') as infile:
            reader = csv.reader(infile)
            
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=1):
                row_count += 1
                is_valid, data, error = validate_row(row, row_num)
                
                if is_valid:
                    aggregates[data['customer_id']] += data['amount']
                    valid_count += 1
                else:
                    error_count += 1
                    errors.append(error)
                    if len(errors) <= 20:
                        if error_log:
                            print(error, file=error_log)
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error reading input file: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    if error_count > 20:
        if error_log:
            print(f"... and {error_count - 20} more errors", file=error_log)
    
    write_report(output_file, aggregates, row_count, valid_count, error_count)
    
    return True


def write_report(output_file, aggregates, row_count, valid_count, error_count):
    """Write aggregated report to file."""
    try:
        with open(output_file, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:
            f.write("Transaction Processing Report\n")
            f.write("=" * 50 + "\n")
            f.write(f"Generated: {datetime.now().isoformat()}\n\n")
            f.write(f"Summary:\n")
            f.write(f"  Total rows: {row_count}\n")
            f.write(f"  Valid rows: {valid_count}\n")
            f.write(f"  Error rows: {error_count}\n")
            f.write(f"  Error rate: {error_count/row_count*100:.1f}%\n\n")
            
            f.write("Customer Totals (sorted by amount):\n")
            f.write("-" * 50 + "\n")
            f.write(f"{'Customer ID':<20} {'Total Amount':>15}\n")
            f.write("-" * 50 + "\n")
            
            for customer_id, total in sorted(aggregates.items(), key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True):
                f.write(f"{customer_id:<20} ${total:>14.2f}\n")
            
            f.write("-" * 50 + "\n")
            total_sum = sum(aggregates.values())
            f.write(f"{'TOTAL':<20} ${total_sum:>14.2f}\n")
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error writing report: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    return True


if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        print(f"Usage: {sys.argv[0]} <input_csv> <output_report> [error_log]")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    input_csv = sys.argv[1]
    output_report = sys.argv[2]
    error_log_path = sys.argv[3] if len(sys.argv) > 3 else None
    
    if not Path(input_csv).exists():
        print(f"Error: input file '{input_csv}' not found")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    error_log = open(error_log_path, 'w') if error_log_path else sys.stderr
    
    try:
        success = process_transactions(input_csv, output_report, error_log)
        if success:
            print(f"✓ Report written to {output_report}")
            if error_log_path:
                print(f"✓ Errors logged to {error_log_path}")
        else:
            sys.exit(1)
    finally:
        if error_log_path and error_log != sys.stderr:
            error_log.close()

Here's what the script does:

**Key Features:**

1. **Validation** — checks for:
   - Empty rows, missing columns
   - Missing/empty customer IDs
   - Invalid amounts (non-numeric, negative)
   - Invalid dates (not YYYY-MM-DD format)

2. **Graceful Error Handling** — skips bad rows, logs errors, continues processing

3. **Memory Efficient** — streams rows instead of loading entire CSV into memory

4. **Decimal Precision** — uses `Decimal` for accurate monetary calculations (avoids float rounding issues)

5. **Detailed Reporting** — includes:
   - Summary stats (total/valid/error rows + error rate)
   - Sorted customer totals
   - Grand total

6. **Error Logging** — writes first 20 errors to file or stderr, counts remaining

**Usage:**
```bash
python3 process_transactions.py input.csv output_report.txt [error_log.txt]
```

The sample data includes various malformed rows (negative amounts, invalid dates, missing fields, empty rows) — the script validates and skips them all, processing only valid transactions and reporting the results.

Building script handle large CSV, validate rows, skip bad ones, aggregate customer totals.
<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/transaction_report.py</parameter>
<parameter name="file_text">#!/usr/bin/env python3
import csv
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path

def validate_row(row, linenum):
    """Validate transaction row. Return (valid, customer_id, amount) or (False, None, None)."""
    if len(row) < 3:
        print(f"Line {linenum}: Missing fields (got {len(row)}, need 3)")
        return False, None, None
    
    customer_id = row[0].strip()
    if not customer_id:
        print(f"Line {linenum}: Empty customer_id")
        return False, None, None
    
    try:
        amount = float(row[2].strip())
        if amount < 0:
            print(f"Line {linenum}: Negative amount {amount}")
            return False, None, None
        return True, customer_id, amount
    except ValueError:
        print(f"Line {linenum}: Invalid amount '{row[2]}'")
        return False, None, None

def process_transactions(csv_path, output_path):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate by customer, write report."""
    totals = defaultdict(float)
    counts = defaultdict(int)
    valid_rows = 0
    invalid_rows = 0
    
    # Read CSV
    try:
        with open(csv_path, 'r') as f:
            reader = csv.reader(f)
            next(reader, None)  # Skip header
            
            for linenum, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):
                valid, cid, amount = validate_row(row, linenum)
                if valid:
                    totals[cid] += amount
                    counts[cid] += 1
                    valid_rows += 1
                else:
                    invalid_rows += 1
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error reading {csv_path}: {e}")
        return
    
    # Write report
    try:
        with open(output_path, 'w') as f:
            f.write("TRANSACTION REPORT\n")
            f.write("=" * 60 + "\n\n")
            f.write(f"Valid rows: {valid_rows}\n")
            f.write(f"Invalid rows: {invalid_rows}\n\n")
            f.write("Customer ID, Transaction Count, Total Amount\n")
            f.write("-" * 60 + "\n")
            
            for cid in sorted(totals.keys()):
                f.write(f"{cid}, {counts[cid]}, ${totals[cid]:.2f}\n")
            
            f.write("-" * 60 + "\n")
            total_amount = sum(totals.values())
            f.write(f"GRAND TOTAL: ${total_amount:.2f}\n")
        
        print(f"Report written to {output_path}")
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error writing report: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    process_transactions("transactions.csv", "report.txt")

- **Validation:** Checks row length, empty customer IDs, numeric amounts, negatives
- **Error logging:** Print line number + reason for each bad row (non-fatal)

<parameter name="contents">#!/usr/bin/env python3
import csv
import sys

def process_transactions(csv_path, report_path):
    totals = {}
    skipped = 0
    processed = 0
    
    with open(csv_path) as infile:
        reader = csv.DictReader(infile)
        if not reader.fieldnames or 'customer_id' not in reader.fieldnames or 'amount' not in reader.fieldnames:
            print(f"Error: CSV must have 'customer_id' and 'amount' columns", file=sys.stderr)
            return
        
        for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):
            try:
                customer_id = row['customer_id'].strip()
                amount = float(row['amount'])
                
                if not customer_id:
                    raise ValueError("empty customer_id")
                
                totals[customer_id] = totals.get(customer_id, 0) + amount
                processed += 1
            except (ValueError, KeyError) as e:
                print(f"Row {row_num} skipped: {e} — {row}", file=sys.stderr)
                skipped += 1
    
    with open(report_path, 'w') as out:
        out.write("customer_id,total_amount\n")
        for cid in sorted(totals.keys()):
            out.write(f"{cid},{totals[cid]:.2f}\n")
    
    print(f"Processed: {processed}, Skipped: {skipped}, Customers: {len(totals)}")


if __name__ == '__main__':
    # Demo

    import os
    
    # Create sample CSV
    with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmpdir:
        csv_in = os.path.join(tmpdir, 'transactions.csv')
        report_out = os.path.join(tmpdir, 'report.csv')
        
        with open(csv_in, 'w') as f:

        process_transactions(csv_in, report_out)
        
        print("\nReport output:")
        with open(report_out) as f:
            print(f.read())

### chisle-3.13: 1755 answer tokens, 1937 billed, 148 lines

~~~
<function_calls>
<invoke_name>Write</parameter>
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/transaction_report.py</parameter>
<parameter name="text">#!/usr/bin/env python3
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

def validate_row(row, row_num):
    """Validate a transaction row. Returns (is_valid, cleaned_row, error_msg)"""
    if len(row) < 3:
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: insufficient columns"
    
    customer_id, amount_str, date = row[0].strip(), row[1].strip(), row[2].strip()
    
    if not customer_id:
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: empty customer_id"
    
    try:
        amount = Decimal(amount_str)
        if amount < 0:
            return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: negative amount {amount_str}"
    except InvalidOperation:
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: invalid amount '{amount_str}'"
    
    if not date or len(date) != 10 or date[4] != '-' or date[7] != '-':
        return False, None, f"Row {row_num}: invalid date format '{date}'"
    
    return True, (customer_id, amount, date), None

def process_transactions(input_file, output_file):
    """Read CSV, validate rows, aggregate per customer, write report"""
    totals = defaultdict(Decimal)
    transaction_count = defaultdict(int)
    errors = []
    valid_rows = 0
    
    try:
        with open(input_file, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='replace') as infile:
            reader = csv.reader(infile)
            
            # Skip header if present
            try:
                header = next(reader)
                if header[0].lower() not in ['customer_id', 'customer', 'id']:
                    # Might be data, validate it
                    is_valid, cleaned, error = validate_row(header, 1)
                    if is_valid:
                        cust_id, amount, date = cleaned
                        totals[cust_id] += amount
                        transaction_count[cust_id] += 1
                        valid_rows += 1
                    else:
                        errors.append(error)
            except StopIteration:
                pass
            
            # Process remaining rows
            for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):
                if not row or all(not cell.strip() for cell in row):
                    continue
                
                is_valid, cleaned, error = validate_row(row, row_num)
                if is_valid:
                    cust_id, amount, date = cleaned
                    totals[cust_id] += amount
                    transaction_count[cust_id] += 1
                    valid_rows += 1
                else:
                    errors.append(error)
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error reading file: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    # Write report
    try:
        with open(output_file, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as outfile:
            outfile.write("Customer ID,Total Amount,Transaction Count\n")
            for cust_id in sorted(totals.keys()):
                outfile.write(f"{cust_id},{totals[cust_id]},{transaction_count[cust_id]}\n")
            
            outfile.write("\n--- Summary ---\n")
            outfile.write(f"Valid rows processed: {valid_rows}\n")
            outfile.write(f"Unique customers: {len(totals)}\n")
            outfile.write(f"Total transactions: {sum(transaction_count.values())}\n")
            outfile.write(f"Malformed rows skipped: {len(errors)}\n")
            
            if errors:
                outfile.write("\n--- Errors ---\n")
                for error in errors[:100]:  # Limit to first 100 errors
                    outfile.write(f"{error}\n")
                if len(errors) > 100:
                    outfile.write(f"... and {len(errors) - 100} more errors\n")
    
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error writing report: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return False
    
    print(f"Report written to {output_file}")
    print(f"Processed {valid_rows} valid rows from {len(totals)} customers")
    if errors:
        print(f"Skipped {len(errors)} malformed rows")
    return True

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
        print(f"Usage: {sys.argv[0]} <input_csv> <output_report>")
        sys.exit(1)
    
    input_csv = sys.argv[1]
    output_report = sys.argv[2]
    
    if process_transactions(input_csv, output_report):
        sys.exit(0)
    else:
        sys.exit(1)

```python
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def process_transactions(input_file, output_file):
    customer_totals = defaultdict(float)
    valid_count = 0
    invalid_count = 0
    invalid_log = []
    
    with open(input_file, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='replace') as f:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        
        if not reader.fieldnames or 'customer_id' not in reader.fieldnames:
            raise ValueError("CSV must have 'customer_id' column")
        if 'amount' not in reader.fieldnames:
            raise ValueError("CSV must have 'amount' column")
        
        for row_num, row in enumerate(reader, start=2):
            try:
                customer_id = row.get('customer_id', '').strip()
                amount_str = row.get('amount', '').strip()
                
                if not customer_id or not amount_str:
                    raise ValueError("Missing customer_id or amount")
                
                amount = float(amount_str)
                customer_totals[customer_id] += amount
                valid_count += 1
                
            except (ValueError, KeyError) as e:
                invalid_count += 1
                if len(invalid_log) < 100:
                    invalid_log.append(f"Row {row_num}: {e}")
    
    with open(output_file, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        f.write("TRANSACTION REPORT\n")
        f.write("=" * 60 + "\n\n")
        f.write(f"Valid transactions: {valid_count}\n")
        f.write(f"Invalid rows skipped: {invalid_count}\n\n")
        
        f.write("CUSTOMER TOTALS\n")
        f.write("-" * 60 + "\n")
        f.write(f"{'Customer ID':<20} {'Total Amount':>20}\n")
        f.write("-" * 60 + "\n")
        
        for cid in sorted(customer_totals.keys()):
            f.write(f"{cid:<20} ${customer_totals[cid]:>19.2f}\n")
        
        f.write("-" * 60 + "\n")
        f.write(f"{'GRAND TOTAL':<20} ${sum(customer_totals.values()):>19.2f}\n\n")
        
        if invalid_log:
            f.write("INVALID ROWS\n")
            for entry in invalid_log:
                f.write(entry + "\n")
            if invalid_count > 100:
                f.write(f"... and {invalid_count - 100} more errors\n")
    
    print(f"Done: {valid_count} valid, {invalid_count} skipped → {output_file}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        print("Usage: python script.py input.csv output.txt")
        sys.exit(1)
    process_transactions(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2])
```

Reads CSV line-by-line (memory efficient), skips malformed rows with try/except, aggregates totals in a dict, writes formatted report with summary + first 100 invalid rows. Usage: `python script.py transactions.csv report.txt`

## large-s2 / migration

> Write a database migration script that moves a users table from a single full_name column to first_name/last_name, backfills existing rows, and is safely re-runnable. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1444 answer tokens, 1773 billed, 133 lines

~~~
I'll create a database migration script that safely splits `full_name` into `first_name` and `last_name`. Here's a reusable, idempotent version:

```sql
-- Migration: Split full_name into first_name and last_name
-- Safe to re-run: checks for existing columns before creating them

BEGIN TRANSACTION;

-- Step 1: Add new columns if they don't exist
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'first_name'
  ) THEN
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN first_name VARCHAR(255);
  END IF;

  IF NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'last_name'
  ) THEN
    ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN last_name VARCHAR(255);
  END IF;
END $$;

-- Step 2: Backfill data only if full_name still exists
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    UPDATE users
    SET 
      first_name = COALESCE(NULLIF(TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', 1)), ''), 'Unknown'),
      last_name = COALESCE(
        NULLIF(TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', -1)), ''),
        ''
      )
    WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL;
  END IF;
END $$;

-- Step 3: Drop old column if migration is complete
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
    WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    -- Only drop if both new columns exist and have data
    IF EXISTS (
      SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
      WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'first_name'
    ) AND EXISTS (
      SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns 
      WHERE table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'last_name'
    ) THEN
      ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;
    END IF;
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;
```

Here's a **Python alternative** using SQLAlchemy (more typical for app codebases):

```python
# migrations/versions/001_split_full_name.py
from alembic import op
import sqlalchemy as sa

def upgrade():
    """Add first_name/last_name and migrate data from full_name."""
    
    # Step 1: Create new columns if they don't exist
    with op.batch_operations() as batch_op:
        # Check if column exists before adding
        ctx = op.get_context()
        insp = sa.inspect(ctx.bind)
        columns = [c['name'] for c in insp.get_columns('users')]
        
        if 'first_name' not in columns:
            batch_op.add_column(sa.Column('first_name', sa.String(255), nullable=True))
        
        if 'last_name' not in columns:
            batch_op.add_column(sa.Column('last_name', sa.String(255), nullable=True))
    
    # Step 2: Backfill data
    if 'full_name' in columns:
        op.execute("""
            UPDATE users
            SET 
              first_name = COALESCE(NULLIF(TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 1)), ''), 'Unknown'),
              last_name = COALESCE(NULLIF(TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 2)), ''), '')
            WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL
        """)
    
    # Step 3: Drop old column (optional — keeping it allows rollback)
    # Only uncomment after verifying the migration in production
    # with op.batch_operations() as batch_op:
    #     batch_op.drop_column('full_name')

def downgrade():
    """Rollback: restore full_name, drop new columns."""
    with op.batch_operations() as batch_op:
        batch_op.add_column(sa.Column('full_name', sa.String(255)))
        # Reconstruct full_name from first_name and last_name
        op.execute("""
            UPDATE users
            SET full_name = TRIM(CONCAT(COALESCE(first_name, ''), ' ', COALESCE(last_name, '')))
            WHERE full_name IS NULL
        """)
        batch_op.drop_column('first_name')
        batch_op.drop_column('last_name')
```

**Key safety features:**

1. **Idempotent** — checks for column existence before creating/modifying
2. **NULL-safe** — handles missing/whitespace-only names with defaults
3. **Transactional** — wraps entire migration in a transaction; rolls back on error
4. **Preserves data** — doesn't drop `full_name` until you're confident (comment it out initially)
5. **Handles edge cases** — single-name entries, extra spaces, empty strings

**To use:**

- **SQL**: Run against your database directly (PostgreSQL shown; adjust `SUBSTRING_INDEX` / `SPLIT_PART` for MySQL/SQLite)
- **Python**: Place in your Alembic migrations folder and run `alembic upgrade head`

For the first run, skip the drop-column step and verify the split worked. Run it again safely to drop the old column once you're confident.

I'll show you a safely re-runnable SQL migration. The key: idempotent operations using `IF EXISTS` and `WHERE` conditions so rerunning does no harm.

```sql
-- Migration: Split full_name to first_name/last_name
-- Safe to rerun. Idempotent at each step.

-- 1. Add new columns if they don't exist
ALTER TABLE users
ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name VARCHAR(255),
ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name VARCHAR(255);

-- 2. Backfill: split full_name only where the new columns are still NULL
-- (This is key for rerunability — subsequent runs won't overwrite existing data)
UPDATE users
SET
  first_name = TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', 1)),
  last_name = TRIM(SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', -1))
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL;

-- 3. Add NOT NULL constraint if both columns are now populated
-- (optional — only if you want to enforce them going forward)
-- ALTER TABLE users MODIFY first_name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL;
-- ALTER TABLE users MODIFY last_name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL;

-- 4. Drop the old column only after you're confident
-- Uncomment this once you've verified the split data in production
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;
```

**For SQLite** (adjust `SUBSTRING_INDEX` which isn't native):
```sql
-- Step 2 for SQLite instead:
UPDATE users
SET
  first_name = TRIM(SUBSTR(full_name, 1, INSTR(full_name, ' ') - 1)),
  last_name = TRIM(SUBSTR(full_name, INSTR(full_name, ' ') + 1))
WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL;
```

**Why it's re-runnable:**
- `IF NOT EXISTS` on column adds → second run skips them
- `WHERE first_name IS NULL` on the backfill → rerunning only backfills rows that weren't migrated yet
- The old column stays until you explicitly drop it (comment out until verified)

**To run it:**
```bash
mysql -u user -p database < migration.sql
# or
psql -d database -f migration.sql
```

**If you need a rollback**, keep the old column around for 1–2 deploys. Once all services read from the new columns, uncomment the `DROP` and run it again.

Skipped: framework-specific wrappers (Alembic, Flyway), transaction wrapping—add those if your deployment system requires them. Add when: you have multiple migrations that must roll back atomically or need version tracking.

### chisle-3.13: 367 answer tokens, 2999 billed, 33 lines

~~~
```sql
-- PostgreSQL: split full_name → first_name + last_name
-- Idempotent (safe to re-run)

BEGIN;

ALTER TABLE users 
ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name VARCHAR(255),
ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name VARCHAR(255);

UPDATE users
SET 
  first_name = TRIM(SPLIT_PART(full_name, ' ', 1)),
  last_name = CASE 
    WHEN POSITION(' ' IN full_name) > 0 
    THEN TRIM(SUBSTRING(full_name FROM POSITION(' ' IN full_name) + 1))
    ELSE ''
  END
WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL
  AND first_name IS NULL
  AND last_name IS NULL;

-- After verification, drop old column:
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;

COMMIT;
```

**Re-runnable:** `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` skips on second run. `WHERE first_name IS NULL` ensures backfill only on unmigrated rows. Comment out the `DROP` until you've verified in staging.

**Edge cases:** Single-word names get empty `last_name`. NULLs in `full_name` stay as-is. Names with 2+ spaces split on first space only (e.g., "John Q Public" → "John" / "Q Public").

For MySQL, use `SUBSTRING_INDEX(full_name, ' ', 1)` and `LOCATE(' ', full_name)` instead.

# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion - 4-Hour Outage

**Date:** November 19, 2026  
**Duration:** 4 hours 23 minutes (13:47 UTC - 18:10 UTC)  
**Severity:** SEV-1 (Critical)  
**Authors:** [On-call Engineer], [Engineering Manager]

---

## Executive Summary

A four-hour outage affecting 100% of user-facing API traffic was caused by connection pool exhaustion in our primary database connection pool. The root cause was a combination of:

1. A slow downstream payment service (3+ minute response times) holding database connections during I/O wait
2. Insufficient connection pool size relative to request concurrency
3. Lack of connection timeout policies and circuit breaker protection for the payment service

**Key Metric Impact:**
- 4h 23m of service unavailability
- Estimated 85K failed requests
- ~$12K revenue impact (failed transactions)
- 2.3M affected users unable to complete checkout

---

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|-----------|-------|
| 13:47 | Payment service begins experiencing elevated latency (~500ms → 3-4s) due to unrelated incident on their infrastructure |
| 13:52 | First payment API requests timeout at checkout service (7s timeout) but do not release connections immediately |
| 13:58 | Connections held by payment service calls accumulate; pool utilization rises to 85% |
| 14:03 | Connection pool reaches 100% capacity; new requests begin queuing |
| 14:05 | Queue timeout threshold exceeded; API begins returning 503 Service Unavailable |
| 14:06 | On-call engineer paged; alert firing: "Database connection pool exhausted" |
| 14:08 | Incident declared SEV-1; war room opened |
| 14:15 | Initial investigation identifies payment service latency; engineer confirms with payment team (3-4 second response times) |
| 14:21 | Temporary mitigation attempted: manual database connection pool restart (causes cascading connection storms; backoff) |
| 14:26 | Decision made to degrade payment service: timeout reduced from 7s to 1.5s and circuit breaker opened |
| 14:29 | Deployment of circuit breaker changes begins |
| 14:38 | Circuit breaker deployed; payment requests begin failing fast (200ms) instead of holding connections |
| 14:40 | Connection pool utilization drops below 20%; API traffic recovers |
| 14:42 | Full service recovery confirmed; monitoring shows healthy response times |
| 15:30 | Payment service stabilizes on their end (latency returns to <100ms) |
| 18:10 | Incident declared resolved; post-incident review scheduled |

---

## Root Cause

**Primary:** Connection pool starvation caused by slow I/O to a critical downstream dependency without connection release guarantees or timeout enforcement.

**Contributing factors:**
1. **No circuit breaker for payment service** — slow requests were retried indefinitely, compounding pool exhaustion
2. **Long connection timeout (7 seconds)** — exceeded SLA for most use cases; threads held connections during entire payment service call
3. **Insufficient pool size (40 connections)** — based on peak load of 50 req/sec with 100ms avg latency, but payment failures caused request queuing
4. **No connection timeout enforcement** — connections blocked waiting for I/O were not reaped
5. **Lack of observability** — connection pool metrics were not visible in dashboards; alert fired only when exhausted

---

## Impact Assessment

**Users:** 2.3M registered users; 487K attempted transactions; 85K failed  
**Revenue:** ~$12K in failed/incomplete transactions  
**Operational:** 4h 23m SEV-1 incident; 8 engineers engaged  
**Downstream:** Delayed order processing for 3h post-recovery due to transaction backlog

---

## Remediation

### Immediate Actions (Completed)
1. ✅ Deployed circuit breaker for payment service (timeout 1.5s, failure threshold 5)
2. ✅ Increased database connection pool from 40 to 120 (temporary; scheduled for review)
3. ✅ Added connection timeout enforcement (60s max connection lifetime)
4. ✅ Added real-time connection pool monitoring dashboard

### Short-term (Target: 1 week)
1. Implement exponential backoff + jitter for payment service retries
2. Add bulkhead isolation pattern for payment service calls (dedicated connection pool)
3. Set up automated alerting on connection pool utilization >70%
4. Document connection pool capacity planning model

### Long-term (Target: 30 days)
1. **Redesign payment integration** — move to async processing model (request → queue → async worker) to decouple checkout latency from payment processing
2. **Connection pool auto-scaling** — implement dynamic pool sizing based on queue depth and latency percentiles
3. **Cascading timeout policy** — establish timeout budgets for all downstream calls (payment: 1.5s, inventory: 500ms, shipping: 300ms)
4. **Chaos engineering** — test connection pool exhaustion scenarios monthly in staging
5. **Multi-region failover** — route payment requests to secondary payment processor if primary SLA violated for >30s

---

## Lessons Learned

| Lesson | Action |
|--------|--------|
| Slow I/O to critical services can cascade to data layer | Implement circuit breakers + timeouts for all external service calls |
| Connection pools are a shared resource; one bad actor starves all others | Bulkhead critical workloads into separate pools |
| Reactive alerting (pool exhausted) arrived too late | Shift to predictive alerts (pool utilization, queue depth, latency percentiles) |
| Manual incident response was needed even with automation | Update runbook to include auto-remediation: auto-open circuit breaker if pool >80% |
| Visibility gap: connection pool metrics not in main dashboard | Add connection pool status to on-call dashboard; add SLO for connection availability |

---

## Prevention Measures Going Forward

**Engineering:**
- Code review checklist: "Does this call an external service? Circuit breaker required."
- Automated connection pool load testing in CI for major changes

**Operations:**
- Monthly chaos: inject payment service latency; verify auto-remediation
- Quarterly load tests with realistic failure scenarios

**Organizational:**
- Establish SLA contracts with downstream services; include latency and availability requirements
- Rotate on-call engineers through post-incident reviews to distribute incident knowledge

---

## Action Items

| ID | Owner | Description | Target Date | Priority |
|----|-------|-------------|------------|----------|
| A1 | Backend Eng | Implement async payment processing | 2026-12-03 | Critical |
| A2 | Infra Eng | Add connection pool auto-scaling | 2026-12-10 | High |
| A3 | Oncall | Update incident runbook with auto-remediation steps | 2026-11-26 | High |
| A4 | Monitoring | Build connection pool health dashboard | 2026-11-25 | High |
| A5 | QA | Set up monthly chaos test for connection exhaustion | 2026-12-03 | Medium |

---

**Approval:** [Engineering Manager] | **Reviewed:** [Oncall Lead], [Database Team], [Payment Team]
~~~

<parameter name="contents"># Postmortem: API Service Outage - Connection Pool Exhaustion

**Date:** 2026-11-15  
**Duration:** 4 hours 23 minutes (14:32 - 19:55 UTC)  
**Severity:** Critical  
**Author:** Platform Team  

---

## Executive Summary

API service became unavailable when connection pool to downstream payment processing service exhausted. Payment service degraded (avg response time 45s, p99 180s) while our pool size remained static at 20 connections. All 20 connections blocked waiting for responses, rejecting new requests. Service recovered after manual intervention to drain connections and implement circuit breaker.

---

## Timeline

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| **14:32** | Payment service experiences partial degradation (slow response times begin). No alerts triggered. |
| **14:38** | First customer complaints: "API endpoints returning 503" |
| **14:45** | On-call engineer joins incident. Observes high error rate (87% 5xx) but normal CPU/memory. |
| **14:52** | Database query metrics normal. Load balancer shows all backend instances healthy but not responding. |
| **15:10** | Root cause identified: all db connection pool slots occupied, waiting on payment service timeouts. |
| **15:15** | Temporary mitigation: manual restart of 2 service instances to drain stale connections. Partial recovery—only 60% of requests succeed. |
| **15:30** | Payment service SLA team acknowledges degradation, investigating. No ETA provided. |
| **16:45** | Decision made to implement emergency circuit breaker for payment service calls (timeout 10s, failure threshold 50% over 1min window). |
| **16:55** | Circuit breaker deployed via hot-config update (no service restart needed). |
| **17:02** | Error rate drops to 2%. Service fully recovered. |
| **19:55** | Payment service returns to normal. No further incidents. |

---

## Impact

- **Users affected:** ~2.3M API calls failed, estimated 180K users impacted
- **Revenue loss:** ~$47K (declined transactions due to timeouts)
- **Internal impact:** Depleted incident response capacity; 8 engineers engaged for 5+ hours
- **Customer SLA:** Breached by 4h 23min (committed 99.95% uptime)

---

## Root Cause

**Primary:** Connection pool to downstream payment service not configured with timeout; blocked connections accumulated.

**Contributing factors:**
1. Payment service experienced degradation (slow API, not fully unavailable)—not severe enough to trigger existing service-level alerts, but severe enough to block all connection pool slots
2. No circuit breaker or timeout on payment service calls
3. No explicit pool timeout configuration; default was unlimited wait
4. Connection pool monitoring alert was disabled due to false positives from previous incident

---

## Remediations

### Immediate (Completed by 17:02)
- Deployed circuit breaker for payment service with:
  - Request timeout: 10 seconds (was unlimited)
  - Failure threshold: 50% error rate over 60s window
  - Half-open state: allow 5 test requests after circuit opens

### Short-term (by 2026-11-29)
- **Database pool configuration:**
  - Reduce wait-queue timeout from unlimited to 5 seconds
  - Add `connectionTimeout` and `idleTimeout` settings
  - Set max pool size alert threshold (trigger at 85% utilization)
  
- **Monitoring:**
  - Re-enable connection pool alerts with stricter thresholds
  - Add circuit breaker state changes as PagerDuty alert
  - Create dashboard for downstream service latency percentiles (p50, p95, p99)

- **Testing:**
  - Chaos test: simulate downstream service latency (p99 > 30s)
  - Verify circuit breaker prevents pool exhaustion
  - Load test with degraded payment service

### Long-term (by 2026-12-31)
- Implement bulkhead pattern—isolate payment service calls to separate thread pool
- Add payment service SLA monitoring and automated escalation
- Migrate to async payment processing (fire-and-forget with webhook callbacks) to decouple critical path
- Establish vendor communication SLA: payment service must notify us within 5 minutes of degradation

---

## Contributing Factors

1. **Alerting gap:** No alert on "payment service latency increasing" until all connections exhausted
2. **Configuration drift:** Circuit breaker disabled in a previous incident; re-enabling it was backlog item
3. **Lack of dependency timeout:** Assumed downstream service would fail fast; it didn't
4. **Insufficient runbook:** Incident commander initially focused on our service health instead of dependency health

---

## Lessons Learned

| What | Why It Matters | Action |
|------|-------|--------|
| Timeout all external calls | Without timeout, one slow service blocks entire pool | Mandatory 30s max timeout for all HTTP calls to external services |
| Circuit breakers ≠ optional | Degradation (not failure) can be worse than outage | All critical dependencies require circuit breaker in 2026-Q4 |
| Bulkheads for critical paths | Payment processing shouldn't block unrelated API requests | Separate thread pool for payment calls; refactor in 2027-Q1 |
| Dependency alerting | Waiting for our service to fail is too late | Add upstream SLA monitoring dashboard; alert on latency > 15s |
| Runbook testing | Incident response time wasted on investigation | Add "check dependency health" to API outage runbook; quarterly drill |

---

## Preventative Controls

Going forward:
- All external HTTP calls via centralized client with enforced timeout (default 15s, configurable per service)
- Automated circuit breaker injection via service framework
- Quarterly chaos engineering drills simulating downstream latency
- Dependency SLA tracking in observability platform (auto-alert on SLA breach)
- Post-incident: payment service reliability partnership review scheduled for 2026-12-05

---

## Appendix: Monitoring Gaps Filled

**Before:** Only monitored response times of our service  
**After:** Monitoring includes:
- Payment service latency percentiles (p50/p95/p99)
- Circuit breaker trip/close events
- Connection pool utilization trend
- Queue depth of blocked requests

---

**Sign-off:** Platform Team Lead  
**Date:** 2026-11-19  
**Follow-up meeting:** 2026-11-26 (action item review)

# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion (Nov 15, 2026)

## Executive Summary
A 4-hour outage (14:32–18:28 UTC) caused by connection pool exhaustion in our upstream service. A degraded downstream dependency (payment-api) began responding in 45–90 seconds instead of <1s. Requests queued in the pool, connections never released, and cascading failures consumed all capacity. Root cause: no circuit breaker on payment-api calls; no timeout enforcement on pool connections.

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|-----------|-------|
| 14:32 | Payment API latency spike detected (+40s response time). No alerts triggered. |
| 14:35 | First user reports: "checkout hanging" |
| 14:38 | On-call pages; identifies stuck HTTP requests in payment-api integration |
| 14:50 | Pool exhaustion confirmed: 100/100 connections consumed, all in WAIT state |
| 15:12 | Attempted restart of service; connections released but immediately re-exhausted |
| 15:18 | Escalation to payment-api team; they report database query degradation (slow index scan) |
| 15:45 | Payment-api DB index rebuilt; response times improve to 2–5s (still high) |
| 16:30 | On-call adds circuit breaker; deploys hotfix to production |
| 16:45 | Gradually re-enable traffic; no re-exhaustion observed |
| 17:15 | Normal traffic restored; monitoring stable |
| 18:28 | Declared resolved; all metrics green |

**Customer impact:** ~3,200 failed checkouts; ~15,000 retried by users (of which ~8,000 succeeded on retry). Estimated revenue loss: $42k.

## Root Cause

Connection pool exhaustion via slow downstream dependency:

1. **Payment-api degraded** (database index scan, unrelated to our service)
   - Response time: <1s → 45–90s
   - No circuit breaker in place; all retry logic used default timeout (60s)
   
2. **Connection pool saturation**
   - 100 connections all acquiring payment-api requests
   - Each request waited 45–90s for a response, then 30s retry backoff
   - New requests waited in queue instead of fast-failing
   - Downstream capacity was exhausted; no connection ever released early
   
3. **Cascading failure**
   - Queue depth = 500+ pending checkout requests
   - Each consumed one pool connection
   - No fast feedback to users (they saw timeout, not "service unavailable")
   - Users retried manually → more connections consumed

The service had no way to degrade gracefully when payment-api was slow.

## Remediations

### Immediate (Completed)

- **Circuit breaker on payment-api calls** (deployed 16:30)
  - Threshold: 5 consecutive failures or >15s response time
  - Open state: fail fast with 503, duration 30s
  - Fallback: queue checkout for async retry (best-effort billing)

- **Connection pool timeout**
  - Per-request timeout: 10s (was unlimited)
  - Idle connection eviction: 5 min (was 30 min)

- **Alerting on pool saturation**
  - Alert at >80% pool utilization
  - Alert on response time SLO miss (p99 latency >3s)

### Preventive (In Progress)

| Action | Owner | Deadline | Rationale |
|--------|-------|----------|-----------|
| Load test with degraded payment-api (45s latency) | Eng | Dec 3 | Verify circuit breaker holds under stress |
| Implement bulkhead for payment-api (separate pool, 20 conns) | Eng | Dec 10 | Prevent payment-api from starving other callers |
| SLI on circuit breaker trips | Observability | Dec 1 | Early warning if downstream degrades frequently |
| Async checkout retry (queue to Kafka) | Checkout team | Jan 15 | Allow graceful degradation; retry when payment-api recovers |
| Payment-api SLA review | Partnerships | Dec 5 | Understand their reliability posture; set incident triggers |

## Lessons Learned

**What went well:**
- PagerDuty worked; alert response in ~6 min
- Payment-api team accessible and responsive
- Hotfix deployed and validated smoothly

**What didn't:**
- No circuit breaker is inexcusable for a critical dependency; it's a one-line add after first incident with any external service
- Pool timeouts weren't enforced; default 60s timeout is dangerous for high-load services
- Metrics didn't show pool saturation until we looked; alerting was reactive, not proactive
- No async fallback for non-instant operations (checkout doesn't *need* synchronous payment confirmation in all cases)

**Apply everywhere:**
- Audit all external service calls for circuit breakers (finish by Dec 20)
- Use connection pooling best practices (bounded queue, timeouts, bulkheads) as default on any HTTP client
- SLO dashboards should include pool/queue depth, not just latency

---

**Postmortem date:** Nov 19, 2026  
**Reviewed by:** On-call, Eng Lead, Payments team  
**Follow-up meeting:** Dec 3 (load test debrief)
~~~
