# Incident Postmortem: Connection Pool Exhaustion

## Summary
API service experienced total availability loss for 4 hours (Nov 18, 2026, 14:22–18:30 UTC) due to downstream payment processor connection pool exhaustion. Response times on their side degraded from 100ms to 30+ seconds; our 20-connection pool filled entirely with slow requests, blocking all traffic. User transactions failed with connection timeout errors.

## Timeline

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|---|---|
| 14:22 | Payment processor begins experiencing slow query performance on their DB (later identified as index lock contention on `transactions` table). Their HTTP latency increases from ~100ms to 15–30s. |
| 14:28 | First customer complaints: transactions timing out in mobile app. |
| 14:34 | On-call alerted via error threshold. API response latencies spike to p95 = 32s. Database connections healthy. |
| 14:41 | Oncall assumes origin DB issue, begins investigation there. No anomalies found. |
| 14:52 | Network team confirms outbound connections to payment processor stable; latency monitoring shows their endpoint at 25s p50 latency. |
| 15:04 | Root cause identified: all 20 connections in `payment_client` pool occupied, each waiting 20–30s for processor response. No connection recycling. New requests queued in-memory, memory usage climbing. |
| 15:11 | Mitigation: deployed timeout reduction on payment processor calls (10s → 2s). Cascading failure stopped; failed requests return to client instead of blocking pool. |
| 15:18 | Service stabilized; response latencies return to normal. Queue drains. |
| 15:45 | Payment processor resolves DB index lock (dropped unused index). Their latency returns to baseline. |
| 15:50 | Reverted 2s timeout to 10s; service remains stable. No further degradation. |
| 18:30 | Full post-incident review completed. Root cause: missing circuit breaker + no timeout on slow dependency. |

## Root Cause
**Primary:** Synchronous payment processor calls with no timeout or circuit breaker. When their latency degraded, slow requests occupied all connection pool slots indefinitely.

**Contributing:**
1. Connection pool size (20) not right-sized for concurrency; no headroom for slow outliers.
2. No observability on per-dependency latency; payment processor latency buried in aggregate metrics.
3. No fallback strategy when processor times out (retry, cached response, graceful degradation).

## Impact
- **Duration:** 4 hours.
- **Scope:** All user-initiated transactions requiring payment processor validation (~87% of requests).
- **Metrics:** 2.1M failed requests, ~12k users impacted, $890k transaction value unprocessed (later retried after recovery).
- **Availability:** 0% for affected endpoint.

## Remediations

### Immediate (Deployed)
- **Timeout:** Set 10s timeout on payment processor calls; failures return 503 instead of hanging.
- **Alerting:** Added alert for payment processor latency > 5s p95 (triggers before pool exhaustion).

### Short-term (1–2 weeks)
- **Circuit breaker:** Implement resilience4j circuit breaker on payment processor client. Open after 5 consecutive timeouts; return cached response or queue for retry.
- **Connection pool tuning:** Increase to 50 connections; add connection timeout (5s) to prevent connection exhaustion.
- **Fallback:** Implement async retry queue for failed transactions; retry every 5 minutes with exponential backoff up to 1 hour.

### Long-term (1–2 months)
- **Dependency observability:** Deploy per-service latency tracking; alert on p95 > 10s for external dependencies.
- **Load testing:** Quarterly chaos engineering runs simulating 50s+ latency on payment processor; verify graceful degradation.
- **Architecture:** Evaluate payment processor webhook model (async) to decouple from critical path.
- **SLA agreements:** Negotiate max latency SLA (≤500ms) with payment processor; include incident response procedures.

## Action Items
- [ ] Deploy timeout + alerting (11/20, assigned: on-call lead)
- [ ] Circuit breaker implementation (11/27, assigned: platform team)
- [ ] Dependency observability (12/01, assigned: SRE)
- [ ] Chaos engineering test plan (12/08, assigned: QA + SRE)
- [ ] Review SLAs with payment processor (11/22, assigned: PM + vendor management)

---

**Incident Lead:** on-call-nov-18  
**Reviewed:** 2026-11-19
~~~

# Incident Postmortem: Service Unavailability 2026-11-19 14:30–18:35 UTC

## Summary
Platform experienced 4h 5min outage affecting all API traffic. Root cause: connection pool exhaustion from slow downstream payment service. Requests queued indefinitely, response times degraded from 50ms to 30s+, then timeouts.

## Timeline
- **14:30** Payment service latency increases from 200ms to 1.2s (not monitored)
- **14:35** Connection pool utilization climbs to 50%, app doesn't scale worker threads (pool fixed at 50)
- **14:38** Pool at 100%; new requests wait in queue
- **14:42** Alert triggers: response time P99 > 5s. On-call investigates dashboards; payment service latency not visible
- **14:55** Assumes database slowdown; queries checked, OK
- **15:10** On-call escalates to backend team lead; connection logs reviewed
- **15:22** Discovers downstream dependency (payment service) is slow; logs show 95% of requests exceed SLA
- **15:30** Mitigation: timeout set to 2s on payment calls, connections released faster
- **15:45** Error rate drops; customer impact subsides
- **18:35** Payment service acknowledged underlying issue (vendor incident with their database)

## Root Cause
Connection pool (50 connections, 30s idle timeout) blocked by slow payment service (latency spiked 1–2s, no timeout configured). Each slow request occupied a connection for the full duration. Pool drained in <30s; subsequent requests queued indefinitely. No alerting on pool utilization or downstream latency.

## Impact
- **Duration:** 4h 5min (14:30–18:35 UTC)
- **Scope:** All API endpoints (100% of traffic)
- **Customer-facing:** Yes; 3 high-priority customers reported; ~10k requests dropped/timed out
- **Recovery:** Manual mitigation; no automatic failover

## Remediations

### Immediate (completed)
- Added 2s timeout to payment service calls (was none)
- Increased pool size to 100 connections
- Added alert on pool utilization > 80%

### Short-term (by end of sprint)
- Dashboard showing downstream dependency latencies (payment, inventory, shipping)
- Automated alert on payment service latency > 500ms
- Implement circuit breaker for payment service (fail fast, don't exhaust pool)

### Long-term (by next quarter)
- Review all outbound service integrations for timeout/pool coverage
- Implement connection pool monitoring in APM
- Add chaos test: simulate slow payment service, verify graceful degradation
- Establish SLA monitoring on downstream dependencies (requires vendor agreement)

## Lessons Learned
1. **Pool exhaustion can mask the real problem:** looked like database issue, was actually downstream
2. **Observable != Visible:** payment latency existed but wasn't dashboarded
3. **No fallback for required services:** payment failures cascade to entire platform; evaluate best-effort vs. critical path

## Owner & Follow-up
- Lead: Backend on-call (follow-up owner: database team lead)
- Remediations tracked in [Linear project INFRA](https://linear.app/example)
- Review in 2 weeks

I'll create a clean order state machine implementation that enforces valid transitions.
<function_calls>
<invoke name="write">
<parameter name="path">/tmp/chisle-ablation-work-03dXUv/order_state_machine.py</parameter>
<parameter name="file_text">from enum import Enum
from typing import Set, Dict

class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"

class OrderStateMachine:
    VALID_TRANSITIONS: Dict[OrderState, Set[OrderState]] = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.REFUNDED, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED, OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }
    
    def __init__(self):
        self.current_state = OrderState.CREATED
    
    def can_transition_to(self, next_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        """Check if transition from current state to next state is valid."""
        return next_state in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.current_state]
    
    def transition_to(self, next_state: OrderState) -> None:
        """Transition to the next state. Raises ValueError if transition is invalid."""
        if not self.can_transition_to(next_state):
            raise ValueError(
                f"Invalid transition: {self.current_state.value} → {next_state.value}"
            )
        self.current_state = next_state
    
    def get_current_state(self) -> str:
        """Get the current state as a string."""
        return self.current_state.value
    
    def get_valid_next_states(self) -> Set[str]:
        """Get all valid next states from the current state."""
        return {state.value for state in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.current_state]}


if __name__ == "__main__":
    order = OrderStateMachine()
    print(f"Initial state: {order.get_current_state()}")
    print(f"Valid next states: {order.get_valid_next_states()}\n")
    
    # Scenario 1: Normal happy path
    print("=== Happy Path ===")
    order.transition_to(OrderState.PAID)
    print(f"After payment: {order.get_current_state()}")
    
    order.transition_to(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    print(f"After shipping: {order.get_current_state()}")
    
    order.transition_to(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    print(f"After delivery: {order.get_current_state()}")
    
    order.transition_to(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(f"After refund: {order.get_current_state()}\n")
    
    # Scenario 2: Cancellation before payment
    print("=== Cancel Before Payment ===")
    order2 = OrderStateMachine()
    order2.transition_to(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    print(f"Cancelled at: {order2.get_current_state()}")
    print(f"Valid next states: {order2.get_valid_next_states()}\n")
    
    # Scenario 3: Refund after payment (before shipping)
    print("=== Refund After Payment ===")
    order3 = OrderStateMachine()
    order3.transition_to(OrderState.PAID)
    order3.transition_to(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(f"Refunded at: {order3.get_current_state()}\n")
    
    # Scenario 4: Invalid transitions
    print("=== Invalid Transitions ===")
    invalid_cases = [
        (OrderState.CREATED, OrderState.SHIPPED, "created → shipped (skips payment)"),
        (OrderState.CREATED, OrderState.DELIVERED, "created → delivered (skips payment/shipping)"),
        (OrderState.PAID, OrderState.DELIVERED, "paid → delivered (skips shipping)"),
        (OrderState.DELIVERED, OrderState.PAID, "delivered → paid (backwards)"),
        (OrderState.REFUNDED, OrderState.CANCELLED, "refunded → cancelled (terminal state)"),
    ]
    
    for from_state, to_state, description in invalid_cases:
        order_test = OrderStateMachine()
        order_test.current_state = from_state
        try:
            order_test.transition_to(to_state)
            print(f"✗ {description} - Should have failed!")
        except ValueError as e:
            print(f"✓ {description} - Correctly rejected")

Here's a complete order state machine implementation with enforced legal transitions:

**Key features:**

- **Valid transitions defined explicitly**: Each state maps to its legal next states in `VALID_TRANSITIONS`
- **Terminal states**: `CANCELLED` and `REFUNDED` have no outgoing transitions
- **Transition enforcement**: `transition_to()` raises `ValueError` on invalid transitions
- **State queries**: `can_transition_to()` checks validity, `get_valid_next_states()` shows options

**Legal transitions:**
- `created` → `paid`, `cancelled`
- `paid` → `shipped`, `refunded`, `cancelled` 
- `shipped` → `delivered`, `refunded`
- `delivered` → `refunded`
- `cancelled` and `refunded` are terminal

The script demonstrates valid scenarios (happy path, early cancellation, refunds) and shows all invalid transitions being correctly rejected.

class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"

    REFUNDED = "refunded"

        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.CANCELLED, OrderState.REFUNDED},

        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},

        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }

    def transition(self, next_state):
        if next_state not in self.TRANSITIONS[self.state]:

I'll implement a state machine that enforces legal order transitions and rejects invalid ones.

```python
from enum import Enum

class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"

class InvalidTransitionError(Exception):
    pass

class Order:
    VALID_TRANSITIONS = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }

    def __init__(self, order_id: str):
        self.order_id = order_id
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED

    def transition_to(self, next_state: OrderState) -> None:
        if next_state not in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise InvalidTransitionError(
                f"Cannot transition from {self.state.value} to {next_state.value}"
            )
        self.state = next_state

    def can_transition_to(self, next_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        return next_state in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def __str__(self):
        return f"Order {self.order_id}: {self.state.value}"


# Usage examples
if __name__ == "__main__":
    order = Order("ORD-123")
    print(order)  # Order ORD-123: created

    # Valid: created → paid
    order.transition_to(OrderState.PAID)
    print(order)  # Order ORD-123: paid

    # Valid: paid → shipped
    order.transition_to(OrderState.SHIPPED)
    print(order)  # Order ORD-123: shipped

    # Valid: shipped → delivered
    order.transition_to(OrderState.DELIVERED)
    print(order)  # Order ORD-123: delivered

    # Valid: delivered → refunded
    order.transition_to(OrderState.REFUNDED)
    print(order)  # Order ORD-123: refunded

    # Invalid: refunded → paid (terminal state)
    try:
        order.transition_to(OrderState.PAID)
    except InvalidTransitionError as e:
        print(f"Error: {e}")  # Cannot transition from refunded to paid

    # Test cancel flow
    order2 = Order("ORD-456")
    order2.transition_to(OrderState.CANCELLED)
    print(order2)  # Order ORD-456: cancelled

    # Can check before transitioning
    if order2.can_transition_to(OrderState.PAID):
        order2.transition_to(OrderState.PAID)
    else:
        print("Cannot pay cancelled order")  # This prints
```

**Flow:** created→paid/cancelled; paid→shipped/refunded; shipped→delivered; delivered→refunded; cancelled/refunded are terminal. Invalid transitions raise `InvalidTransitionError`.

```python
from enum import Enum

class OrderState(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"

class OrderStateMachine:
    VALID_TRANSITIONS = {
        OrderState.CREATED: {OrderState.PAID, OrderState.CANCELLED},
        OrderState.PAID: {OrderState.SHIPPED, OrderState.CANCELLED, OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.SHIPPED: {OrderState.DELIVERED},
        OrderState.DELIVERED: {OrderState.REFUNDED},
        OrderState.CANCELLED: set(),
        OrderState.REFUNDED: set(),
    }

    def __init__(self, order_id: str):
        self.order_id = order_id
        self.state = OrderState.CREATED

    def transition(self, new_state: OrderState) -> None:
        """Transition to new_state or raise ValueError if invalid."""
        if new_state not in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Cannot transition from {self.state.value} to {new_state.value}"
            )
        self.state = new_state

    def can_transition_to(self, new_state: OrderState) -> bool:
        """Check if transition is allowed without changing state."""
        return new_state in self.VALID_TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def __str__(self):
        return f"Order {self.order_id}: {self.state.value}"


# Usage
order = OrderStateMachine("ORD-001")
print(order)  # Order ORD-001: created

order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
order.transition(OrderState.SHIPPED)
order.transition(OrderState.DELIVERED)
print(order)  # Order ORD-001: delivered

# Invalid transition → rejected
try:
    order.transition(OrderState.PAID)
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Error: {e}")  # Error: Cannot transition from delivered to paid
```

Transitions: created→{paid, cancelled}, paid→{shipped, cancelled, refunded}, shipped→{delivered}, delivered→{refunded}, cancelled/refunded terminal.
